=====================================================================
MISSION ORDER FOR GEMINI: FLY8 STUDY, NP1-NP5 REVIEW-NOTE EXPANSION
(Philippine nursing licensure exam / PNLE review content) - PACK NP3
=====================================================================

READ THIS WHOLE ORDER FIRST. DO NOT START WORKING YET.
When you have read everything and opened the files in PART 2, reply ONLY with:
  1. the word READY,
  2. a 10-line-max summary of what you understood,
  3. your proposed batch plan (which file and which notes come first),
  4. any question you have.
Then STOP and WAIT. Do nothing else until I (the owner) type the single word: GO
After GO, do exactly ONE batch per reply (see PART 6), then stop and wait for me to type NEXT.
If at any point I type STOP, stop immediately.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 1. WHO YOU ARE AND WHAT THE PROJECT IS
---------------------------------------------------------------------
You are a research-and-writing agent working for the owner of "FLY8 Study",
a personal Android study app for the Philippine Nurse Licensure Examination
(PNLE). The owner is a nursing reviewee, not a programmer. Another AI (Claude)
builds and maintains the app; it will check and merge what you produce. You do
NOT edit the GitHub repository directly. You produce exact, ready-to-merge
JSON patches in your replies (format in PART 5). The owner pastes them back to
Claude.

The app holds about 974 short review notes. Each note is a JSON object inside
a "pack" file. Your job: EXPAND and STRENGTHEN the NP1 to NP5 notes so every
disease / condition / problem note follows one clinical structure, is
Philippine-based, is fact-checked, and carries real references.

The five PNLE areas used in this app (current understanding, verify if you can):
  NP1  Community Health Nursing (+ communicable diseases, PH health programs)
  NP2  Care of Healthy/At-Risk Mother and Child (OB, newborn, pediatrics, growth and development)
  NP3  Physiologic and Psychosocial Alterations, Part A (cardio, respiratory, blood/hema, oncology,
       perioperative, fluids-electrolytes and renal, burns)
  NP4  Physiologic and Psychosocial Alterations, Part B (GI, endocrine, neuro, eye and ear, ortho)
  NP5  Physiologic and Psychosocial Alterations, Part C (psychiatric nursing, emergency, disaster, critical care)
  (Fundamentals, pharmacology, ethics/laws run through all five. They are NOT in scope for this mission.)


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 2. THE FILES (ALL INCLUDED BELOW IN THIS SAME DOCUMENT)
---------------------------------------------------------------------
You do NOT need GitHub or any link. Everything you need is pasted at the end of this
document, between lines that start with "===== FILE:" and "===== END FILE".
This pack covers NP3 ONLY. Work only on the files in this pack, in this order:
  1. data/presets/np3-cardioresp.json
  2. data/presets/np3-hop.json
  3. data/presets/np3-erdn-renal.json

Also included for context: data/presets/bundles.json (which subject each note belongs to).
Use the file path shown after "===== FILE:" as the "target" in your JSON patches.
Do not touch anything that is not in this pack. Other NP areas come in separate packs.
If a file below looks cut off or will not parse, say which one and STOP.

---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 3. WHAT A NOTE LOOKS LIKE TODAY (keep these fields)
---------------------------------------------------------------------
Each note is a JSON object like this (shortened):
{
  "id": "np1-cd-dengue",
  "area": "NP1",
  "title": "Dengue Fever",
  "body": "## What it is\n...markdown...\n\n## Related topics\n[[Malaria]], [[Filariasis]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n...",
  "tags": ["communicable disease", "..."],
  "priority": "high",
  "outlineRefs": ["..."],
  "aiWritten": true,
  "nurseReviewed": false
}
Rules for fields:
- NEVER change "id", "area", "outlineRefs". Keep "tags" (you may ADD tags, never remove).
- "priority" stays as is unless clearly wrong (then explain why in your batch notes).
- "aiWritten" must be true. "nurseReviewed" must stay false. You are not a nurse reviewer.
- "body" is markdown with only: "## " headings, paragraphs, "- " bullets, "1. " numbered lists,
  **bold**, *italic*, and [[Wikilinks]] to other note titles. No tables, no HTML, no images, no code fences.
- Keep [[Wikilinks]] that exist. Only add a [[Link]] if a note with that exact title exists in the packs.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 4. THE NEW STRUCTURE FOR EVERY DISEASE / CONDITION NOTE
---------------------------------------------------------------------
Rewrite the "body" of each disease, disorder or clinical-problem note into EXACTLY these sections,
in this order. Use plain, exam-ready wording. Short bullets. Philippine context everywhere it applies.

## What it is
  1-3 sentences: definition, who gets it, why it matters on the PNLE.

## Causative agent
  The organism (genus/species, type: virus/bacteria/parasite/fungus), or the cause/risk factors
  if not infectious (e.g. autoimmune, genetic, trauma). Include mode of transmission, incubation
  period and period of communicability for communicable diseases. Include the vector or reservoir
  common in the Philippines (e.g. Aedes aegypti, Anopheles flavirostris, Oncomelania snail).
  If there is no causative agent (e.g. a nursing concept), write "Not applicable" and the main risk factors.

## Main problem
  The core pathophysiology in 1-3 sentences: what goes wrong in the body (the "why" behind the signs).

## Initial manifestations
  The EARLY / first signs and symptoms (what the nurse sees first). Mark pathognomonic signs with
  "(pathognomonic)" only if a reference supports it.

## Other manifestations
  Later, severe or complicating signs; warning signs; danger signs that need referral.

## Laboratory and diagnostic data
  Key tests and the expected abnormal result (with normal ranges ONLY when a reference gives them,
  in SI or conventional units as used in the Philippines). Confirmatory test vs screening test.

## Nursing diagnoses
  2-5 priority nursing diagnoses in NANDA-I style (problem related to cause as evidenced by signs),
  ordered by priority (ABC, Maslow, safety). Mark the top one "(priority)".

## Nursing interventions
  Numbered, in priority order. Each line: the action, then " - " and a short rationale.
  Include: assessment first, safety, infection control/isolation type, positioning, fluids/nutrition,
  medications by CLASS (drug names only if the reference names them; DOSES ONLY if a cited
  reference states the dose, otherwise write "dose per physician order / current guideline"),
  health teaching, referral, and prevention.

## Philippine context
  DOH program or strategy that covers it (e.g. EPI, National TB Program DOTS, Dengue Prevention and
  Control Program, Rabies Prevention and Control Program, Integrated Management of Childhood Illness),
  relevant Republic Act or DOH Administrative Order (number + name, only if verified),
  local terms (Filipino names, e.g. "bulutong", "tigdas"), common local exam traps.

## Exam traps
  3-5 bullets: classic PNLE-style confusions and the correct thinking (e.g. "fever falls in the
  critical phase of dengue = danger, not improvement").

## Related topics
  Keep the existing [[Wikilinks]]; add only links to notes that exist.

## References
  2-5 numbered references you ACTUALLY opened and read. Format:
  1. Organization or author. Title. Year (or "accessed YYYY-MM-DD"). URL
  Each key fact you added must be supported by at least one reference in this list.

## Check against your textbook
  Keep the existing text, then add any fact you could NOT verify, written as
  "UNVERIFIED: ..." so the owner can check it in their textbook.

For NON-disease notes (programs, laws, concepts, procedures, growth and development stages,
therapeutic communication, etc.): do NOT force the disease sections. Use only the sections that
make sense (always keep "What it is", add "Philippine context", "Exam traps", "References",
"Check against your textbook"). Say in your batch notes which notes you treated as non-disease.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 5. VERIFY AND REFERENCE (SEARCH FOR THESE SOURCES)
---------------------------------------------------------------------
You MUST search the web and verify facts. Prefer, in this order:
  1. Philippine Department of Health: https://doh.gov.ph (programs, Administrative Orders,
     clinical practice guidelines, disease fact sheets), DOH Epidemiology Bureau.
  2. Official Philippine law and issuances: https://www.officialgazette.gov.ph , https://lawphil.net
     (Republic Acts, e.g. RA 9173 Philippine Nursing Act of 2002; verify every RA number).
  3. Professional Regulation Commission (PRC): https://www.prc.gov.ph (PNLE scope / table of
     specifications if available).
  4. WHO and WHO Western Pacific Region: https://www.who.int , https://www.who.int/westernpacific
  5. Philippine medical societies' public guidelines (e.g. Philippine Society for Microbiology and
     Infectious Diseases, Philippine Pediatric Society, Philippine Heart Association), PhilHealth.
  6. US CDC / NIH MedlinePlus / StatPearls (NCBI Bookshelf) for pathophysiology and lab data.
  7. NANDA-I terminology for nursing diagnoses (name the diagnosis; do not paste copyrighted definitions).

Search queries you should use (adapt per topic):
  "<disease> DOH Philippines", "<disease> clinical practice guideline Philippines",
  "<disease> administrative order DOH", "<disease> WHO fact sheet", "<disease> StatPearls",
  "<program name> DOH manual of operations", "Republic Act <number> <title> lawphil".

Verification rules:
- Open every source you cite and confirm the fact is on that page. Never cite a page you did not open.
- Never invent a URL, a law number, a guideline year, a page number, an author, or a drug dose.
- If two good sources disagree, state both and which is current (newest DOH guidance wins for PH practice).
- If the existing note contains a fact that your sources contradict, FIX it and list the fix in your
  batch notes as "CORRECTED: old -> new (source #)".
- If you cannot verify something, keep it out of the main sections and put it under
  "Check against your textbook" as UNVERIFIED.
- Write in your own words. Do not copy paragraphs from sources or textbooks. Short facts and
  official names/terms are fine.
- NEVER use, copy or reconstruct real PNLE board questions or "recalled questions" sites.
  They are confidential/copyrighted. Do not write any test questions in this mission.
- No patient-specific medical advice. This is exam review content.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 6. HOW TO DELIVER (EXACT OUTPUT FORMAT, ONE BATCH PER REPLY)
---------------------------------------------------------------------
Batch size: 5 notes per reply (fewer if the notes are long). Work file by file in the PART 2 order,
NP1 first. Inside a file, do disease/condition notes before non-disease notes.

Each reply after GO / NEXT must contain exactly these 3 parts:

(1) A heading line:  BATCH <n> - <file path> - notes <i>-<j> of <total in file>

(2) ONE fenced JSON code block (this is the patch; it must be valid JSON, nothing else inside it):
```json
{
  "target": "data/presets/np1-comm.json",
  "batch": 1,
  "ops": [
    {
      "op": "update",
      "id": "np1-cd-dengue",
      "note": { ...the FULL updated note object with all fields... }
    }
  ]
}
```
  - "op": "update" replaces the note with that id in the target file (send the FULL note, not a diff).
  - "op": "add" adds a NEW note (only if an important PNLE disease/condition is clearly missing from the
    NP area). New ids: "<area lowercase>-x-<short-slug>", e.g. "np1-x-leptospirosis". Check every pack
    first so you do not duplicate an existing note under another title. Put a new note in the pack file
    of the same subject. Mark it "priority": "medium" unless it is a core PNLE topic.
  - Escape newlines inside "body" as \n and quotes as \". The block must parse as JSON.

(3) "BATCH NOTES" in plain text, short:
  - which notes were treated as disease vs non-disease,
  - every CORRECTED item,
  - every UNVERIFIED item,
  - sources you could not open,
  - suggested new notes for later (title + why), without writing them yet.

Then stop and wait for NEXT. Do not start the next batch on your own.
If your reply would be too long, split the batch smaller. Never cut a JSON block in half.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 7. QUALITY BAR (CHECK BEFORE SENDING EACH BATCH)
---------------------------------------------------------------------
[ ] Every disease note has all sections of PART 4 in order (non-disease notes: the sensible subset).
[ ] Causative agent, main problem, initial and other manifestations, lab data, nursing diagnoses,
    interventions are present and specific (no filler like "monitor the patient" without what/why).
[ ] Philippine context is real (program names, RA/AO numbers verified, local terms).
[ ] Every reference was opened; URLs are real; each added fact traces to a reference.
[ ] No invented doses, laws, years, page numbers or URLs. Unverified items are labelled.
[ ] id/area/outlineRefs unchanged; aiWritten true; nurseReviewed false.
[ ] Body uses only allowed markdown (no tables/HTML/images/code).
[ ] The JSON block parses (no trailing commas, quotes escaped).
[ ] No PNLE board questions or recalled-question content anywhere.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PART 8. COMMANDS I (THE OWNER) WILL USE
---------------------------------------------------------------------
  GO          start BATCH 1
  NEXT        do the next batch
  REDO <n>    redo batch n with my comments
  SKIP        skip the current note and continue
  JUMP <file> move to another file in PART 2
  STATUS      tell me which file/notes are done and what is left (no new work)
  STOP        stop now

Remember: right now, only reply READY + summary + batch plan + questions, then WAIT for GO.

"id": "np3-cr-heart-structure",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Heart Structure, Layers and Chambers",
"body": "## What it is\nThe heart is a hollow muscular pump (about 300 g) sitting in the mediastinum between the lungs, resting on the diaphragm. It delivers oxygen and nutrients to tissues by pumping blood.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Three wall layers, inside to outside:\n  - Endocardium: lines the chambers and valves.\n  - Myocardium: the muscle layer that actually contracts.\n  - Epicardium: the outermost layer; the main coronary arteries run on it.\n- The pericardium is a fibrous sac with a thin film of lubricating fluid. Its visceral layer hugs the epicardium; the parietal layer anchors the heart in the mediastinum. Fluid or scarring here causes [[Pericarditis and Pericardiocentesis]] and [[Cardiac Tamponade]].\n- Atria (upper) are the receiving chambers; ventricles (lower) are the pumping chambers.\n- Systole = contraction; diastole = relaxation and ventricular filling. Pumping is a rhythmic cycle of the two.\n- The apical impulse (point of maximal impulse) is felt over the apex at the left mid-clavicular line, normally in the 5th intercostal space.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- \"Which layer contracts?\" (myocardium) or \"which layer is inflamed in endocarditis / myocarditis / pericarditis?\" Match the suffix to the layer: endo = inner, myo = muscle, peri = sac.\n- Auscultation and pulse-point questions usually build on landmarks, so know where the apex sits.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Cardiac Output, Preload, Afterload and Contractility]], [[Heart Sounds S1 to S4]], [[Cardiac Conduction System and Autonomic Control]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout says the apical impulse is at the \"15th\" intercostal space. That is a typo; the adult apex is at the 5th ICS, left mid-clavicular line.",

"id": "np3-cr-cardiac-output",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Cardiac Output, Preload, Afterload and Contractility",
"body": "## What it is\nCardiac output (CO) is the volume of blood the heart ejects each minute, about 5 L/min at rest.\n- CO = heart rate (HR) x stroke volume (SV).\n- Normal heart rate is 60 to 100 beats per minute, controlled by the autonomic nervous system.\n- Stroke volume is the blood ejected by the left ventricle per beat.\n\n## The three determinants of stroke volume\n- Preload: how stretched the ventricle is at the end of diastole, set by the blood returning to the heart. Starling's law: the more the heart fills, the more forcefully it contracts, so higher preload gives higher SV (up to a limit).\n- Contractility: the force of muscle contraction. Raised by catecholamines, sympathetic activity and positive inotropes (the \"3 D's\": digoxin, dopamine, dobutamine). Higher contractility gives higher SV.\n- Afterload: the resistance the ventricle must push against to open the semilunar valves. It rises with high blood pressure and narrowed vessels. Higher afterload gives LOWER SV.\n\n## Why it matters for nursing\n- Heart failure, shock and many cardiac drugs are all explained by moving one of these levers. Diuretics and nitrates lower preload; ACE inhibitors and vasodilators lower afterload; digoxin raises contractility.\n- Low CO means poor perfusion: watch for hypotension, weak pulses, cool skin, low urine output, confusion.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- \"Which drug reduces afterload?\" or \"which change would lower stroke volume?\" Remember: preload and contractility go with SV, afterload goes against it.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Heart Failure: Types, Causes and Compensation]], [[Drugs Used in Heart Failure]], [[Heart Structure, Layers and Chambers]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout says that in exercise cardiac output rises fourfold \"to 2 L/min\". That cannot be right (a four-fold rise from 5 L/min is about 20 L/min). Verify the figure.",

"area": "NP3",
"title": "Heart Sounds S1 to S4",
"body": "## What it is\nHeart sounds come from valve closure and from blood hitting the ventricular walls.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- S1 (\"lub\"): closure of the atrioventricular (mitral and tricuspid) valves; loudest at the apex. Marks the start of systole.\n- S2 (\"dub\"): closure of the semilunar (aortic and pulmonic) valves; loudest at the base.\n- S3 (\"ventricular gallop\"): heard early in diastole when the ventricle is stiff or overloaded, as in heart failure or valve regurgitation. It can be normal in people younger than about 30.\n- S4: heard in late diastole with atrial contraction against a stiff, resistant ventricle (hypertrophy, injury to the ventricular wall). Considered abnormal.\n\n## Why it matters\n- A new S3 in a client with dyspnea and crackles supports [[Left-Sided versus Right-Sided Heart Failure]]. An S3 or S4 can also appear in [[Myocardial Infarction: Presentation and Cardiac Markers]].\n- Murmurs (turbulent flow through narrowed or leaky valves) are covered in [[Valvular Heart Disease: Overview and Mitral Stenosis]].\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Location questions: apex = S1, base = S2.\n- \"A new S3 in a middle-aged client with orthopnea suggests what?\" Heart failure.\n\n## Memory aid (made up here)\nS3 = \"Slosh\" (fluid overload), S4 = \"Stiff\" ventricle.",

"id": "np3-cr-conduction-system",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Cardiac Conduction System and Autonomic Control",
"body": "## What it is\nThe heart makes and spreads its own electrical impulse. Three properties: automaticity (starts an impulse itself), excitability (responds to one) and conductivity (passes it on).\n\n## The pathway and the built-in rates\n- SA node (junction of the superior vena cava and right atrium): the natural pacemaker, 60 to 100 bpm.\n- AV node (near the interatrial septum): briefly delays the impulse so the ventricles can fill; its own rate is 40 to 60 bpm.\n- Bundle of His, then right and left bundle branches in the interventricular septum.\n- Purkinje fibers in the ventricular walls: cause ventricular contraction; their own rate is 20 to 40 bpm. They act as the last-resort backup pacemaker.\n- Rule: if a higher pacemaker fails, the next one down takes over at a slower rate.\n\n## Autonomic control\n- Parasympathetic impulses via the vagus nerve slow the rate; sympathetic impulses speed it up.\n- Baroreceptors in the aortic arch and both internal carotid arteries sense blood pressure. When pressure falls, less baroreceptor signalling reduces vagal inhibition of the SA node, so sympathetic activity rises: vasoconstriction and a faster heart rate bring pressure back up.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- \"Which structure is the normal pacemaker?\" or \"what happens to the rate when the SA node fails?\" (AV junction takes over at 40 to 60.)\n- Low BP with a fast pulse is the baroreceptor response, a clue to early shock ([[Circulatory Shock: Hypovolemic and Cardiogenic]]).\n\n## Related topics\n[[ECG Waves and Intervals]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout says the AV delay is \"0.8 milliseconds\"; the usual figure is about 0.08 to 0.12 second (that is the PR interval portion). It also lists the AV node's location loosely; confirm.",

"id": "np3-cr-ecg-basics",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "ECG Waves and Intervals",
"body": "## What it is\nThe ECG records the heart's electrical activity as waves. Learn what each part means and its normal size.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- P wave: atrial depolarization. Small and rounded, no wider than 0.12 second.\n- QRS complex: ventricular depolarization (atrial repolarization is hidden within it). Normal width 0.04 to 0.10 second.\n- T wave: ventricular repolarization. Tall T waves (over about 5 mm in limb leads) are abnormal.\n- PR interval: start of P to start of QRS; the time for the impulse to cross atria, AV junction and Purkinje system. Normal 0.12 to 0.20 second.\n- QT interval: total ventricular depolarization plus repolarization, roughly 0.32 to 0.40 second. A prolonged QT raises the risk of torsades de pointes, a lethal ventricular dysrhythmia.\n- PP interval gives the atrial rate and rhythm; RR interval gives the ventricular rate and rhythm.\n- U wave: may reflect Purkinje repolarization; seen mainly when potassium is low (hypokalemia).\n\n## Link to disease\n- ST elevation, abnormal Q waves and T-wave inversion point to infarction: [[Myocardial Infarction: Presentation and Cardiac Markers]].\n- ST depression and T inversion suggest ischemia: [[Angina Pectoris: Types, Triggers and Findings]].\n- Low-voltage QRS, ST elevation or heart block can appear in [[Myocarditis]].\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- \"Which wave represents ventricular repolarization?\" and \"a U wave suggests which electrolyte problem?\"\n\n## Related topics\n[[Cardiac Conduction System and Autonomic Control]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Normal QT varies with heart rate; the handout's single range is a simplification.",

"id": "np3-cr-cad-atherosclerosis",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Coronary Artery Disease and Atherosclerosis",
"body": "## What it is\nCoronary artery disease (CAD) is the most common cardiovascular disease in adults. Its usual cause is atherosclerosis: fatty plaque building up in the artery wall. (Arteriosclerosis is the general term for thickened, hardened arteries; atherosclerosis is the fatty-plaque type.) It also drives stroke (CVA). When the narrowed lumen cannot meet the heart's demand, the cells become ischemic.\n\n## Risk factors\n- Modifiable: hyperlipidemia, smoking and tobacco, hypertension, diabetes, metabolic syndrome, obesity, physical inactivity.\n- Non-modifiable: family history (a first-degree relative with heart disease before 55 in men or 65 in women), increasing age (over 45 men, over 55 women), male sex, and race.\n\n## Findings\n- May be silent for years. Then: chest pain, palpitations, dyspnea, syncope, marked fatigue.\n\n## Treatment\n- Procedures: PTCA (balloon compresses plaque and widens the vessel), laser angioplasty (vaporizes plaque), atherectomy (removes plaque), stent (keeps the vessel open, reduces re-narrowing), CABG (bypass graft around the blockage).\n- Drugs: nitrates (dilate coronary arteries, lower preload and afterload), calcium channel blockers (dilate, reduce spasm), cholesterol-lowering drugs, beta-blockers.\n\n## Nursing focus\nTeach risk-factor control: stop smoking, manage BP and glucose, lose weight, exercise. See [[Lipid Profile and Heart-Healthy Diet]] and [[Angina Pectoris: Types, Triggers and Findings]].\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Which risk factor can the client change? Stopping smoking is usually the strongest teaching answer. Also see [[Stop Smoking and Tobacco Cessation]].\n\n## Related topics\n[[Angina Treatment: Procedures and Drugs]], [[Myocardial Infarction: Management and Drug Therapy]]",

"title": "Lipid Profile and Heart-Healthy Diet",
"body": "## What it is\nThe fasting lipid profile (total cholesterol, LDL, HDL, triglycerides) measures cardiac risk. All adults 20 and older should have one at least every 5 years, more often if abnormal.\n\n## Handout values\n- Total cholesterol: 140 to 199 mg/dL\n- LDL: lower than 130 mg/dL\n- HDL: 30 to 70 mg/dL\n- Triglycerides: lower than 200 mg/dL\n\n## Must-know facts\n- LDL is the \"bad\" carrier that deposits cholesterol in vessel walls. High total cholesterol, high LDL and high triglycerides raise CAD risk.\n- HDL is \"good\": it carries lipoproteins such as LDL to the liver for breakdown and excretion, so a high HDL is protective.\n- Cholesterol is found in all tissues, in nerve and brain cells and cell membranes, and in some gallstones.\n\n## Diet teaching\n- Low calorie, low sodium, low cholesterol, low fat, with more dietary fiber.\n- A Mediterranean-style pattern (vegetables, fish, limited red meat) is reported to lower cardiovascular deaths.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Pick the meal choice that fits a client with high LDL; or which lab result is protective (high HDL).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Coronary Artery Disease and Atherosclerosis]], [[Hypertension: Classification and Risk Factors]], [[Nutrition]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Reference ranges differ between sources and labs (especially LDL targets and HDL lower limits). Use your textbook's values.",

"id": "np3-cr-hf-overview",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Heart Failure: Types, Causes and Compensation",
"body": "## What it is\nHeart failure (HF) is the heart's inability to pump enough blood to meet tissue needs for oxygen and nutrients. The dominant problem is poor tissue perfusion from low cardiac output.\n\n## Causes\nHypertension, CAD, cardiomyopathy, substance abuse (alcohol, cocaine, amphetamines), valve disease, previous MI, congenital defects, cardiac infection or inflammation, and high-output (hyperkinetic) states.\n\n## Types\n- Systolic HF: weakened muscle that cannot contract well.\n- Diastolic HF: stiff, non-compliant muscle that cannot fill well.\n- Symptoms depend on which ventricle fails: see [[Left-Sided versus Right-Sided Heart Failure]].\n\n## ACC/AHA stages\n- A: high risk, no structural disease, no symptoms.\n- B: structural disease or LV dysfunction, still no symptoms.\n- C: structural disease with current or past symptoms.\n- D: refractory end-stage disease needing specialized treatment.\n\n## Compensation (and why it backfires)\n- Sympathetic stimulation: faster heart rate, stronger beat, but arterial vasoconstriction raises afterload and workload.\n- Renin-angiotensin system: low renal perfusion releases renin, making angiotensin I, which ACE (in the lungs) converts to angiotensin II, a strong vasoconstrictor. Angiotensin II also triggers aldosterone (sodium and water retention) and ADH. Result: fluid overload. This is why ACE inhibitors and diuretics help ([[Drugs Used in Heart Failure]]).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Explain why daily weights and sodium limits matter, or which drug blocks which step of the cascade.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Cardiac Output, Preload, Afterload and Contractility]], [[Cardiomyopathies: Dilated, Hypertrophic and Restrictive]]",

"pathophysiology"

"id": "np3-cr-hf-left-right",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Left-Sided versus Right-Sided Heart Failure",
"body": "## What it is\nLeft HF backs blood into the lungs; right HF backs blood into the body. Quick rule from the handout: Left = Lung.\n\n## Left-sided findings\n- Pulmonary congestion: dyspnea, cough, crackles, low oxygen saturation, orthopnea.\n- Frothy, pink, blood-tinged sputum signals pulmonary edema (an emergency).\n- An S3 \"ventricular gallop\" may be heard.\n\n## Right-sided findings\n- Jugular vein distention, dependent edema (feet, or sacrum if bed-bound), hepatomegaly, ascites, weight gain from fluid.\n- Left failure is the most common cause of right failure; sustained pulmonary hypertension also causes it.\n\n## Priority nursing actions (reasoning: breathing and oxygenation first)\n1. Sit the client upright (semi-Fowler's or higher; some prefer a chair), give oxygen as ordered to ease work of breathing.\n2. Monitor vital signs, cardiac rhythm and breath sounds.\n3. Give diuretics as prescribed; record intake and output.\n4. Weigh daily, same scale and time, to catch fluid gain early.\n5. For right HF: assess edema, measure abdominal girth, avoid unnecessary IV fluids.\n6. Teach low-sodium eating.\n\n## Common nursing diagnoses\nImpaired gas exchange, decreased cardiac output, activity intolerance, and risk for pulmonary edema, pneumonia and dysrhythmias.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- \"Which finding needs action first?\" Pink frothy sputum or worsening dyspnea outranks edema (Maslow: oxygenation).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Heart Failure: Types, Causes and Compensation]], [[Drugs Used in Heart Failure]], [[Heart Sounds S1 to S4]]",

"id": "np3-cr-hf-drugs",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Drugs Used in Heart Failure",
"body": "## What it is\nHeart failure drugs work on volume, afterload, heart rate and contractility.\n\n## Drug groups from the handout\n- Diuretics (furosemide, bumetanide, metolazone, hydrochlorothiazide, spironolactone): reduce blood volume and relieve symptoms. Watch for potassium changes; most waste potassium, spironolactone keeps it.\n- ACE inhibitors (captopril, enalapril, lisinopril): lower afterload.\n- Beta-blockers (metoprolol, atenolol, carvedilol): help raise ejection fraction and reduce ventricular size.\n- Inotrope (digoxin): strengthens contraction.\n- Vasodilators (nitroprusside, nitroglycerin ointment): lower preload and ease dyspnea.\n\n## Nursing points\n- Check potassium before and during diuretic or digoxin therapy; low potassium raises digoxin toxicity risk.\n- Take apical pulse before digoxin and hold and report if it is below the ordered threshold or if toxicity signs appear (see [[Myocarditis]] for the toxicity picture).\n- Monitor BP before ACE inhibitors and beta-blockers.\n- Pair drugs with daily weights and sodium restriction.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Match drug to purpose (digoxin = contractility, ACE inhibitor = afterload, diuretic = volume, nitrate = preload).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Heart Failure: Types, Causes and Compensation]], [[Left-Sided versus Right-Sided Heart Failure]], [[Essential Drugs]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Specific dosing and hold parameters are not in the handout. Use your pharmacology text.",

"title": "Valvular Heart Disease: Overview and Mitral Stenosis",
"body": "## What it is\nValve disease means a valve does not open fully (stenosis) or does not close fully, letting blood leak back (insufficiency / regurgitation). Types: mitral stenosis, mitral regurgitation, mitral valve prolapse, aortic stenosis, aortic regurgitation.\n\n## Mitral stenosis\n- Usually follows rheumatic endocarditis: fibrosis and calcification thicken and narrow the valve.\n- Blood cannot leave the left atrium easily, so left atrial pressure rises and the atrium dilates; pressure backs up to the pulmonary artery and may eventually cause right-sided failure.\n\n## Findings\n- Low-pitched, rumbling diastolic murmur at the apex.\n- Dyspnea on exertion, orthopnea, paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea (sudden breathlessness during sleep), dry cough, hemoptysis, pulmonary edema, atrial dysrhythmias.\n\n## Treatment\n- Anticoagulants to prevent atrial clots.\n- Valvuloplasty (including percutaneous balloon type) or mitral valve replacement.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Position high Fowler's to ease breathing.\n- Monitor for pulmonary edema, thrombus/embolism, arrhythmias (palpitations, anxiety), ABGs, and daily weight.\n- Teach symptoms to report; low-sodium, low-fat diet.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Identify murmur timing: diastolic rumble at apex = mitral stenosis. Also expect rheumatic fever history.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Mitral Regurgitation and Mitral Valve Prolapse]], [[Aortic Regurgitation]], [[Aortic Stenosis]], [[Infective Endocarditis]]",

"id": "np3-cr-valve-mitral-regurg",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Mitral Regurgitation and Mitral Valve Prolapse",
"body": "## What it is\n- Mitral regurgitation (insufficiency): the valve leaflets fail to close in systole, so blood flows back from the left ventricle into the left atrium.\n- Mitral valve prolapse: the leaflets bulge into the left atrium during systole. It is one of the commonest causes of regurgitation, along with rheumatic heart disease.\n\n## Findings\n- Most common symptoms: dyspnea, fatigue, weakness.\n- Palpitations, exertional breathlessness and cough from pulmonary congestion.\n- Murmur: high-pitched, blowing SYSTOLIC murmur at the apex.\n\n## Management\n- Afterload reduction (arterial dilation) helps, especially with heart failure: ACE inhibitors.\n- Surgery: valve repair (valvuloplasty) or replacement.\n\n## Nursing points\nAssess for heart failure signs, teach to report worsening breathlessness or swelling, and see HF care in [[Left-Sided versus Right-Sided Heart Failure]].\n\n## Compare stenosis and regurgitation\n- Mitral stenosis: diastolic rumble (see [[Valvular Heart Disease: Overview and Mitral Stenosis]]).\n- Mitral regurgitation: systolic blowing murmur.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Murmur timing and quality questions. Memory trick (made up): regurgitation leaks, so it \"blows\".\n\n## Related topics\n[[Aortic Regurgitation]], [[Infective Endocarditis]]",

"id": "np3-cr-valve-aortic-regurg",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Aortic Regurgitation",
"body": "## What it is\nAortic regurgitation (insufficiency): the aortic valve fails to close in diastole, so blood flows back from the aorta into the left ventricle.\n\n## Causes\nInflammatory lesions deforming the leaflets, rheumatic endocarditis, congenital defects, syphilis, dissecting aneurysm; often idiopathic.\n\n## Findings\n- Forceful heartbeat felt in the head and neck; visible or palpable pulsations at the carotid or temporal arteries.\n- Exertional dyspnea and fatigue.\n- High-pitched, blowing DIASTOLIC murmur at the 3rd or 4th intercostal space, left sternal border.\n- Widened pulse pressure and the water-hammer (Corrigan's) pulse, a sharp rise and quick collapse.\n\n## Management\n- Treatment of choice is aortic valvuloplasty or valve replacement, ideally before left ventricular failure develops; surgery is advised with left ventricular hypertrophy even without symptoms.\n- Drugs: vasodilators (nifedipine), ACE inhibitors (captopril, enalapril, lisinopril, ramipril) or hydralazine.\n- Advise avoiding physical exertion.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Widened pulse pressure and water-hammer pulse point to aortic regurgitation.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Aortic Stenosis]], [[Mitral Regurgitation and Mitral Valve Prolapse]], [[Aneurysms]]",

"id": "np3-cr-valve-aortic-stenosis",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Aortic Stenosis",
"body": "## What it is\nNarrowing of the opening between the left ventricle and aorta. The usual cause is degenerative calcification from years of normal mechanical stress and inflammation.\n\n## Pathophysiology\nAs the opening narrows, the left ventricle pushes harder, uses more energy and compensates by thickening (hypertrophy). The thick muscle needs more oxygen but outflow to body and brain falls.\n\n## Findings (the classic triad)\n- Exertional dyspnea (raised pulmonary venous pressure) and possible pulmonary edema.\n- Syncope and dizziness (less blood to the brain).\n- Angina (high oxygen demand of the left ventricle).\n- Loud, rough systolic murmur over the aortic area.\n- Blood pressure may be normal.\n\n## Treatment\nSurgical aortic valve replacement or percutaneous valvuloplasty.\n\n## Nursing points\nSyncope risk means fall precautions; avoid heavy exertion; watch for angina and heart failure.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Chest pain, fainting and breathlessness on exertion with a systolic ejection murmur at the aortic area.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Aortic Regurgitation]], [[Angina Pectoris: Types, Triggers and Findings]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout says BP is normal; many texts add a narrowed pulse pressure in severe stenosis.",

"cardiovascular",
"valves",
"aortic stenosis"
],
"priority": "medium",
"outlineRefs": [

"title": "Infective Endocarditis",
"body": "## What it is\nA microbial infection of the heart's inner (endothelial) surface, usually in people with prosthetic valves or structural heart defects. Hospital-acquired cases follow indwelling catheters. Damaged endocardium collects fibrin and platelets (vegetations) and infection can erode into valves.\n\n## Assessment findings\n- Petechiae.\n- Osler's nodes: small, PAINFUL nodules in finger or toe pads.\n- Janeway lesions: flat, red-purple, PAINLESS macules on palms, fingers, toes.\n- Roth spots: retinal hemorrhages with pale centers (from emboli).\n- Splinter hemorrhages under nails.\n- Fever and new or changing murmur are typical; emboli can travel (stroke, kidney).\n\n## Treatment\n- Long IV antibiotics, often a continuous infusion for 2 to 6 weeks; penicillin is usually the drug of choice. Fungal cases get an antifungal such as amphotericin B.\n- Prevention: antibiotic prophylaxis for high-risk clients before dental procedures, tonsillectomy or adenoidectomy, bronchoscopy, cystoscopy, or surgery on infected skin or musculoskeletal tissue.\n\n## Home teaching\n- Aseptic technique for IV antibiotics; check the catheter site daily and report infection.\n- Record temperature daily for up to 6 weeks and report fever.\n- Oral hygiene twice daily with a soft toothbrush; avoid flossing and oral irrigators (they cause bacteremia).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Match sign to name (painful Osler, painless Janeway). Also: which procedure needs prophylaxis.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Valvular Heart Disease: Overview and Mitral Stenosis]], [[Myocarditis]]",

"area": "NP3",
"title": "Myocarditis",
"body": "## What it is\nInflammation of the myocardium with cell injury and necrosis. Viral infection is the most common cause; bacteria, protozoa, fungi and parasites can also cause it. It is usually diagnosed when it impairs heart function.\n\n## Findings\n- Early: nonspecific fatigue, dyspnea, palpitations.\n- Later: heart failure signs such as tachycardia, pulmonary edema, diaphoresis, neck vein distention, cardiomegaly.\n- ECG: low-voltage QRS, ST elevation or heart block. S4 and systolic ejection murmur may be heard.\n- Sudden cardiac death or rapid severe HF is possible.\n\n## Management\n- Treat the cause if known (for example penicillin for hemolytic streptococci).\n- Inotropic support (dopamine, dobutamine); nitroprusside or nitroglycerin may reduce afterload.\n- AVOID beta-blockers because they lower contractility (negative inotropes).\n- Sedation and rest lower cardiac workload; an intra-aortic balloon pump or ventricular assist device may be used.\n\n## Nursing alert\nClients with myocarditis are unusually sensitive to digitalis. Watch for toxicity: dysrhythmia, anorexia, nausea, vomiting, headache, malaise.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Which drug should be avoided or monitored closely (beta-blockers avoided, digoxin toxicity).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Heart Failure: Types, Causes and Compensation]], [[Pericarditis and Pericardiocentesis]], [[ECG Waves and Intervals]]",

"title": "Pericarditis and Pericardiocentesis",
"body": "## What it is\nInflammation of the pericardial sac. It may be a primary illness or arise during other medical or surgical conditions. It can lead to fluid accumulation (effusion) and [[Cardiac Tamponade]], or to scarring and a stiff pericardium that restricts filling (constrictive pericarditis), raising systemic venous pressure.\n\n## Assessment\n- Chest pain beneath the clavicle, in the neck or left scapular area; worse with deep inspiration, relieved by sitting and leaning forward.\n- Hallmark sign: pericardial friction rub, creaky or scratchy, heard best at the left lower sternal border (4th intercostal space) with the diaphragm pressed firmly, louder at end-exhalation, best with the client sitting forward.\n\n## Management\n- Analgesics and NSAIDs. The handout says indomethacin is contraindicated because it may reduce coronary blood flow.\n- Pericardial window (drainage into the chest cavity) or pericardiectomy for constriction.\n\n## Pericardiocentesis (removing pericardial fluid)\n- Have emergency resuscitation equipment ready; start a slow IV; raise the bed to 45 to 60 degrees; ultrasound guides the needle.\n- Desired results: lower central venous pressure, rising blood pressure, disappearance of pulsus paradoxus and neck vein distention.\n- Complications: coronary puncture, myocardial trauma, dysrhythmias, pleural laceration, gastric puncture.\n- After: monitor rhythm, BP, venous pressure and heart sounds for return of tamponade.\n\n## Nursing care\nPain control, positioning, emotional support; always be alert for tamponade.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Pain that eases leaning forward plus a friction rub = pericarditis (not MI).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Myocardial Infarction: Presentation and Cardiac Markers]], [[Myocarditis]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Indomethacin guidance is from the handout and differs from some references; verify.",

"id": "np3-cr-tamponade",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Cardiac Tamponade",
"body": "## What it is\nFluid collecting in the pericardial sac squeezes the heart so it cannot fill, and cardiac output falls. A sudden build-up of just over 50 mL can cause acute tamponade.\n\n## Causes\nCardiac trauma, complication of myocardial infarction, pericarditis.\n\n## Findings\n- Beck's triad: jugular vein distention, hypotension, muffled/distant heart sounds.\n- Pulsus paradoxus: BP falls by more than 10 mm Hg on inspiration.\n- Raised central venous pressure, low cardiac output, anxiety, dyspnea.\n- Echocardiogram shows the fluid; chest X-ray also used.\n\n## Priority nursing actions (unstable, so act fast)\n1. Call for help; move or keep the client in a critical care unit for hemodynamic monitoring.\n2. Give IV fluids as prescribed to support output.\n3. Prepare for pericardiocentesis (see [[Pericarditis and Pericardiocentesis]]).\n4. Monitor for recurrence after drainage.\n5. Recurrent cases may need a pericardial window or pericardiectomy.\n\n## Link to shock\nTamponade is a cause of cardiogenic shock: [[Circulatory Shock: Hypovolemic and Cardiogenic]].\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Falling BP with distended neck veins and quiet heart sounds after chest trauma or MI = tamponade; the action is prepare for pericardiocentesis and notify the provider.\n\n## Memory aid\nBeck's triad = \"3 D's\": Distended veins, Decreased BP, Distant heart sounds (made-up mnemonic).",

"title": "Angina Pectoris: Types, Triggers and Findings",
"body": "## What it is\nEpisodes of chest pain or pressure from a mismatch: coronary blood flow is too low for the heart's oxygen demand. Usually due to atherosclerotic obstruction of at least one major coronary artery.\n\n## Types\n- Stable: predictable, on exertion, relieved by rest or nitroglycerin.\n- Unstable (pre-infarction/crescendo): more frequent and severe, may not ease with rest or nitroglycerin. Treat as an emergency.\n- Intractable/refractory: severe, disabling pain.\n- Variant (Prinzmetal's): pain at rest with reversible ST elevation, from coronary spasm.\n- Silent ischemia: ischemic changes without pain.\n\n## Triggers\nExertion, cold, heavy meals (blood shunts to digestion), stress and strong emotion (catecholamines raise BP, heart rate and workload).\n\n## Findings\n- Heavy retrosternal pressure; radiation to neck, jaw, shoulders, inner upper arms (often left); apprehension, sense of doom.\n- Hallmark: it subsides with rest or nitroglycerin.\n- ECG may show ST depression or T-wave inversion; nuclear scans and angiography confirm.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\nStop activity, sit/rest, check vital signs and give oxygen and nitroglycerin as ordered, get an ECG, and escalate if pain is not relieved (could be MI: [[Myocardial Infarction: Presentation and Cardiac Markers]]).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Differentiate stable from unstable angina, and angina from MI (MI pain lasts longer than about 30 minutes and is not relieved by rest).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Angina Treatment: Procedures and Drugs]], [[Coronary Artery Disease and Atherosclerosis]]",

"id": "np3-cr-angina-treatment",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Angina Treatment: Procedures and Drugs",
"body": "## What it is\nTreatment aims to cut myocardial oxygen demand and raise supply.\n\n## Procedures\n- PTCA: balloon catheter opens a blocked vessel by compressing the plaque.\n- Intracoronary stent: metal mesh scaffold that prevents sudden closure.\n- Atherectomy: catheter with a cutting blade removes plaque.\n- CABG: a graft vessel carries blood beyond the blockage.\n\n## Drug classes\n- Nitrates (nitroglycerin): dilate veins, so blood pools peripherally, less blood returns to the heart and workload falls. Forms: sublingual tablet, spray, topical, IV.\n- Beta-blockers (metoprolol, atenolol): block beta stimulation of the heart, lowering oxygen use.\n- Calcium channel blockers (amlodipine, diltiazem, felodipine): reduce workload; diltiazem is a main choice for vasospasm; useful if beta-blockers fail.\n- Antiplatelets (aspirin, clopidogrel; glycoprotein IIb/IIIa agents abciximab, tirofiban, eptifibatide): prevent platelet clumping.\n- Anticoagulants (heparin, low-molecular-weight heparins enoxaparin, dalteparin): prevent clot formation.\n\n## Nursing points\n- Sit the client down before giving nitroglycerin (headache and hypotension are common).\n- Monitor BP, pulse and pain relief; watch for bleeding with antiplatelets and anticoagulants.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Which drug class acts by venous pooling (nitrates) and why they lower preload.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Angina Pectoris: Types, Triggers and Findings]], [[Myocardial Infarction: Management and Drug Therapy]], [[Coronary Artery Disease and Atherosclerosis]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Nitroglycerin dosing, storage, and how many tablets to take before calling for help are not in the handout; confirm in your text.",

"title": "Myocardial Infarction: Presentation and Cardiac Markers",
"body": "## What it is\nIn myocardial infarction (MI) part of the heart muscle is permanently destroyed, typically because a ruptured plaque forms a clot that completely blocks a coronary artery. The goal is to limit tissue death and prevent complications.\n\n## Risk factors\n- Non-modifiable: age, family history, ethnicity.\n- Modifiable: hypertension, smoking, hyperlipidemia, obesity, diabetes, inactivity, stress.\n\n## Presentation\n- Severe chest pain usually longer than 30 minutes, radiating to left arm, back or jaw, often without a trigger and often in the morning; relieved only by opioids.\n- Nausea, sweating, dyspnea, fear and anxiety, palpitations, fatigue.\n- Cool clammy skin, S3 or S4, dysrhythmias, JVD if pump failure, crackles if congestion, pounding or thready pulse.\n\n## Diagnostics\n- ECG: T-wave changes, ST elevation, abnormal Q wave.\n- Cardiac markers, in order of appearance:\n  - Myoglobin: 1 to 3 hours, first to rise.\n  - Troponin I: 2 to 4 hours, highly specific.\n  - AST: about 8 hours (also liver).\n  - CK-MB: about 24 hours; cardiac-specific.\n  - LDH: about 72 hours.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Order of markers; \"which is the earliest?\" (myoglobin), \"which is most cardiac-specific?\" (troponin, CK-MB).\n- Chest pain priority: ECG, oxygen, notify provider. Compare [[Angina Pectoris: Types, Triggers and Findings]].\n\n## Related topics\n[[Myocardial Infarction: Management and Drug Therapy]], [[ECG Waves and Intervals]], [[Circulatory Shock: Hypovolemic and Cardiogenic]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Marker timing differs slightly between sources; the handout's values are used here. Troponin timing is usually quoted as rising within a few hours.",

"id": "np3-cr-mi-management",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Myocardial Infarction: Management and Drug Therapy",
"body": "## What it is\nManagement reopens the blocked artery and balances oxygen supply with demand.\n\n## Goals\n- Reperfuse the area (emergency thrombolytics or invasive procedures).\n- Reduce oxygen demand and raise supply: oxygen, bed rest, drugs.\n\n## Drugs\n- Morphine IV: handout calls it the drug of choice; relieves pain, dilates vessels and lowers oxygen demand.\n- Oxygen.\n- Nitroglycerin: vasodilator.\n- Aspirin (salicylate) and thienopyridine antiplatelets (clopidogrel, prasugrel); heparin as anticoagulant.\n- Thrombolytics dissolve the clot so blood can flow again.\n- ACE inhibitors (or ARBs) lower BP and workload.\n- Beta-blockers (-olol).\n- Statins (atorvastatin, rosuvastatin) lower cholesterol.\n- Old shorthand is MONA (Morphine, Oxygen, Nitroglycerin, Aspirin); the handout's longer \"THROMBINS2\" mnemonic adds the rest.\n\n## Priority nursing actions (ABCs and time-critical)\n1. Assess ABCs, give oxygen, attach cardiac monitor, 12-lead ECG.\n2. Establish IV access, give aspirin/nitroglycerin/morphine as ordered.\n3. Prepare for reperfusion; \"time is muscle\".\n4. Monitor for dysrhythmias, pump failure, bleeding after thrombolytics.\n5. Keep the client on bed rest and calm.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Immediate action for suspected MI, and which finding after thrombolytics must be reported (bleeding).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Myocardial Infarction: Presentation and Cardiac Markers]], [[Angina Treatment: Procedures and Drugs]], [[Cardiac Arrest and Basic Life Support Principles]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Modern guidelines treat morphine and oxygen more selectively than the older MONA approach (oxygen mainly when saturation is low). Verify with your text. The handout's spelling \"prasurgel\" is prasugrel.",

"id": "np3-cr-cardiomyopathy",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Cardiomyopathies: Dilated, Hypertrophic and Restrictive",
"body": "## What it is\nCardiomyopathy is a disease of the heart muscle with cardiac dysfunction. Three types.\n\n## Dilated\n- Damage to muscle fibres; both ventricles dilate with normal wall thickness and weak systolic function, so output falls.\n- Causes: alcohol abuse, chemotherapy.\n- Findings: fatigue, weakness, left-sided HF, dysrhythmias, moderate to severe cardiomegaly, exertional dyspnea, palpitations.\n\n## Hypertrophic\n- Asymmetric thickening of the ventricle, stiff LV with poor filling, and obstructed outflow. About half are inherited.\n- Findings: dyspnea, angina, fatigue, syncope, palpitations, mild cardiomegaly, ventricular dysrhythmias, heart failure; sudden death is common.\n\n## Restrictive\n- Rigid, fibrosed walls cannot expand or contract; chambers narrowed; cause often unknown. Looks like constrictive pericarditis.\n- Findings: dyspnea, fatigue, right-sided HF, mild to moderate cardiomegaly, heart block.\n\n## Nursing focus\nTreat as heart failure ([[Heart Failure: Types, Causes and Compensation]]), teach alcohol avoidance, monitor rhythm, and teach energy conservation.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Match type to feature: alcohol = dilated; inherited with syncope and sudden death = hypertrophic; right HF with stiff walls = restrictive.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Left-Sided versus Right-Sided Heart Failure]], [[Pericarditis and Pericardiocentesis]]",

"id": "np3-cr-shock-hypo-cardio",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Circulatory Shock: Hypovolemic and Cardiogenic",
"body": "## What it is\nShock is inadequate organ perfusion for the tissues' oxygen needs; hypoperfusion can exist even without marked hypotension. Three groups: hypovolemic, cardiogenic and distributive (see [[Distributive Shock: Neurogenic, Anaphylactic and Septic]]).\n\n## Hypovolemic shock\n- Fluid (blood or plasma) is lost: hemorrhage, severe vomiting or diarrhea, dehydration, burns or fistulas, massive third spacing.\n- Treatment: stop or treat the cause and replace volume with blood, plasma, fluids and electrolytes.\n- Goal: restore intravascular volume, redistribute fluid and correct the cause as fast as possible.\n\n## Cardiogenic shock\n- The pump fails: heart failure, MI, cardiac tamponade.\n- Treatment: limit further heart damage, protect healthy muscle, and improve function by raising contractility and/or lowering afterload.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Airway, breathing, oxygen.\n2. Control visible bleeding (hypovolemic); IV access.\n3. Monitor BP, pulse, urine output, level of consciousness, skin.\n4. In cardiogenic shock, be careful with big fluid volumes (lungs already congested) and follow orders.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Identify type from the cause; recognise early signs (anxiety, restlessness, tachycardia) before BP drops.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Types of Shock]], [[Management of Shock]], [[Circulation and Shock Overview]], [[Cardiac Tamponade]]",

"id": "np3-cr-shock-distributive",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Distributive Shock: Neurogenic, Anaphylactic and Septic",
"body": "## What it is\nIn distributive shock, widespread vasodilation lowers blood pressure and tissue perfusion even though blood volume may be normal.\n\n## Neurogenic\n- Loss of autonomic (sympathetic) tone below a spinal cord lesion causes rapid vasodilation and pooling of blood in peripheral vessels.\n- Treatment restores sympathetic tone: stabilise the spinal injury and position properly.\n- To avoid it with spinal or epidural anesthesia, keep the head of the bed raised at least 30 degrees so the anesthetic does not spread upward.\n\n## Anaphylactic\n- Allergic reaction releases histamine, causing systemic vasodilation.\n- Remove the trigger (stop the drug), support airway and breathing, give epinephrine (vasoconstriction; emergency drug) and diphenhydramine (blocks histamine, reduces capillary leak).\n\n## Septic\n- Body reaction to bacterial toxins (often gram-negative) leaks plasma into tissues.\n- Find and eliminate the infection.\n\n## Summary of mechanisms\n- Hypovolemic: lost volume. Cardiogenic: weak pump. Neurogenic: lost vasomotor tone. Anaphylactic: allergy. Septic: infection.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\nAirway first (anaphylaxis can close the airway), then oxygen, IV access, epinephrine as ordered, monitor vital signs.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- First action for a client who develops hives and wheeze after an antibiotic: stop the drug, check airway, call for help.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Circulatory Shock: Hypovolemic and Cardiogenic]], [[Sepsis and Septic Shock Recognition]], [[Types of Shock]]",

"title": "Hypertension: Classification and Risk Factors",
"body": "## What it is\nThe handout defines hypertension as systolic BP above 140 mm Hg and diastolic above 90 mm Hg.\n\n## Types\n- Essential (primary): no known direct cause. Risk factors: age over 60, family history, excess calories, inactivity, heavy alcohol use, hyperlipidemia, high salt or caffeine, low potassium/calcium/magnesium intake, smoking.\n- Secondary: caused by another disease, such as renal vascular or parenchymal disease, primary aldosteronism, pheochromocytoma, Cushing's disease, coarctation of the aorta, brain tumors, encephalitis.\n\n## Nursing goals\nLower and control BP without side effects or unnecessary cost by:\n- Adhering to the treatment plan.\n- Making lifestyle changes (weight, salt, alcohol, smoking, exercise).\n- Taking drugs as prescribed.\n- Keeping follow-up appointments.\n\n## Teaching points\nHypertension is often silent, so clients stop drugs when they feel well; stress that medicines are lifelong unless the provider changes them, and rise slowly from lying or sitting (orthostatic hypotension).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Which risk factor is modifiable; which sign suggests secondary hypertension in a young person.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Antihypertensive Drug Classes]], [[Coronary Artery Disease and Atherosclerosis]], [[Aneurysms]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Many current guidelines (for example ACC/AHA) use 130/80 as the threshold. "title": "Antihypertensive Drug Classes",
"body": "## What it is\nFor uncomplicated hypertension without another indication, the handout lists diuretics, beta-blockers and ACE inhibitors as initial choices.\n\n## Classes\n- Beta-blockers: lower BP by reducing cardiac output, decreasing sympathetic stimulation and inhibiting renin release.\n- ACE inhibitors (captopril, enalapril, lisinopril): reduce peripheral resistance without raising CO, HR or contractility; decrease aldosterone.\n- Central alpha agonist (clonidine): acts on the CNS to reduce sympathetic outflow; does not reduce renal blood flow.\n- Calcium channel blockers (verapamil, amlodipine, diltiazem, nicardipine).\n- Diuretics: thiazide (chlorothiazide, hydrochlorothiazide), loop (furosemide, bumetanide), potassium-sparing (spironolactone).\n\n## Nursing points\n- Check BP and pulse before giving; hold and report per order or policy.\n- Diuretics and electrolytes: monitor potassium (loop and thiazide lower it; spironolactone raises it).\n- Teach slow position changes, daily weight if on diuretics, and not to stop abruptly (especially beta-blockers and clonidine).\n- Encourage lifestyle measures along with drugs.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Match class to action; or which lab to watch with each diuretic.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Hypertension: Classification and Risk Factors]], [[Drugs Used in Heart Failure]], [[Essential Drugs]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout calls beta-blockers \"first line\"; modern guidance generally favours ACE inhibitors/ARBs, calcium channel blockers or thiazides first. "id": "np3-cr-buergers",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Buerger's Disease (Thromboangiitis Obliterans)",
"body": "## What it is\nRecurring inflammation of the small and medium arteries and veins of the arms and legs, with clot formation that narrows the vessels. It is strongly linked to heavy smoking and tobacco chewing and typically affects men aged 20 to 35.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Tobacco is the driver: the disease flares with nicotine and calms when all nicotine stops.\n- Both arms and legs can be involved, mostly the legs.\n- Cause of damage: reduced blood flow leads to tissue ischemia.\n- Vasodilators are rarely prescribed (the handout's point).\n\n## Assessment\n- Foot cramps, especially in the arch (instep claudication), after exercise; eased by rest.\n- Intense rubor, a reddish-blue color of the foot, and absent pedal pulses.\n- Check temperature, color, capillary refill, pulses and skin breaks in all four limbs.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Stop all nicotine, including gum and patches: this is the single action that changes the course (cause removal).\n2. Protect the limbs from injury and cold: skin breaks heal poorly with poor blood flow (safety).\n3. Inspect feet daily; wear well-fitting footwear; keep feet clean and dry.\n4. Report new pain at rest, sores, black or very cold skin at once.\n\n## Treatment and medications\n- Improve circulation and prevent progression. Pain control as needed. Vasodilators are rarely used.\n\n## Complications and red flags\n- Ulcers and gangrene, which may lead to amputation (beyond the handout, standard).\n\n## Client teaching\n- Quit smoking and avoid secondhand smoke; walking within limits; avoid very hot or cold exposure.\n- Support for cessation: see [[Stop Smoking and Tobacco Cessation]].\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Young male heavy smoker, instep pain with exercise relieved by rest, absent pedal pulse = Buerger's. Best teaching: stop smoking and tobacco. "area": "NP3",
"title": "Raynaud's Phenomenon",
"body": "## What it is\nIntermittent narrowing of small arteries (arteriolar vasoconstriction) in the fingertips or toes, causing coldness, pain and pallor. It is most common in women 16 to 40 and is more frequent in cold climates.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Color sequence: white (no flow), blue (oxygen used up), then red (blood returns).\n- Numbness, tingling and burning pain as color changes.\n- Usually bilateral and symmetric; toes and fingers.\n- Triggers: cold and tobacco; stress is a commonly added trigger (standard teaching).\n\n## Assessment\n- Skin color, temperature, pain, sensation, nail and skin condition. Ask what brought on the attack.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Warm the hands or feet gently and remove from the cold (remove the trigger first).\n2. Keep skin intact; avoid tight rings, gloves or shoes.\n3. Teach to avoid triggers: cold exposure, smoking, nicotine gum and patches.\n4. Give calcium channel blockers as ordered and monitor BP.\n\n## Treatment and medications\n- Avoiding triggers is the primary control.\n- Calcium channel blockers (nifedipine, amlodipine) widen vessels.\n- Sympathectomy (cutting or removing sympathetic ganglia or branches) is an option for some.\n\n## Complications and red flags\n- Sores or tissue damage on fingertips that do not heal (beyond the handout).\n\n## Client teaching\n- Wear gloves, warm socks and layers; carry a sweater into air-conditioned rooms.\n- Avoid all nicotine.\n- Report non-healing sores.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Sequence white-blue-red; young woman; best teaching = avoid cold and nicotine. Compare [[Buerger's Disease (Thromboangiitis Obliterans)]] (male, smoker, absent pulses).\n\n## Memory aid\nWhite, blue, red - no flow, no oxygen, flow returns (made-up aid).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Peripheral Vascular Disorders Compared: Buerger, Raynaud, DVT, Aneurysm]], [[Stop Smoking and Tobacco Cessation]], [[Antihypertensive Drug Classes]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Stress as a trigger and non-healing sore warning are standard teaching, not in the handout.",

"title": "Deep Vein Thrombosis",
"body": "## What it is\nA blood clot in a deep vein, usually of the leg. Virchow's triad explains why: vessel wall injury, venous stasis and altered coagulation. Stasis risks: bed rest, obesity, varicose veins, spinal cord injury, age over 65.\n\n## Findings\nSwelling of the limb (outflow blocked), pain, heaviness, ankle engorgement, edema, reduced function.\n\n## Prevention\n- Graduated compression stockings.\n- Subcutaneous heparin or low-molecular-weight heparin for surgical clients.\n- Weight loss, quitting smoking, regular exercise.\n\n## Treatment\n- Anticoagulants: heparin first, warfarin orally long term; thrombolytics (alteplase, urokinase, streptokinase) in selected cases.\n- Monitor aPTT with heparin; PT and INR with warfarin.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Do not massage the leg (a clot could break off and travel to the lungs: [[Pulmonary Embolism]]).\n2. Elevate the leg, give analgesics, apply warm moist packs.\n3. Monitor clotting tests and watch for bleeding.\n4. Walk once anticoagulated; avoid long sitting or standing.\n\n## Stocking tip\nFor ambulatory clients, remove stockings at night and put them on before the legs touch the floor in the morning.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Which lab for warfarin (INR/PT) vs heparin (aPTT); what sign suggests the clot has moved (sudden dyspnea, chest pain).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Pulmonary Embolism]], [[Buerger's Disease (Thromboangiitis Obliterans)]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout says to apply warm moist packs; some references limit local heat. "title": "Aneurysms",
"body": "## What it is\nA localized sac or dilation at a weak point in an artery wall.\n\n## Causes\nCongenital connective tissue disease (Marfan's), mechanical/hemodynamic (post-stenotic, arteriovenous fistula, amputation-related), trauma (pseudoaneurysm), infection (mycotic), pregnancy-related changes, inflammation, and graft or anastomosis failure.\n\n## Types\n- True aneurysm: one, two or all three layers of the artery are involved.\n- False aneurysm: a pulsating hematoma; clot and connective tissue lie outside the arterial wall.\n- Fusiform: symmetric spindle-shaped swelling of the whole circumference.\n- Saccular: bulge on one side of the wall.\n- Dissecting: a hematoma that splits the layers of the wall.\n\n## Management\n- Medical: keep BP controlled to prevent rupture using diuretics, beta-blockers, ACE inhibitors, ARBs or calcium channel blockers.\n- Surgical: resection with bypass graft, or endovascular stent-graft placed across the aneurysm.\n\n## Nursing focus\nMonitor BP, teach to report sudden severe pain (rupture or dissection), and avoid straining.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Match the shape to the name; identify the BP goal.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Hypertension: Classification and Risk Factors]], [[Antihypertensive Drug Classes]], [[Aortic Regurgitation]]",

"id": "np3-cr-resp-anatomy",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Respiratory Anatomy and Control of Breathing",
"body": "## What it is\nThe respiratory tract has an upper part (nose, sinuses, pharynx, larynx, trachea) that filters, warms and moistens air, and a lower part (lungs, pleura, bronchi, bronchioles, alveoli) that exchanges gas to regulate PaO2, PaCO2 and pH.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Nose: passageway that filters, humidifies and warms air.\n- Paranasal sinuses: resonating chambers for speech.\n- Pharynx: tube joining nasal and oral cavities to the larynx.\n- Larynx: the voice organ, joining pharynx and trachea.\n- Trachea (windpipe): passage between larynx and bronchi.\n- Lungs sit in the airtight thoracic cage; the pleura is the serous membrane lining the lungs and chest wall.\n- Alveoli are the basic gas-exchange unit; oxygen and carbon dioxide cross the alveolar-capillary membrane by diffusion.\n\n## Control of breathing\n- Neural control is in the medulla, stimulated mainly by the carbon dioxide level in the blood.\n- Chemoreceptors in the carotid arteries and aortic arch are a secondary system that respond to low oxygen (hypoxemia) and also stimulate the medulla.\n- Alveolar stretch receptors signal the brain stem to stop inspiration and prevent over-distention.\n\n## Why it matters\n- This explains why high oxygen can suppress breathing in chronic CO2 retainers: see [[COPD Management and Nursing Care]].\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- \"What is the main stimulus to breathe in a healthy person?\" (rising CO2).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Rhinitis: Allergic and Viral]], [[Breathing Assessment in the Primary Survey]]",

"anatomy",
"physiology"

"body": "## What it is\nPharyngitis is inflammation of the pharynx, commonly called a sore throat.\n\n## Acute\n- Sudden painful inflammation of the back of the throat including the posterior tongue, soft palate and tonsils.\n- Findings: fiery-red pharyngeal membrane and tonsils, swollen lymphoid follicles, tender cervical nodes, fever, malaise, sore throat.\n- Drugs: penicillin is the treatment of choice; cephalosporins or macrolides as alternatives; benzocaine gargles for relief.\n- Nursing: liquid or soft diet, cool or warm fluids, frozen desserts, warm saline gargles, more fluids, ice collar, bed rest during fever, prevention teaching.\n\n## Chronic\n- Persistent inflammation, common in adults who work in dust, overuse the voice, have chronic cough, or use alcohol and tobacco.\n- Types: hypertrophic (thick congested mucosa); atrophic (late stage, thin, whitish, shiny, wrinkled); granular (\"clergyman's sore throat\", swollen lymph follicles on the wall).\n- Findings: constant irritation or fullness, mucus in the throat, difficulty swallowing.\n- Management: decongestant sprays (ephedrine, phenylephrine), antihistamine-decongestants, acetaminophen.\n- Nursing: avoid contact with others while febrile; avoid alcohol, tobacco, smoke, cold and pollutants.\n\n## Why it matters\nUntreated streptococcal throat infection can cause rheumatic fever and valve damage: [[Valvular Heart Disease: Overview and Mitral Stenosis]].\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- First-line antibiotic: penicillin. Alkaline mouthwash or warm saline for thick mucus and halitosis; cool-mist humidifier at home.\n7. "body": "## What it is\nInflammation of the larynx (voice box), often from voice abuse or exposure to dust, chemicals, smoke and other pollutants. The larynx is the voice organ, a cartilage structure joining pharynx and trachea.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- First sign is hoarseness.\n- It can progress to aphonia (complete loss of voice).\n- Severe cough.\n- Throat feels worse in the morning and better in a warmer climate.\n- A hoarse voice lasting more than about 2 weeks is a warning for laryngeal cancer.\n\n## Assessment\n- Voice quality, cough, throat pain, breathing effort, noisy breathing (stridor), smoking and voice-use history.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Give antibacterial therapy if ordered and inhaled corticosteroids if prescribed.\n\n## Treatment and medications\n- Supportive: voice rest, humidity, fluids. Antibiotic only if bacterial. Topical corticosteroids may be given by inhalation.\n\n## Complications and red flags\n- Airway swelling with noisy breathing.\n- Hoarseness lasting more than 2 weeks: refer.\n\n## Client teaching\n- Do not whisper (standard teaching, strains the cords); avoid smoking and alcohol; keep air humid.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Best advice: voice rest, humidity, stop smoking. "id": "np3-cr-copd-types",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "COPD: Chronic Bronchitis and Emphysema",
"body": "## What it is\nCOPD is airflow limitation that is not fully reversible, usually progressive, with an inflammatory lung response to irritants (smoking). It includes chronic bronchitis and emphysema.\n\n## Chronic bronchitis (\"blue bloater\")\n- Chronic inflammation with excess mucus; defined by productive cough for at least 3 months a year for 2 consecutive years.\n- Thick, gelatinous sputum, more with infection; wheeze and dyspnea as disease progresses; insidious onset.\n\n## Emphysema (\"pink puffer\")\n- Destruction of alveolar walls and enlarged air spaces; slow decline over years.\n- Panlobular type: whole lobule affected, hyperinflated chest, marked exertional dyspnea, weight loss.\n- Centrilobular type: damage in the center of the lobule; V/Q mismatch gives chronic hypoxemia, hypercapnia, polycythemia, right-sided heart failure, central cyanosis.\n- Findings: dyspnea, reduced exercise tolerance, minimal cough, barrel chest (increased anteroposterior diameter from air trapping and flat diaphragm).\n\n## Diagnostics\n- Spirometry shows airflow obstruction.\n- ABG: low PaO2 and pH, raised CO2.\n- Chest X-ray late: hyperinflation, flat diaphragm, widened retrosternal space, fewer vascular markings, bullae.\n- Alpha-1-antitrypsin assay identifies the inherited deficiency.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Identify type from appearance: blue bloater (cyanotic, edematous, productive cough) vs pink puffer (thin, breathless, barrel chest).\n\n## Related topics\n[[COPD Management and Nursing Care]], [[Bronchial Asthma]], [[Respiratory Failure Recognition]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout's panlobular details (\"negative pressure required during inspiration\") are unclear; confirm. Many texts attribute the \"blue bloater\" label more to bronchitis and \"pink puffer\" to emphysema, as given here.",

"area": "NP3",
"title": "COPD Management and Nursing Care",
"body": "## What it is\nCare aims to slow progression, relieve symptoms and keep the client active.\n\n## Medical and surgical management\n- Smoking cessation (most important), bronchodilators, inhaled and systemic corticosteroids, alpha-1-antitrypsin augmentation, antibiotics, mucolytics, antitussives.\n- Bullectomy: removes large non-functioning air spaces.\n- Lung volume reduction surgery: removes diseased lung tissue.\n\n## Priority nursing actions (breathing first)\n1. Monitor respiratory rate and pattern, breath sounds, signs of distress.\n2. Give LOW-flow oxygen (1 to 2 L/min): in chronic CO2 retainers, high oxygen can remove the hypoxic drive and depress breathing.\n3. Give bronchodilators as prescribed; hydrate to thin secretions.\n4. Teach pursed-lip breathing (slows expiration, keeps small airways open) and diaphragmatic breathing coordinated with activity.\n5. Chest physiotherapy morning and night if ordered.\n6. Small frequent meals and nutritional supplements to counter weight loss.\n7. Alternate activity with rest; teach relaxation; avoid bronchial irritants.\n8. Refer to pulmonary rehabilitation.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Oxygen flow for COPD; best breathing technique; position (upright, leaning forward).\n\n## Related topics\n[[COPD: Chronic Bronchitis and Emphysema]], [[Stop Smoking and Tobacco Cessation]], [[Nutrition]]",

"title": "Occupational Lung Diseases: Asbestosis, Silicosis, Sarcoidosis",
"body": "## What it is\nLung scarring from breathed-in dusts, or granuloma formation.\n\n## The conditions\n- Asbestosis: diffuse interstitial fibrosis from asbestos dust; workers who make, cut or demolish asbestos materials. Progressive, irreversible exertional dyspnea and bibasal crackles.\n- Silicosis: chronic fibrosis from silica dust; mining (gold, coal, tin, copper), stone cutting, quarrying, abrasives, ceramics, pottery, foundry work. Productive chronic cough.\n- Sarcoidosis: granulomas (clumps of inflammatory cells) in many organs, mainly lungs; lymph node enlargement on chest X-ray.\n\n## Management\n- No specific cure; remove exposure and treat symptoms.\n- Prophylactic isoniazid for silicosis clients with a positive tuberculin test (high TB risk).\n- Asbestos-exposed clients should stop smoking and be watched for lung cancer (changing cough, hemoptysis, weight loss, melena).\n- Bronchodilators if airway obstruction.\n\n## Nursing actions\nOxygen, bronchodilator teaching, smoking cessation, pacing activity, and prevention education for healthy workers.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Match the dust to the disease and the occupation; link silicosis to TB risk.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Pulmonary Tuberculosis: Diagnosis and Classification]], [[Anti-TB Drugs and Nursing Care]]",

"id": "np3-cr-pneumothorax",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Pneumothorax: Types and Findings",
"body": "## What it is\nAir in the pleural space because the pleura was breached, so the lung collapses.\n\n## Types\n- Simple/spontaneous: air enters through a ruptured bleb or bronchopleural fistula.\n- Traumatic: from rib fracture, stab or gunshot wounds, or diaphragmatic tear.\n- Open: a chest wall wound large enough for air to pass in and out with each breath (\"sucking chest wound\").\n- Tension: air enters on inspiration but cannot leave; pressure builds, the lung collapses and the heart, great vessels and trachea shift toward the unaffected side (mediastinal shift). A life-threatening emergency.\n\n## Findings\n- Hyperresonance, diminished breath sounds, reduced movement on the affected side.\n- Tension: tracheal deviation away from the injured side, air hunger, agitation, hypotension, cyanosis, heavy sweating.\n- Spontaneous: mild to moderate dyspnea and chest discomfort.\n\n## Why it matters\nTension pneumothorax threatens cardiovascular collapse, so it is treated before imaging: decompress immediately.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Recognise tension (deviated trachea, shock) vs simple. Then see [[Pneumothorax: Management and Chest Tube Care]].\n\n## Related topics\n[[Hemothorax]], [[Breathing Assessment in the Primary Survey]], [[Primary vs Secondary Survey]]",

"title": "Pneumothorax: Management and Chest Tube Care",
"body": "## What it is\nTreatment re-expands the lung and restores ventilation.\n\n## By type\n- Spontaneous: if small in a healthy person, observe for spontaneous resolution; larger ones need needle aspiration or chest tube. Recurrent cases: pleurodesis or thoracotomy with resection of apical blebs.\n- Tension: immediate decompression (needle thoracentesis or chest tube) to prevent cardiovascular collapse, then chest tube with underwater-seal drainage.\n- Open: close the wound immediately with petroleum gauze/occlusive dressing; chest tube with water-seal drainage; surgery may be needed.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Airway, breathing, oxygen; monitor oximetry and ABGs.\n2. Cover a sucking wound at once.\n3. Assist with emergency thoracentesis or thoracostomy.\n4. Upright position if condition allows (best lung expansion).\n5. Keep chest tubes patent (no kinks, drainage below the chest, tubing unclamped unless ordered).\n6. Splint the chest when turning or coughing; give pain medicine so the client can breathe deeply.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- First action for a stab wound with bubbling air: cover it. For tension signs: prepare for decompression and notify the provider.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Pneumothorax: Types and Findings]], [[Hemothorax]], [[Pleural Effusion]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Size thresholds for observation (the handout says under 50%) vary; many texts use smaller cutoffs.\n- The handout describes a Valsalva-type breath while applying the dressing. Common teaching is an occlusive dressing taped on three sides to allow air to escape. Verify which your exam favors.",
