"body": "## What it is\nBlood in the pleural space from penetrating or blunt chest trauma. It accompanies a high share of chest injuries and can hide large blood loss, so the client may deteriorate quickly.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- The client may be symptom-free, short of breath, apprehensive, or in shock.\n- Often seen together with air (hemopneumothorax), so drainage must remove blood and air.\n- Compare: pneumothorax (air), effusion (fluid), empyema (pus).\n\n## Assessment\n- Breath sounds on both sides, respiratory rate and effort, oxygen saturation, BP and pulse, skin color, level of consciousness, chest wall injuries.\n- Chest drainage: amount, color, and rate of blood.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Airway, breathing, oxygen; watch for shock (ABC).\n2. Establish IV access and replace volume with IV fluids or blood products as ordered (circulation).\n3. Assist with thoracentesis or chest tube insertion; connect drainage for complete and continuous removal of blood and air.\n4. Auscultate lungs and watch for relief of dyspnea.\n5. Record the amount of bloody drainage regularly; report sudden large output or a sudden stop with worsening dyspnea (possible clotted tube).\n6. Keep the drainage system below chest level and sealed.\n\n## Treatment and medications\n- Chest tube drainage, volume replacement, pain relief; surgery may be needed if bleeding continues (beyond handout).\n\n## Complications and red flags\n- Hypovolemic shock; respiratory failure; infection (empyema).\n\n## Client teaching\n- Splint the chest to cough; report increasing breathlessness; keep tubing free of kinks.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- After chest trauma, falling BP, rising pulse and decreased breath sounds mean bleeding into the chest. First action: airway, oxygen, IV access.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Pneumothorax: Management and Chest Tube Care]], [[Pleural Effusion]], [[Circulatory Shock: Hypovolemic and Cardiogenic]], [[Pleural and Chest Cavity Problems Compared]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The thresholds for surgery and the clotted-tube warning are general practice.",

"title": "Pleurisy (Pleuritis)",
"body": "## What it is\nInflammation of both pleural layers (parietal and visceral). It may accompany pneumonia, an upper respiratory infection, TB or a collagen disease. The inflamed surfaces rub together during breathing, giving sharp, knife-like pain that is worse on inspiration.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Pain is stabbing, limited to one area, and worse with deep breathing, coughing or sneezing.\n- Pleural friction rub heard on auscultation.\n- Breathing becomes shallow to avoid pain, which can lead to poor lung expansion.\n- Compare: pericarditis pain eases when leaning forward; angina and MI do not change with breathing.\n\n## Assessment\n- Pain pattern, breathing rate and depth, breath sounds and friction rub, fever, cough.\n- Diagnostics: chest X-ray, sputum analysis, thoracentesis, pleural biopsy.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Treat the underlying cause (give ordered antibiotics for infection).\n2. Pain relief: analgesics as ordered; heat or cold application.\n3. Teach to lie on the affected side to splint the chest wall and reduce stretching of the pleura.\n4. Teach splinting the ribcage with hands or a pillow while coughing.\n5. Still encourage deep breathing and coughing, so the lungs do not collapse or fill with secretions (atelectasis, pneumonia prevention).\n\n## Treatment and medications\n- Treat the cause; indomethacin for pain relief (handout); intercostal nerve block for severe pain.\n\n## Complications and red flags\n- Pleural effusion, empyema, reduced ventilation from guarding.\n\n## Client teaching\n- Use pain relief before deep breathing practice; report fever, worsening breathlessness.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Pain worse on inspiration with a rub, no relief with rest: pleurisy. Positioning: lie on the painful side.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Pneumonia]], [[Pleural Effusion]], [[Pericarditis and Pericardiocentesis]], [[Chest Pain Compared: Angina, MI, Pericarditis, Pulmonary Embolism, Pleurisy]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Indomethacin guidance is from the handout; its use in pleurisy and the risks of breathing depression with strong opioids should be checked against your drug book.",

"body": "## What it is\nPus (purulent fluid) in the pleural space. It develops as a complication of bacterial pneumonia, lung abscess or chest trauma. The client is acutely ill with signs like an acute respiratory infection.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Diagnosis is confirmed by chest CT.\n- The main objective is to drain the pleural cavity.\n- Drainage choice depends on how thick or complicated the pus is:\n  - Thoracentesis if fluid is not too thick.\n  - Tube thoracostomy (chest tube) for loculated (walled-off) or complicated effusions.\n  - Open chest drainage (thoracotomy) to remove thickened pleura, pus and debris.\n- Antibiotics are also given (standard care, not stated in the handout).\n\n## Assessment\n- Fever, chills, cough, pleuritic pain, dyspnea; breath sounds, oxygen saturation; amount and character of drainage.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Support breathing: oxygen as ordered, upright position (breathing first).\n2. Prepare and assist with the drainage procedure.\n3. Care specific to the drainage method: keep chest tube system sealed, below chest level, patent; record drainage amount and character; dressing care.\n4. Give antibiotics and pain relief on time.\n5. Encourage deep breathing and coughing with chest splinting.\n\n## Treatment and medications\n- Drainage plus antibiotics; surgery for thick pleura.\n\n## Complications and red flags\n- Persistent fever, sepsis, scarring of lung and pleura (beyond the handout).\n\n## Client teaching\n- Importance of finishing antibiotics; deep breathing; report fever or increasing breathlessness.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Empyema = pus; effusion = fluid; hemothorax = blood. "title": "Pneumonia",
"body": "## What it is\nInflammation of the lung tissue caused by bacteria, mycobacteria, fungi or viruses.\n- Community-acquired: occurs in the community or within 48 hours of admission.\n- Hospital-acquired (nosocomial): starts more than 48 hours after admission.\n- Aspiration pneumonia: from entry of substances into the lower airway.\n\n## Findings\n- Sudden high fever (38.3 to 40.5 C), productive cough with purulent or rusty blood-tinged sputum.\n- Pleuritic chest pain, dyspnea, fast breathing, grunting, nasal flaring, accessory muscle use.\n- Rapid bounding pulse, orthopnea, sweating, poor appetite.\n\n## Diagnostics\nChest X-ray (consolidation), sputum Gram stain and culture and sensitivity, blood culture for bacteremia.\n\n## Treatment\n- Antibiotic matched to the organism; the handout lists a macrolide (azithromycin, clarithromycin, erythromycin) for S. pneumoniae.\n- Viral pneumonia: supportive. Oxygen for poor gas exchange.\n- Complications: shock, respiratory failure, pleural effusion.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Oxygenation and semi-Fowler's position.\n2. Collect sputum specimen BEFORE the first antibiotic dose.\n3. Encourage coughing and deep breathing, splinting the chest; suction if needed.\n4. Hydration 2 to 3 L/day to thin secretions.\n5. Rest periods; nutritious fluids.\n6. Teach covering the mouth when coughing; finish the whole 7 to 10 day course; improvement is expected within 48 to 72 hours.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Culture before antibiotics; position for best expansion; signs of failing oxygenation.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Pulmonary Tuberculosis: Diagnosis and Classification]], [[Pleurisy (Pleuritis)]], [[Respiratory Failure Recognition]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Drug choice depends on organism and local guidelines; the handout's list is brief.",

"title": "Pulmonary Tuberculosis: Diagnosis and Classification",
"body": "## What it is\nTB is an infection mainly of the lung tissue; it can spread to meninges, kidneys, bones and lymph nodes. The organism, Mycobacterium tuberculosis, is an acid-fast aerobic rod, slow-growing, sensitive to heat and ultraviolet light, and spread person to person by AIRBORNE transmission.\n\n## Findings\n- Fatigue, loss of appetite, weight loss, low-grade fever, night sweats.\n- Cough starting slowly, becoming more frequent with mucoid or pus-like sputum.\n- Hemoptysis, chest pain and dyspnea suggest extensive disease.\n\n## Diagnosis\n- Sputum smear and culture confirm TB.\n- Chest X-ray shows extent.\n- Tuberculin skin test (PPD/Mantoux) shows exposure, not necessarily active disease.\n\n## Classification (handout)\n- Class 0: no exposure, no infection.\n- Class 1: exposure, no infection evidence.\n- Class 2: latent infection, no disease (positive PPD).\n- Class 3: active disease.\n- Class 4: disease, not clinically active.\n- Class 5: suspected, diagnosis pending.\n\n## Nursing priority\nAirborne precautions: negative-pressure room and a particulate (N95-type) respirator for staff, mask for the client during transport.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Which test confirms (sputum); which precautions (airborne); what a positive PPD means.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Anti-TB Drugs and Nursing Care]], [[Expanded Program on Immunization]], [[BCG and Pentavalent Vaccine]], [[Occupational Lung Diseases: Asbestosis, Silicosis, Sarcoidosis]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Airborne precaution details are added from standard textbooks. Philippine TB programs use their own case definitions; the class system here is the older American one.",

"title": "Anti-TB Drugs and Nursing Care",
"body": "## What it is\nPulmonary TB is treated with several drugs together for a long time (handout: 6 to 12 months).\n\n## Regimen from the handout\n- Initial phase: isoniazid (INH), rifampin, pyrazinamide and ethambutol daily for 8 weeks.\n- Continuation phase: INH and rifampin for another 4 or 7 months.\n- Vitamin B6 (pyridoxine) is given with INH to prevent peripheral neuropathy.\n\n## Main side effects\n- INH: peripheral neuritis, raised liver enzymes, hepatitis, hypersensitivity.\n- Rifampin: hepatitis, fever, nausea and vomiting; (orange-colored body fluids is standard teaching).\n- Pyrazinamide: high uric acid, liver toxicity, rash, joint pain, GI upset.\n- Ethambutol: optic neuritis (vision changes, color discrimination), rash.\n- Handout doses: INH 5 mg/kg (max 300 mg); rifampin 10 mg/kg (max 600 mg); pyrazinamide 15 to 30 mg/kg (max 2 g); ethambutol 15 to 25 mg/kg.\n\n## Nursing actions\n- Take on an empty stomach or at least 1 hour before meals (food reduces absorption).\n- Avoid tyramine- and histamine-rich foods with INH (headache, flushing, low BP, palpitations, sweating).\n- Monitor for side effects, especially liver and vision.\n- Stress completing the whole regimen: stopping early causes drug-resistant TB.\n- Hygiene: cover the mouth when coughing, dispose of tissues, wash hands.\n- Rest, fluids, small frequent nutritious meals.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Match drug to adverse effect; what to teach about adherence.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Pulmonary Tuberculosis: Diagnosis and Classification]], [[Essential Drugs]], [[Treatment of CD, NCD and Emergency Services]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Doses, the 8-week/4-7 month schedule and the food advice are from the handout and may differ from the DOH program. Verify before relying on them. Rifampin sometimes taken with food in practice if GI upset.",

"title": "Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS)",
"body": "## What it is\nA severe form of acute lung injury: sudden, progressive pulmonary edema with bilateral infiltrates on chest X-ray and hypoxemia that does not improve with oxygen, without raised left atrial pressure. Lung compliance falls.\n\n## Findings\n- Develops over about 4 to 48 hours.\n- Severe dyspnea, labored fast breathing, severe hypoxemia unresponsive to oxygen.\n- Crackles and rhonchi, increased alveolar dead space.\n- X-ray looks like cardiogenic pulmonary edema.\n\n## Diagnostics\nHistory and presentation, ABG showing hypoxemia despite more oxygen, bilateral infiltrates, BNP and echocardiogram (to exclude heart failure), pulmonary artery catheter.\n\n## Treatment\n- Treat the cause; optimise oxygenation.\n- Intubation and mechanical ventilation with PEEP, sedation, sometimes paralytics, antibiotics as indicated.\n- Supportive: surfactant replacement and other drugs mentioned in the handout.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. ICU monitoring, frequent reassessment.\n2. Turn the client often to improve ventilation, perfusion and drainage.\n3. Promote rest to cut oxygen demand.\n4. Nutrition: 35 to 45 kcal/kg/day (handout).\n5. Reassure; look for anxiety linked to ventilator problems.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Hypoxemia not corrected by oxygen with bilateral white-out. See also [[Respiratory Failure Recognition]].\n\n## Related topics\n[[Pneumonia]], [[Sepsis and Septic Shock Recognition]], [[Pulmonary Embolism]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Surfactant and \"pulmonary antihypertensive\" drugs are not standard in all references; verify.",

"area": "NP3",
"title": "Pulmonary Embolism",
"body": "## What it is\nBlockage of the pulmonary artery or a branch by a clot, usually from a deep vein (see [[Deep Vein Thrombosis]]). Risks: trauma, surgery (orthopedic, abdominal, pelvic), pregnancy, heart failure, age over 50, clotting disorders, immobility.\n\n## Findings\n- Dyspnea (most common symptom) and tachypnea (most frequent sign).\n- Sudden pleuritic chest pain that can mimic angina or MI.\n- Anxiety, tachycardia, fever, cough, sweating, hemoptysis, syncope, petechiae over the chest.\n\n## Diagnostics\n- Pulmonary angiography (best method per the handout), V/Q scan, spiral CT.\n- ABG: low oxygen and low CO2. ECG: sinus tachycardia, nonspecific T changes. Chest X-ray: infiltrates, atelectasis, raised diaphragm.\n\n## Treatment\n- Oxygen immediately, IV access, dobutamine or dopamine for hypotension, small doses of IV morphine for anxiety and chest pain.\n- Anticoagulants (heparin, warfarin); thrombolytics (urokinase, streptokinase, alteplase) in severe cases; embolectomy for massive PE.\n\n## Priority nursing actions (ABC)\n1. Oxygen, raise the head of the bed, call the provider.\n2. Monitor hypoxia, bleeding from anticoagulants, and signs of right ventricular failure or cardiogenic shock.\n3. Prevent recurrence: early ambulation, leg exercises, avoid crossing legs, constrictive clothing and long sitting or bed rest.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Sudden dyspnea and chest pain after surgery or long immobility. Compare with [[Myocardial Infarction: Presentation and Cardiac Markers]] and [[Angina Pectoris: Types, Triggers and Findings]].\n\n## Related topics\n[[Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS)]], [[Respiratory Failure Recognition]], [[Cardiac Arrest and Basic Life Support Principles]]",

"id": "np3-cr-x-nitroglycerin",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Nitroglycerin: Forms, Action and Safe Use",
"body": "## What it is\nNitroglycerin is the classic nitrate for angina. It dilates the veins, so blood pools in the body's veins and less blood returns to the heart. That lowers preload and the heart's workload and oxygen demand. It also widens coronary arteries and lowers afterload to some degree.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Forms named in the handout: sublingual tablet, spray, topical (ointment), and intravenous.\n- Brand names listed: Nitrostat, Nitro-Bid.\n- In angina, the aim is short-term and long-term cutting of myocardial oxygen use by vasodilation.\n- In heart failure, nitroglycerin ointment or nitroprusside is used as a vasodilator to reduce preload and ease dyspnea.\n- In MI, nitroglycerin is part of the standard drug set (the MONA idea: morphine, oxygen, nitroglycerin, aspirin).\n\n## Assessment\n- Before each dose: chest pain description, blood pressure and heart rate.\n- After: is the pain gone? Is the client dizzy or hypotensive?\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Stop activity and have the client sit or rest: rest alone reduces oxygen demand and sitting guards against a fall if pressure drops (safety).\n2. Check blood pressure before giving it; vasodilation can drop pressure, so do not give if the pressure is dangerously low (follow the order).\n3. Give the form ordered. For a tablet under the tongue, let it dissolve; do not swallow it.\n4. Reassess pain and pressure. Pain that is not relieved, or is lasting or worsening, is treated as possible MI: call for help, get an ECG (the exam wants action, not waiting).\n\n## Complications and red flags\n- Hypotension, dizziness, headache and flushing are common effects of vasodilation.\n- Pain not relieved by rest and nitroglycerin suggests unstable angina or MI.\n\n## Client teaching\n- Keep the medicine with you; use it at the first sign of an attack as prescribed.\n- Rise slowly, because pressure may fall.\n- Report chest pain that is not relieved, so the care team can act.\n- Do not stop the regular medicines or change the schedule without the prescriber.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Why does nitroglycerin help angina? Fewer veins returning blood = less preload = less oxygen demand.\n- Stable angina eases with rest or nitroglycerin; pain that does not ease raises the alarm.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Angina Treatment: Procedures and Drugs]], [[Angina Pectoris: Types, Triggers and Findings]], [[Myocardial Infarction: Management and Drug Therapy]], [[Drugs Used in Heart Failure]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Exact number of doses, spacing and storage rules, and interactions (for example with erectile-dysfunction drugs) are not in the handout. Confirm them in your drug book.",

"id": "np3-cr-x-antiplatelet-anticoag",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Antiplatelet and Anticoagulant Drugs in Cardiac Care",
"body": "## What it is\nTwo different jobs. Antiplatelets stop platelets sticking together (arterial clots, as in angina and MI). Anticoagulants slow the clotting cascade so a thrombus does not form or grow (as in venous clots, atrial thrombus, PE). Thrombolytics (clot busters) are a third group that dissolves a clot that already exists.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Antiplatelets in the handout: aspirin; clopidogrel (Plavix); glycoprotein agents abciximab (ReoPro), tirofiban (Aggrastat), eptifibatide (Integrilin). Prasugrel is also named under the \"thienopyridines\".\n- Anticoagulants in the handout: heparin (unfractionated); low-molecular-weight heparins enoxaparin (Lovenox) and dalteparin (Fragmin); warfarin (Coumadin) by mouth.\n- Thrombolytics in the handout: alteplase (Activase, t-PA), urokinase, streptokinase.\n- Monitoring in the handout: aPTT, prothrombin time (PT) and INR for the anticoagulated client.\n- Mitral stenosis: anticoagulants may be used to lower the risk of atrial thrombus.\n- DVT: subcutaneous heparin or LMWH is used in prevention for surgical clients; warfarin is the oral drug.\n\n## Assessment\n- Bleeding signs: bruising, gum or nose bleeding, blood in urine, stool or vomit, falling BP with rising pulse.\n- Lab trends: aPTT, PT/INR as ordered; platelet count.\n- Neurologic checks if headache or confusion appears (possible bleeding).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Check the right lab before each dose, because dose depends on the result (safety).\n2. Watch for bleeding at every contact; report promptly.\n3. Use soft techniques: soft toothbrush, electric razor, gentle handling, pressure on puncture sites.\n4. Give subcutaneous injections as ordered without rubbing the site (general practice; check your fundamentals book).\n5. Keep the antidote and emergency measures known to the unit.\n\n## Treatment and medications\n- Antiplatelet aim: prevent platelet aggregation, so artery stents and plaques do not clot.\n- Heparin/LMWH: prevent thrombus formation or extension of an existing one.\n- Warfarin: long-term oral control; the dose is guided by blood tests.\n\n## Complications and red flags\n- Bleeding is the main danger of every drug in this group. Sudden severe headache, black stools, or a falling BP means act at once.\n\n## Client teaching\n- Report bleeding or black stools; avoid unnecessary falls and injuries.\n- Take at the same time daily; do not skip or double.\n- Keep appointments for blood tests.\n- Tell every provider and dentist what you take.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Which lab monitors warfarin (PT/INR) versus heparin (aPTT)? Which group works on arterial clots (antiplatelets) versus venous clots (anticoagulants)?\n\n## Related topics\n[[Anticoagulants: Heparin and Warfarin]], [[Thrombolytic Drugs]], [[Deep Vein Thrombosis]], [[Myocardial Infarction: Management and Drug Therapy]], [[Angina Treatment: Procedures and Drugs]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout does not say which lab goes with which drug; the pairing of aPTT with heparin and PT/INR with warfarin is standard teaching: verify. Antidotes, diet rules for warfarin and dosing are not in the handout.",

"area": "NP3",
"title": "Chest Pain Compared: Angina, MI, Pericarditis, Pulmonary Embolism, Pleurisy",
"body": "## What it is\nMany conditions hurt in the chest. The exam gives you a short description and expects you to sort it by what makes the pain better or worse, how long it lasts, and what else is present.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Stable angina: pressure or heaviness behind the sternum, brought on by exertion, cold, a heavy meal or stress; eased by rest or nitroglycerin; short.\n- Unstable angina: more frequent, more severe, may not ease with rest or nitroglycerin.\n- Prinzmetal (variant) angina: pain at rest, ST elevation that reverses, thought to be coronary spasm.\n- Myocardial infarction: pain usually longer than 30 minutes, spreads to the left arm, back or jaw, often starts without a cause (often morning), relieved only by opioids; with nausea, sweating, fear, shortness of breath.\n- Pericarditis: pain under the clavicle, in the neck or left scapula; worse on deep inspiration, eased by sitting and leaning forward; friction rub.\n- Pleurisy: sharp, knife-like pain on inspiration, cough or sneeze; limited to one area; pleural friction rub; eased by lying on the painful side.\n- Pulmonary embolism: sudden pleuritic pain, may mimic angina or MI; dyspnea and tachypnea are the most frequent signs.\n- Pneumonia: pleuritic pain with fever and productive cough.\n- Pneumothorax: sudden chest discomfort with dyspnea, hyperresonance and diminished breath sounds.\n\n## Assessment\n- PQRST of pain: provoking factors, quality, radiation, severity, time.\n- Vital signs, oxygen saturation, breath sounds, heart sounds (rub, S3, S4, muffled).\n- ECG for any chest pain with a cardiac look.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Airway, breathing, circulation: oxygen, monitor, vital signs (ABC).\n2. Get an ECG and notify the provider for chest pain that may be cardiac: the exam rewards early action over long assessment when MI is possible.\n3. Give ordered drugs (nitroglycerin, aspirin, opioid) in the order given by the protocol.\n4. Keep the client at rest and calm: anxiety raises oxygen demand.\n\n## Complications and red flags\n- Pain with falling BP, sweating and dysrhythmia suggests MI or shock.\n- Pain with muffled heart sounds, distended neck veins and low BP suggests tamponade.\n- Sudden dyspnea with tracheal shift suggests tension pneumothorax.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Matching clue to disease: leaning forward eases = pericarditis; pain worse with breathing and rub = pleurisy; not relieved by rest or nitroglycerin and lasts over 30 minutes = MI.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause, finding, action: if you cannot rule out the heart, treat it as the heart. Oxygen and ECG come before comfort measures.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Angina Pectoris: Types, Triggers and Findings]], [[Myocardial Infarction: Presentation and Cardiac Markers]], [[Pericarditis and Pericardiocentesis]], [[Pleurisy (Pleuritis)]], [[Pulmonary Embolism]]",

"id": "np3-cr-x-ecg-changes",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "ECG Changes Linked to Cardiac and Electrolyte Disorders",
"body": "## What it is\nA collection of the ECG clues the handout ties to specific disorders. Know the normal values first, then the changes.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- P wave: atrial depolarization; no wider than 0.12 second.\n- PR interval: 0.12 to 0.20 second (conduction through atria, AV junction, Purkinje system).\n- QRS: ventricular depolarization; 0.04 to 0.10 second.\n- T wave: ventricular repolarization; over 5 mm is not normal.\n- QT interval: usually 0.32 to 0.40; prolonged QT raises risk of torsades de pointes, a lethal ventricular dysrhythmia.\n- U wave: usually unseen; may appear with hypokalemia.\n- Disease-linked changes:\n  - Angina (ischemia): T-wave inversion, ST depression.\n  - Prinzmetal angina: reversible ST elevation at rest.\n  - MI: T-wave changes, ST elevation, abnormal Q wave.\n  - Myocarditis: low-voltage QRS, ST elevation or heart block.\n  - Pulmonary embolism: sinus tachycardia, PR-interval depression, non-specific T-wave changes.\n  - Restrictive cardiomyopathy: heart block can occur.\n  - Mitral stenosis: atrial dysrhythmias.\n- Rate: PP interval gives atrial rate; RR interval gives ventricular rate.\n\n## Assessment\n- Read rate, rhythm, then intervals. Any new change in a client with chest pain is reported.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Treat the client, not the monitor: check pulse, BP, color and symptoms first (assess before intervening).\n2. For a dangerous rhythm or new ST change, call for help and keep emergency equipment ready.\n3. Check serum potassium and other electrolytes when the pattern suggests it, per orders.\n\n## Complications and red flags\n- Prolonged QT or runs of wide complexes, new Q waves, heart block.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Match change to condition: U wave = low potassium; ST elevation = MI or variant angina; T inversion and ST depression = ischemia.\n\n## How to reason about it\nST depression = ischemia that is reversible; ST elevation with abnormal Q = tissue damage or spasm. The exam usually wants you to act on the client first.\n\n## Related topics\n[[ECG Waves and Intervals]], [[Angina Pectoris: Types, Triggers and Findings]], [[Myocardial Infarction: Presentation and Cardiac Markers]], [[Myocarditis]], [[Pulmonary Embolism]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout states the T wave limit as \"not normal more than 5 mm\" and the AV node delay as \"0.8 milliseconds\"; both look garbled. Confirm normal values in your textbook.",

"id": "np3-cr-x-valve-murmurs-compared",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Valve Lesions and Murmurs Compared",
"body": "## What it is\nFour valve problems share a family of exam questions. Learn each by three things: what part of the heart cycle is affected, the sound, and the main symptom.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Mitral stenosis: narrowed mitral valve; usually rheumatic. Low-pitched rumbling diastolic murmur at the apex. Blood backs up into the left atrium then lungs: dyspnea on exertion, orthopnea, paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea, hemoptysis, atrial dysrhythmias; right heart failure late.\n- Mitral regurgitation: leaky mitral valve; blood flows back into left atrium in systole. High-pitched blowing systolic murmur at the apex. Common causes: mitral valve prolapse and rheumatic heart disease. Fatigue, weakness, palpitations, dyspnea, cough.\n- Mitral valve prolapse: leaflets bulge into the left atrium in systole.\n- Aortic stenosis: narrowed aortic valve; loud rough systolic murmur over the aortic area; the left ventricle thickens. Classic triad: exertional dyspnea, angina, syncope or dizziness. BP is normal.\n- Aortic regurgitation: blood flows back into the left ventricle in diastole. High-pitched blowing diastolic murmur at the left sternal border, 3rd to 4th intercostal space. Widened pulse pressure, water-hammer (Corrigan's) pulse, visible pulsations in neck and head.\n\n## Assessment\n- Auscultate at the correct site and phase; note pulse pressure, rhythm, weight, edema, lung sounds.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Breathing first: high Fowler's position for dyspnea (mitral stenosis).\n2. Watch for pulmonary edema, thrombus and dysrhythmias; check ABG as ordered.\n3. Daily weight for fluid balance; low-sodium diet teaching.\n4. Reduce exertion in aortic regurgitation; vasodilators and ACE inhibitors may be ordered.\n\n## Treatment and medications\n- Mitral stenosis: anticoagulants against atrial clot; valvuloplasty or valve replacement.\n- Mitral regurgitation: afterload reduction with ACE inhibitors; valve repair or replacement.\n- Aortic regurgitation: valvuloplasty or replacement, ideally before left ventricular failure; vasodilators.\n- Aortic stenosis: valve replacement or percutaneous valvuloplasty.\n\n## Complications and red flags\n- Pulmonary edema, heart failure, atrial clots, syncope, infective endocarditis risk.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- \"Rumbling diastolic at the apex\" = mitral stenosis. \"Blowing systolic at the apex\" = mitral regurgitation. \"Rough systolic at the aortic area with syncope\" = aortic stenosis. \"Water-hammer pulse, wide pulse pressure\" = aortic regurgitation.\n\n## Memory aid\nStenosis = a stiff, narrow door: sound occurs when blood must pass through (mitral stenosis in diastole when the ventricle fills; aortic stenosis in systole when it empties). Regurgitation = leak: sound when the valve should be closed.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Valvular Heart Disease: Overview and Mitral Stenosis]], [[Mitral Regurgitation and Mitral Valve Prolapse]], [[Aortic Stenosis]], [[Aortic Regurgitation]], [[Heart Sounds S1 to S4]]",

"area": "NP3",
"title": "Acute Pulmonary Edema",
"body": "## What it is\nFluid floods the lung tissue and alveoli, usually because the left heart cannot move blood forward. Pressure backs up into the pulmonary veins and fluid is forced out of the capillaries. Gas exchange fails.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Main cause in the handout: left-sided heart failure. Also a complication of mitral stenosis, aortic stenosis, and of surgery on a stenosed mitral valve.\n- ARDS also has pulmonary edema but is a lung injury problem with normal left atrial pressure.\n- Hallmark sign: frothy, pink (blood-tinged) sputum.\n- Other findings: severe dyspnea, orthopnea, cough, crackles, low oxygen saturation, anxiety, S3 gallop.\n\n## Assessment\n- Rate and effort of breathing, oxygen saturation, breath sounds, sputum, BP and pulse, mental state.\n- Daily weight, intake and output, edema.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Sit the client upright (high Fowler's or semi-Fowler's), legs dependent if ordered: gravity pools blood in the legs and eases breathing (airway and breathing first).\n2. Give oxygen as ordered to treat hypoxemia and reduce heart workload.\n3. Give diuretics as ordered to pull fluid off; watch potassium.\n4. Give other ordered vasodilators and cardiac drugs; opioid for anxiety and chest tightness per order.\n5. Stay with the client; fear worsens workload.\n6. Record intake and output and monitor response.\n\n## Treatment and medications\n- Diuretics (furosemide and others), vasodilators (nitroglycerin, nitroprusside), inotropes (digoxin, dobutamine), ACE inhibitors and beta-blockers for long-term HF control.\n\n## Complications and red flags\n- Respiratory failure, shock, dysrhythmias, pneumonia.\n\n## Client teaching\n- Low-sodium diet; weigh daily and report quick gain; take heart failure drugs as prescribed; sleep with head raised.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Pink frothy sputum plus crackles in HF = pulmonary edema. First action: upright position and oxygen, then drugs.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause (weak left ventricle) -> finding (fluid in lungs, froth) -> action (sit up, oxygen, diuretic). Trap: lying the client flat for comfort or for an exam.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Left-Sided versus Right-Sided Heart Failure]], [[Drugs Used in Heart Failure]], [[Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS)]], [[Diuretics: Thiazide, Loop, Osmotic and Potassium-Sparing]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Dependent leg positioning and the opioid role are standard teaching rather than handout text: verify.",

"body": "## What it is\nThe most frequent viral infection in the general population. The handout names coronavirus as the cause. The virus spreads easily because it is shed for about 2 days before symptoms and in the early symptomatic phase, so people spread it without knowing.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Symptoms: low-grade fever, nasal congestion, rhinorrhea, sneezing, watery eyes, scratchy sore throat, bad breath, malaise, chills, headache, muscle aches.\n- No cure: care is symptomatic.\n- Allergic rhinitis differs: itching of nose, palate, throat, eyes and ears with sneezing, but no fever, and it is tied to pollen season (seasonal) or all year (perennial).\n\n## Assessment\n- Temperature, nasal discharge, throat, lung sounds, hydration.\n- Look for signs that it has gone beyond a cold: high fever, chest pain, ear pain, difficulty swallowing.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. "body": "## What it is\nA quick map from lung disease to the test the handout names.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Spirometry: measures airflow obstruction in COPD.\n- ABG: COPD (low PaO2 and pH, high CO2); ARDS (hypoxemia despite more oxygen); PE (hypoxemia and low CO2).\n- Chest X-ray: COPD late (hyperinflation, flat diaphragm, bullae); pneumonia (consolidation); TB (presence and extent); pleurisy; PE (infiltrates, atelectasis, raised diaphragm on affected side); ARDS (bilateral infiltrates).\n- CT scan: bronchiectasis (dilated bronchi); empyema; pleural effusion; laryngeal cancer work-up.\n- Lateral decubitus X-ray: pleural effusion.\n- Alpha-1-antitrypsin assay: inherited emphysema.\n- Sputum Gram stain and culture/sensitivity: pneumonia and bronchiectasis; sputum smear/culture confirms TB.\n- Tuberculin skin test (PPD, Mantoux): TB infection.\n- Blood culture: bacteremia with pneumonia.\n- V/Q scan, pulmonary angiography (best method), spiral CT: pulmonary embolism.\n- Thoracentesis, pleural biopsy: pleural disease.\n- Direct laryngoscopy, virtual endoscopy, optical imaging, CT/MRI: laryngeal cancer.\n- BNP, echocardiogram, pulmonary artery catheter: help separate ARDS from cardiac causes.\n\n## Assessment\n- Check the order, consent, allergies and how the client must prepare.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. "body": "## What it is\nSix conditions put something abnormal in or around the pleural space. The content decides the name.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Pneumothorax: air. Hyperresonance, diminished breath sounds, reduced chest movement; tension type shifts the trachea away.\n- Pleural effusion: fluid (transudate or exudate), from heart failure, infection or nephrotic syndrome. Dyspnea, cannot lie flat, cough, pleuritic pain.\n- Hemothorax: blood, from trauma. Possible shock, hidden blood loss.\n- Empyema: pus, from pneumonia, abscess or chest trauma. Acutely ill client.\n- Pleurisy: inflamed pleural layers rubbing; friction rub, sharp pain.\n- Drainage rules: thoracentesis for effusion; chest tube plus water seal for pneumothorax and hemothorax; tube thoracostomy for loculated effusion or empyema; open thoracotomy drainage for thick pus.\n\n## Assessment\n- Breath sounds, percussion, tracheal position, pain pattern, chest X-ray.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Splint the chest for coughing and give pain relief as ordered.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Air = pneumothorax; fluid = effusion; blood = hemothorax; pus = empyema. "title": "Heart Failure: Nursing Diagnoses and Care Priorities",
"body": "## What it is\nA practical care plan for the client with heart failure (HF), built from the handout's nursing diagnoses and interventions.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Handout nursing diagnoses: impaired gas exchange (ventilation-perfusion imbalance); decreased cardiac output (altered contractility, preload, afterload); activity intolerance (oxygen supply below demand); risk for pulmonary edema, pneumonia and dysrhythmias.\n- Stages (ACC/AHA): A = at risk, no disease or symptoms; B = structural disease or left ventricular dysfunction, no symptoms; C = structural disease with current or past symptoms; D = refractory end-stage needing special care.\n- Compensation: sympathetic stimulation (faster rate, vasoconstriction, more afterload) and renin-angiotensin activation (angiotensin II vasoconstriction; aldosterone causes sodium and water retention) lead to fluid overload.\n- Left HF = lung signs (dyspnea, crackles, orthopnea, pink frothy sputum, S3). Right HF = body signs (neck vein distention, edema, hepatomegaly, ascites, weight gain).\n\n## Assessment\n- Breath sounds, rate, oxygen saturation, heart sounds, JVD, edema, liver size, abdominal girth, daily weight, intake and output.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Support breathing: semi-Fowler's or sitting up, oxygen as ordered (airway and breathing first).\n2. Monitor vital signs, rhythm with a cardiac monitor, and changes.\n3. Weigh daily at the same time; record intake and output (fluid overload check).\n4. Give diuretics, ACE inhibitors, beta-blockers, digoxin and vasodilators as prescribed; monitor potassium with diuretics.\n5. Avoid unnecessary IV fluids.\n6. Space activity with rest to match oxygen supply.\n7. Assess dependent edema, including sacrum and thighs in bed-bound clients.\n\n## Treatment and medications\n- Diuretics reduce volume; ACE inhibitors lower afterload; beta-blockers raise ejection fraction and reduce ventricle size; digoxin strengthens contraction; vasodilators lower preload.\n\n## Complications and red flags\n- Pulmonary edema, dysrhythmias, worsening weight gain, drop in urine output.\n\n## Client teaching\n- Low-sodium diet; daily weight; report weight gain and worsening breathlessness; pace activity; sleep propped up.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Which finding needs action first: rising dyspnea and crackles. Which teaching is best: daily weights and sodium limits.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Heart Failure: Types, Causes and Compensation]], [[Left-Sided versus Right-Sided Heart Failure]], [[Drugs Used in Heart Failure]], [[Acute Pulmonary Edema]]",

"area": "NP3",
"title": "COPD Types Compared: Blue Bloater and Pink Puffer",
"body": "## What it is\nCOPD is airflow limitation that is not fully reversible and usually worsens over time. It has two classic forms that appear in many exam questions.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Chronic bronchitis (\"blue bloater\"): productive cough for at least 3 months a year for 2 years in a row; thick gelatinous sputum; wheezing and dyspnea later.\n- Emphysema (\"pink puffer\"): destruction of alveolar walls; dyspnea, low exercise tolerance, minimal cough, barrel chest (raised anteroposterior diameter, flat diaphragm), weight loss.\n- Panlobular emphysema: all air spaces enlarge; little inflammation; hyperinflated chest.\n- Centrilobular emphysema: damage in the center of the lobule; chronic hypoxemia, hypercapnia, polycythemia, right-sided heart failure, central cyanosis, peripheral edema.\n- Alpha-1-antitrypsin deficiency: inherited cause of emphysema.\n- Tests: spirometry, ABG (low PaO2 and pH, high CO2), chest X-ray (hyperinflation, bullae).\n- Treatment: smoking cessation, bronchodilators, corticosteroids, antibiotics and mucolytics; surgery includes bullectomy and lung volume reduction.\n\n## Assessment\n- Respiratory rate and pattern, breath sounds, oxygen saturation, weight, edema, sputum color.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Oxygen at low flow (1 to 2 L/min) as ordered: in COPD the drive to breathe can depend on low oxygen, so high flow risks respiratory depression (airway and breathing first, but safe dose).\n2. Bronchodilators as prescribed; hydration to thin secretions.\n3. Teach pursed-lip and diaphragmatic breathing; chest physiotherapy as ordered.\n4. Small frequent meals and supplements to counter weight loss.\n5. Alternate activity with rest; pulmonary rehabilitation.\n\n## Complications and red flags\n- Respiratory failure, right heart failure, recurrent infection; increased confusion or sleepiness signals rising CO2.\n\n## Client teaching\n- Stop smoking, avoid bronchial irritants, plan rests, recognize early infection.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Barrel chest and thin = pink puffer (emphysema). Cyanotic, productive cough, edema = blue bloater (bronchitis). Oxygen flow for COPD: low.\n\n## Related topics\n[[COPD: Chronic Bronchitis and Emphysema]], [[COPD Management and Nursing Care]], [[Respiratory Acidosis]], [[Oxygen Therapy: Safety and Delivery Devices]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout places centrilobular and panlobular features loosely; confirm which type causes which signs. "title": "Peripheral Vascular Disorders Compared: Buerger, Raynaud, DVT, Aneurysm",
"body": "## What it is\nFour vessel problems that students confuse. Sort them by who gets them, what the color or pain does, and what is the main teaching.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Buerger's disease: men 20 to 35, heavy smoking or chewing tobacco; small and medium arteries and veins inflamed. Foot cramps in the arch after exercise relieved by rest, rubor of the foot, absent pedal pulse. Stop all nicotine.\n- Raynaud's phenomenon: women 16 to 40, cold climates; episodic color change white, blue, red; bilateral, symmetric. Avoid cold and nicotine; calcium channel blockers.\n- Deep vein thrombosis: Virchow's triad (vessel injury, stasis, altered clotting). Swelling, heaviness, limb pain. Anticoagulants, compression stockings, elevate, walk.\n- Aneurysm: localized dilation at a weak arterial wall. True, false, fusiform, saccular, dissecting. Control BP; surgery or endovascular graft; watch for rupture.\n\n## Assessment\n- Color, temperature, pulses, edema, pain pattern, skin integrity in both limbs.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Protect circulation: keep warm, avoid tight clothing and crossing legs.\n2. For DVT, do not massage the leg (risk of embolism: standard teaching) and report chest pain or dyspnea at once (possible PE).\n3. For suspected aneurysm rupture, treat sudden severe pain or falling BP as an emergency.\n4. Teach stopping nicotine for Buerger's and Raynaud's.\n\n## Complications and red flags\n- Gangrene (Buerger's), pulmonary embolism (DVT), rupture (aneurysm).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- White-blue-red = Raynaud's. Young male smoker with absent pedal pulse = Buerger's. Unilateral leg swelling = DVT. Pulsating mass or sudden tearing pain = aneurysm.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Buerger's Disease (Thromboangiitis Obliterans)]], [[Raynaud's Phenomenon]], [[Deep Vein Thrombosis]], [[Aneurysms]], [[Pulmonary Embolism]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The no-massage rule is standard teaching, not in the handout.",

"area": "NP3",
"title": "Blood Components and Normal Hematologic Values",
"body": "## What it is\nThe hematologic system is the blood plus the places that make it: bone marrow and the reticuloendothelial system (RES). Blood is a fluid \"organ\" made of plasma and cells.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Plasma is the fluid part, about 55% of blood volume. It carries albumin, globulin, fibrinogen, clotting factors, electrolytes, waste and nutrients.\n- Serum = plasma without the clotting factors (that is why it does not clot).\n- WBC (leukocytes), normal 4,500-11,000/mm3: they fight infection.\n  - Neutrophil: first line against bacteria by phagocytosis.\n  - Eosinophil: allergic reactions, neutralizes histamine, digests foreign proteins.\n  - Monocyte: becomes a macrophage in tissue; good against fungus; immune surveillance.\n  - T lymphocyte: cell-mediated immunity. Plasma cell (mature B cell): makes antibodies (immunoglobulins).\n- RBC (erythrocyte): carries hemoglobin to deliver oxygen; lifespan about 120 days.\n  - Hemoglobin: male 13-18 g/dL, female 12-16 g/dL.\n  - Hematocrit: male 42-52%, female 35-47%.\n- Platelet (thrombocyte): a fragment of a megakaryocyte; needed for clotting and hemostasis; lifespan about 10 days; normal 150,000-450,000/mm3.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nExpect \"which cell is responsible for...\" and \"which value is abnormal\". Low counts of one cell line tell you the problem: low RBC = anemia and hypoxia signs; low WBC = infection risk; low platelets = bleeding risk. Women have lower Hgb/Hct than men.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Iron Deficiency Anemia]], [[Aplastic Anemia]], [[Polycythemia Vera]], [[Chemotherapy Side Effects: Bone Marrow Suppression]]",

"lab values"

"id": "np3-ho-iron-deficiency-anemia",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Iron Deficiency Anemia",
"body": "## What it is\nAnemia that develops when dietary iron is too little to make hemoglobin, so the blood carries less oxygen to tissues.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Causes: chronic blood loss (trauma, heavy menstruation, GI bleeding: hematemesis, melena, hematochezia), poor iron intake, and poor absorption (chronic diarrhea, malabsorption syndrome, gastrectomy, celiac disease).\n- Easy fatigability is the hallmark sign. Think \"oxygen-starved tissues\".\n- Tests: RBC, hemoglobin, hematocrit, reticulocyte count, serum iron, ferritin.\n\n## Assessment\n- General: pallor, fatigue, palpitations, cold sensitivity, dizziness and dyspnea (cerebral hypoxia).\n- Hair and nails: brittle hair, spoon-shaped nails (koilonychia) from atrophy of epidermal cells.\n- Mouth: smooth sore tongue, angular cheilosis (cracks at mouth corners).\n- Plummer-Vinson syndrome: atrophic glossitis, stomatitis and dysphagia from wasting of the tongue, mouth and throat lining.\n- Pica (craving non-food items such as ice or clay).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Find and correct the bleeding source and watch for any bleeding. Why: replacing iron fails if the blood keeps leaving.\n2. Bed rest during severe symptoms to cut oxygen demand.\n3. Give iron as ordered (ferrous sulfate, gluconate, fumarate); parenteral iron when oral iron fails, using the Z-track method (see the linked note).\n4. Teach iron-rich foods: green leafy vegetables, liver and organ meats, egg yolk, legumes, raisins, red meat, molasses.\n\n## Treatment and medications\nCorrect blood loss; oral or parenteral iron. Take oral iron with vitamin C for better absorption; avoid antacids and dairy at the same time; best on an empty stomach between meals (with food if it upsets the stomach).\n\n## Complications and red flags\nSevere fatigue, chest pain or fast heartbeat on effort, black tarry stools with weakness (active GI bleeding, not just iron). Expected side effects: anorexia, nausea and vomiting, abdominal pain, constipation or diarrhea, dark stools.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nSpoon nails, craving for ice, sore smooth tongue = iron deficiency. A teaching question: \"Which statement shows understanding?\" Correct: take with orange juice, not with milk or antacids; stools may turn dark.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause (blood loss or poor intake) -> finding (fatigue, pallor, koilonychia) -> action (stop bleeding, give iron, teach). The trap: calling dark stools from iron an emergency, or giving iron without looking for the source of loss.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Parenteral Iron Therapy and the Z-Track Technique]], [[Anemias Compared: Cause, Key Sign and First Priority]], [[Pernicious Anemia (Vitamin B12 Deficiency)]], [[Blood Components and Normal Hematologic Values]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nDoses and iron lab ranges are not in the handout. The cause of pica is described by the handout as neuronal degeneration; confirm in your text.",

"area": "NP3",
"title": "Pernicious Anemia (Vitamin B12 Deficiency)",
"body": "## What it is\nA megaloblastic anemia caused by lack of intrinsic factor, so vitamin B12 cannot be absorbed. The handout calls it the most dangerous of the chronic anemias.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Risks: unknown cause, subtotal gastrectomy, heredity, ileal inflammation (Crohn's disease), no intrinsic factor, strict vegetarian diet.\n- Because the problem is absorption, B12 is given by injection, not by mouth.\n\n## Assessment findings\n- General anemia signs: headache, dizziness, dyspnea, palpitations, extreme pallor, cold sensitivity.\n- GI: sore mouth, anorexia, nausea/vomiting, weight loss, epigastric discomfort, diarrhea or constipation.\n- Red, beefy tongue (glossitis) is the pathognomonic sign.\n- Neurologic: paresthesia of the extremities, poor balance, loss of position sense. Neurologic signs separate B12 deficiency from folate deficiency.\n- Tests: CBC and smear (low Hgb/Hct). Schilling test: oral radioactive B12 then 24-hour urine; positive (deficiency) means B12 is absent in urine.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Safety first: bed rest and fall precautions because of weakness and impaired balance.\n2. Give vitamin B12 injections, monthly for life.\n3. Diet: small frequent bland soft meals; more carbohydrate, protein, iron and vitamin C.\n4. Mouth care: soft toothbrush, avoid irritating mouthwash; avoid very hot or cold temperatures (sensory loss).\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The handout lists Z-track parenteral iron under this topic; that belongs to iron deficiency anemia, not B12 deficiency. See [[Iron Deficiency Anemia]].\n- The Schilling test is rarely used today; many textbooks now stress B12 level and antibody tests. Check what your review book expects.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Folic Acid Deficiency Anemia]]",

"area": "NP3",
"title": "Aplastic Anemia",
"body": "## What it is\nRare disease in which marrow stem cells are damaged or lost and the marrow is replaced by fat. The result is pancytopenia: low RBCs, WBCs and platelets.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Causes: benzene and pesticides, radiation, immune injury (T cells attack marrow), marrow-depressing drugs (chloramphenicol, sulfonamides such as Bactrim, methotrexate, nitrogen mustard, vincristine), infections and pregnancy.\n- Diagnosis: bone marrow aspiration shows a hypoplastic or aplastic, fatty marrow; CBC and smear show pancytopenia.\n\n## Assessment findings (think by cell line)\n- Low RBC: pallor, weakness, fatigue, exertional dyspnea, palpitations.\n- Low WBC: fever, malaise, infections, wound exudate, enlarged nodes and spleen.\n- Low platelets: bleeding gums, nose, GI/GU; purpura, petechiae, ecchymoses, retinal hemorrhage, oozing at venipuncture sites.\n\n## Treatment\nRemove the cause; bone marrow or peripheral blood stem cell transplant; immunosuppressive therapy; supportive platelet and RBC transfusions, antibiotics, antifungals.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Protect from infection (the most life-threatening risk): reverse isolation, monitor for infection, teach infection prevention.\n2. Prevent bleeding: avoid SQ and IM injections, soft toothbrush, electric razor.\n3. Bed rest and oxygen to lower oxygen demand; give transfusions and immunosuppressants as ordered.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Chemotherapy Side Effects: Bone Marrow Suppression]], [[Blood Components and Normal Hematologic Values]]",

"title": "Sickle Cell Anemia and Crises",
"body": "## What it is\nA severe hemolytic anemia from inheriting the sickle hemoglobin gene. Abnormal hemoglobin S forms crystals and bends red cells into sickle shapes when oxygen is low, blocking small vessels.\n\n## Assessment findings\n- Severe pain in many body parts, tachycardia, murmurs, cardiomegaly, chest pain, dyspnea, jaundice.\n- Enlarged skull and facial bones from marrow expansion.\n\n## Complications\nHypoxia, ischemia, infection, poor wound healing (leg ulcers), impotence, stroke, renal failure, heart failure, pulmonary hypertension.\n\n## Types of crisis\n- Sickle (vaso-occlusive) crisis: most common and most painful; tissue hypoxia and necrosis from blocked flow.\n- Aplastic crisis: marrow shuts down after human parvovirus infection.\n- Sequestration crisis: sickled cells pool in organs.\n\n## Treatment\nBone marrow or peripheral blood stem cell transplant, hydroxyurea, long-term RBC transfusion, splenectomy.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Treat the triggers of sickling: oxygen for hypoxia, IV and oral fluids for dehydration, antibiotics for infection.\n2. Manage pain: analgesics as ordered, support and elevate inflamed joints, relaxation techniques.\n3. Prevent complications: keep well hydrated, avoid cold (vasoconstriction), protect legs from trauma, use aseptic technique.\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout says hydroxyurea \"increases production\"; textbooks say it raises fetal hemoglobin, which reduces sickling. Confirm.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Thalassemia]], [[Blood Components and Normal Hematologic Values]]",

"title": "Thalassemia",
"body": "## What it is\nA group of inherited anemias with defective hemoglobin chain synthesis. Red cells are pale (hypochromic) and very small (microcytic), and are destroyed early (hemolysis).\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Alpha-thalassemia: mainly Asia and Middle East; milder, often symptom-free, very small cells with mild or no anemia.\n- Beta-thalassemia: mainly Mediterranean; mild forms cause microcytosis and mild anemia.\n- Thalassemia major (Cooley's anemia): severe beta form; severe anemia, marked hemolysis, ineffective red cell production; can be fatal in the first years of life if untreated.\n\n## Treatment\n- Bone marrow transplant; regular packed RBC transfusions.\n- With early regular transfusions, growth and development in childhood are supported.\n- Stem cell transplant is aimed before liver damage occurs.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Arrange and monitor regular transfusions.\n2. Watch for iron overload from repeated packed RBCs; give chelation therapy regularly as ordered.\n3. Support growth and development, and teach the family about the inherited nature.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Sickle Cell Anemia and Crises]], [[Iron Deficiency Anemia]]. Iron supplements are not given in thalassemia; compare this with iron deficiency.\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout says \"globulin chains\"; the correct term is globin chains. "body": "## What it is\nA proliferative disorder of myeloid stem cells. Marrow is hypercellular and RBCs, WBCs and platelets are all raised. Blood becomes thick (high viscosity).\n\n## Assessment findings\n- Ruddy complexion, splenomegaly, headache, dizziness, tinnitus, blurred vision, fatigue, paresthesia.\n- Thick blood: angina, claudication, dyspnea, thrombophlebitis, high blood pressure.\n- High uric acid: gout and renal stones. Generalized itching; burning pain of fingers and toes (erythromelalgia, written \"erythromyalgia\" in the handout).\n- Diagnosis: CBC and bone marrow aspiration.\n\n## Complications\nStroke, myocardial infarction, bleeding (platelets work poorly).\n\n## Treatment\n- Phlebotomy: remove about 500 mL once or twice weekly at first to lower viscosity and iron stores.\n- Hydroxyurea (suppresses marrow), anagrelide (inhibits platelet aggregation), interferon alfa-2b for itching (watch flu-like symptoms and depression), antihistamines, allopurinol.\n\n## Client teaching\n- Avoid sitting still for long, crossing legs, tight clothing (clot risk).\n- Avoid aspirin products, iron supplements and excess alcohol.\n- Itching: cool or tepid baths, gentle patting dry, oatmeal or cocoa-butter products, baking soda in bath water.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Blood Components and Normal Hematologic Values]], [[Aplastic Anemia]] (opposite problem: too few cells)",

"body": "## What it is\nInherited bleeding disorder. Hemophilia A: deficient factor VIII. Hemophilia B (Christmas disease): deficient factor IX. Both are X-linked, so nearly all patients are male and females are carriers. Usually recognized in the toddler years.\n\n## Assessment findings\n- Bleeding anywhere: hemarthroses (joint bleeds) and hematomas; about 75% of bleeding is into joints, causing chronic pain and ankylosis.\n- Spontaneous hematuria and GI bleeding.\n- Intracranial or extracranial bleeding is the most dangerous.\n\n## Treatment\nFactor VIII or IX concentrates, fresh frozen plasma, aminocaproic acid (blocks clot breakdown), desmopressin (DDAVP) for a temporary rise in factor levels; plasmapheresis or immunosuppression when inhibitors develop.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Support independence and coping; offer genetic counselling to female carriers.\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout says desmopressin raises \"factor VII\"; it should read factor VIII. "id": "np3-ho-cancer-basics-tumors",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Cancer Basics: Neoplasm, Benign and Malignant Tumors",
"body": "## What it is\nCancer begins when a cell is changed by a mutation in its DNA and loses the controls that normally limit its growth. A neoplasm (\"new growth\") grows without any body need. \"Tumor\" in oncology nursing means a solid neoplasm. Surgical oncology is the branch that uses surgery to diagnose and treat cancer.\n\n## Word parts\n- neo = new; plasia = growth; plasm = substance; trophy = size; oma = tumor; meta = change; dys = bad or deranged; hyper = excessive; a/ana = none or lack.\n\n## Benign versus malignant\n- Benign: normal-looking, well-differentiated cells, encapsulated, grows by expanding, slow, no metastasis, good prognosis; treated mainly by surgery.\n- Malignant: abnormal, poorly differentiated cells, not encapsulated, infiltrates, fast, metastasizes, poor prognosis; treated with surgery, radiation, chemotherapy and bone marrow transplant.\n\n## Naming pairs (benign -oma / malignant)\n- Fat: lipoma / liposarcoma. Bone: osteoma / osteosarcoma. Muscle: myoma / myosarcoma.\n- Blood vessels: hemangioma / hemangiosarcoma. Fibrous tissue: fibroma / fibrosarcoma.\n- Glands: adenoma / adenocarcinoma. Nerve cell: neuroma / neuroblastoma. Cartilage: chondroma / chondrosarcoma.\n- Meninges: meningioma / meningeal sarcoma. Nerve sheath: neurilemmoma / neurilemmal sarcoma. Glial tissue: glioma / glioblastoma.\n- Pattern: carcinoma arises from epithelium, sarcoma from connective tissue.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Grading, Staging and Carcinogenesis]], [[Cancer Warning Signs and Diagnostic Tests]], [[Levels of Prevention]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout's \"meningeal sarcoma\" and \"neurilemmal sarcoma\" are the handout's own naming; textbooks may use other terms.",

"id": "np3-ho-grading-staging-carcinogenesis",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Grading, Staging and Carcinogenesis",
"body": "## Grading versus staging\n- Grading classifies the tumor cells: what tissue they came from and how much they still look and work like that tissue (well versus poorly differentiated).\n- Staging describes how far the cancer has spread, using TNM:\n  - T = extent of the primary tumor.\n  - N = lymph node involvement.\n  - M = metastasis.\n- Memory trick: Grade = cell look, Stage = spread.\n\n## Theories of cancer\n- Cellular transformation theory: normal cells turn cancerous after exposure to chemicals.\n- Failure of immune response theory: the immune system fails to destroy cancer cells.\n\n## Stages of cancer development\n1. Initiation: carcinogens escape the cell's normal enzyme defenses and change the structure of DNA.\n2. Promotion: repeated exposure to promoting agents lets the abnormal genetic information show.\n3. Progression: altered cells become more malignant, invade nearby tissue and metastasize.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nNurses are asked to separate grade from stage, and to place a statement in the right development stage (e.g. \"repeated exposure\" = promotion).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Cancer Basics: Neoplasm, Benign and Malignant Tumors]], [[Cancer Warning Signs and Diagnostic Tests]]",

"body": "## Warning signs (CAUTION US)\n- Change in bowel or bladder habits.\n- A sore that does not heal.\n- Unusual bleeding or discharge.\n- Thickening or lump (breast or elsewhere).\n- Indigestion or difficulty swallowing.\n- Obvious change in a wart or mole.\n- Nagging cough or hoarseness.\n- Unexplained anemia.\n- Sudden weight loss.\n\n## Diagnostic tests and what they are for\n- Tumor markers: substances made by the tumor found in tissue, blood or body fluids; used in breast, colon, lung, ovarian, testicular and prostate cancer.\n- Mammography: breast cancer.\n- MRI: neurologic, pelvic, abdominal, thoracic and breast cancers.\n- Fluoroscopy (x-rays showing contrast in tissue): skeletal, lung and GI cancers.\n- PET scan: lung, colon, liver, pancreatic cancer, and lymphomas.\n- Endoscopy (diagnosis and treatment): bronchial and GI cancers.\n- Biopsy: tissue sample examined for cells; confirms the diagnosis.\n\n## Nursing points\nExplain the test and what to expect; give emotional support. A tissue biopsy is what confirms cancer.\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout has a stray line about pernicious anemia and IM vitamin B12 under endoscopy; this seems misplaced (gastric endoscopy may be done for pernicious anemia). "id": "np3-ho-cancer-surgery-local-treatments",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Cancer Surgery and Local Treatments",
"body": "## What it is\nSurgery removes cancer wholly or partly. Surgical oncology is the branch of medicine that uses manual and instrument-based means to diagnose and treat cancer.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Local excision: removal of the mass plus some normal tissue around it.\n- Radical excision: removal of the primary tumor, lymph nodes and surrounding tissues (larger operation, more recovery).\n- Electrosurgery: electrical current destroys the tumor.\n- Cryosurgery: liquid nitrogen freezes the tissue and destroys cells.\n- Chemosurgery: topical chemotherapy together with surgical removal of tissue.\n- Surgery serves diagnosis (biopsy), cure (removal), and sometimes comfort (palliation).\n\n## Assessment\nBefore surgery: understanding of the plan, anxiety level, nutrition, other conditions. After surgery: incision, drains, pain, vital signs, and the signs below.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\nBefore surgery:\n1. Teach what to expect after surgery (pain control, drains, coughing and moving). Why: informed clients cooperate better and fear less.\n2. Give emotional support to reduce anxiety; anxious clients learn poorly, so teach in short steps.\nAfter surgery, watch for and report:\n1. Infection (fever, redness, drainage).\n2. Bleeding.\n3. Thrombophlebitis (leg pain, swelling).\n4. Wound dehiscence.\n5. Fluid and electrolyte imbalance.\n6. Organ dysfunction.\n\n## Treatment and medications\nSurgery is often combined with radiation, chemotherapy or bone marrow transplant for malignant tumors; benign tumors are usually treated by surgery alone.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nSudden heavy bleeding, wound edges separating, fever, calf pain, low urine output.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nDefinitions: local versus radical excision; which method freezes (cryosurgery); which uses liquid nitrogen. Also: which complication to watch after cancer surgery (any of the six above).\n\n## How to reason about it\nLarger resection (radical) = more tissue and nodes removed = bigger risk of bleeding, infection and swelling. The trap: choosing \"local excision\" for removal of lymph nodes.\n\n## Memory aid\n\"Electro burns, cryo freezes, chemo coats.\"\n\n## Related topics\n[[Perioperative Phases and Surgical Classifications]], [[Preoperative Assessment and Patient Teaching]], [[Surgical Wound Infection, Dehiscence and Evisceration]], [[Skin Cancer]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nDetails of drains, lymphedema care and nutrition after specific cancer operations are not in the handout.",

"area": "NP3",
"title": "External Radiation Therapy (Teletherapy)",
"body": "## What it is\nRadiation therapy uses ionizing radiation to interrupt cancer cell growth. In external radiation (teletherapy) a machine aims x-rays at the tumor from a distance, destroying cancer cells at the skin surface or deeper in the body.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- The radiation source is positioned far from the client.\n- Higher energy means deeper penetration.\n- The client is NOT radioactive and does not emit radiation, so there is no special isolation, and people can touch the client safely.\n- Main effects: radiodermatitis (skin changes at the treated area) and fatigue.\n\n## Assessment\nSkin in the treated area (redness, dryness, peeling, moist breakdown), energy level, appetite, mouth condition when the head or neck is treated.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Protect the treated skin: no ointments, lotions or powders on the area. Why: they can irritate skin and change the dose absorbed.\n2. Do not wash off or remove the marks drawn on the skin; they guide each treatment.\n3. Give gentle oral hygiene.\n4. Plan rest periods and spread out activities for fatigue.\n5. Report skin breakdown or worsening symptoms.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nMoist or broken skin, severe fatigue, inability to eat or drink, burns that worsen.\n\n## Client teaching\nYou are not radioactive; keep markings; avoid rubbing, heat, cold and sun on the area (general advice, confirm with your team); wear loose clothing over the site; keep appointments.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"Which statement by the client shows understanding?\" Correct: \"I will leave the marks on and I won't put lotion on the site.\" Wrong: \"I must stay away from my family.\"\n\n## Related topics\n[[Radiation Therapy Compared: External, Sealed and Unsealed]], [[Internal Radiation Therapy (Brachytherapy) and Radiation Precautions]], [[Chemotherapy Side Effects: GI, Hair and Mouth]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nSkin care products allowed and the exact schedule of fractions differ between centers; the handout gives only the points above.",

"id": "np3-ho-internal-radiation-brachytherapy",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Internal Radiation Therapy (Brachytherapy) and Radiation Precautions",
"body": "## What it is\nA high dose of radiation delivered to a small area by a source placed inside the body. The client is radioactive during and after the procedure, so staff protection matters.\n\n## Sealed versus unsealed\n- Sealed: the source stays in place (needles, seeds or beads, catheter, intracavitary for gynecologic cancer). Body fluids are NOT radioactive.\n- Unsealed: the source is absorbed in the body (IV or oral, e.g. iodine-131 for thyroid cancer). Body fluids ARE radioactive.\n\n## Radiation precautions: shielding, time, distance\n- Shielding: lead gown and a dosimeter badge to measure exposure.\n- Time: handout limits are a maximum of 30 minutes per shift and 5 minutes per visit.\n- Distance: stay about 6 feet away when possible.\n- Private room. Never assign a pregnant staff member; no child visitors.\n\n## Intracavitary brachytherapy care\nEmpty bladder and bowel before; complete bed rest so the implant stays in place; low-residue diet to slow stools; indwelling urinary catheter.\n\n## Unsealed source care\nFlush the toilet 3 times after voiding or defecating; visitors must not share the client's toilet.\n\n## Check against your textbook\nTime limits differ between textbooks and hospital policy; learn the handout numbers for the exam but verify with your text.\n\n## Related topics\n[[External Radiation Therapy (Teletherapy)]]",

"id": "np3-ho-chemo-drug-classes",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Chemotherapy Drug Classes and Cell Cycle",
"body": "## What it is\nChemotherapy uses antineoplastic drugs to destroy tumor cells.\n\n## Cell cycle\n- G1: RNA and protein synthesis. S: DNA synthesis. G2: pre-mitotic phase. M: mitosis. G0: resting (dormant) phase.\n\n## Cell-cycle-specific drugs (work in one phase)\n- Antimetabolites (block enzymes for DNA synthesis): methotrexate, 6-mercaptopurine, 6-thioguanine, cytarabine, fludarabine, hydroxyurea, pentostatin.\n- Topoisomerase inhibitors: irinotecan, topotecan.\n- Mitotic inhibitors (stop metaphase by blocking spindle formation): vincristine, vinblastine, vinorelbine, teniposide, paclitaxel.\n\n## Cell-cycle-non-specific drugs (work in all phases)\n- Alkylating agents (break the DNA helix): busulfan, carboplatin, cyclophosphamide, ifosfamide, cisplatin, thiotepa.\n- Antitumor antibiotics (names end -cin; interfere with DNA and RNA synthesis): bleomycin, dactinomycin, daunorubicin, doxorubicin, plicamycin.\n- Nitrosoureas (like alkylating agents but cross the blood-brain barrier): carmustine, lomustine, semustine, streptozocin.\n- Hormonal agents: block hormone receptors (androgens/anti-androgens, estrogens/anti-estrogens).\n- Monoclonal antibodies (kill cancer cells while sparing normal cells): rituximab, trastuzumab, alemtuzumab, gemtuzumab.\n\n## Routes\nOral (most convenient), IV (most common), intramuscular, intrathecal (Ommaya reservoir), intra-arterial, intracavitary, intravesical, topical.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Safe Handling of Chemotherapy Drugs]], [[Chemotherapy Side Effects: GI, Hair and Mouth]], [[Chemotherapy Side Effects: Bone Marrow Suppression]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nSome drugs are classified differently between books (hydroxyurea, cisplatin, bleomycin). Check your own list.",

"id": "np3-ho-chemo-safe-handling",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Safe Handling of Chemotherapy Drugs",
"body": "## Core idea\nChemo drugs are hazardous: there should be no contact with them. Pregnant women should not handle or receive chemotherapy (teratogenic).\n\n## Preparing chemo\n- Ventilated area; wash hands before and after.\n- Wear gloves at all times, a gown and a face shield.\n- Wrap gauze or an alcohol pad around ampule necks and around the injection site when withdrawing the needle.\n- Label prepared drugs as hazardous.\n- Dispose in a leak-proof and puncture-proof container.\n- Do not eat, chew or smoke while preparing.\n\n## Handling waste\n- Wear clean gloves; discard gloves and gown in a leak-proof container.\n- Linens with excreta go in a separate container and are washed in hot water.\n- Spill: use a spill kit.\n\n## Mnemonic (handout CHEMOTHERAPY)\nCheck phlebitis and extravasation; High calorie high protein diet; Encourage hydration; Monitor CBC; Oral exam for stomatitis; Teratogenic; Hair loss; Encourage counseling; Report complications; Administer antiemetics; Practice aseptic technique; You wear gloves, gown, mask.\n\n## Priority action in IV chemo\nCheck for phlebitis and extravasation; a leaking vesicant damages tissue, so stop the infusion and follow policy.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Chemotherapy Drug Classes and Cell Cycle]], [[Chemotherapy Side Effects: GI, Hair and Mouth]]",

"id": "np3-ho-chemo-side-effects-gi-hair-mouth",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Chemotherapy Side Effects: GI, Hair and Mouth",
"body": "## Nausea and vomiting\nMost common side effect; may last 24-48 hours, and delayed nausea can appear a week later. Linked to receptor activation, autonomic and vestibular stimulation, and serotonin. Care: good oral hygiene, check for dehydration, ice chips, antiemetics round-the-clock (prevent, do not wait).\n\n## Alopecia\nBegins about 2-3 weeks after starting, ends by about 3 months; regrowth can start near 8 weeks. Give accurate information before chemo; wigs for women, caps for men; pre-emptive haircut.\n\n## Stomatitis and xerostomia\nStomatitis = mouth sores; xerostomia = dry mouth.\n- Inspect the mouth routinely; oral care; soft toothbrush; saline rinses.\n- Avoid spicy and citrus food, and alcohol-based mouthwash.\n- Ice chips or popsicles; soft bland diet.\n- Viscous lidocaine for adults; contraindicated in children (suppressed gag reflex).\n\n## Anorexia\nTaste buds are affected; food (especially meat) tastes metallic. Comfortable position, hygiene, attractive meals, general comfort.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Chemotherapy Side Effects: Bone Marrow Suppression]], [[Chemotherapy Drug Classes and Cell Cycle]], [[Safe Handling of Chemotherapy Drugs]]",

"title": "Chemotherapy Side Effects: Bone Marrow Suppression",
"body": "## Why it matters\nChemo damages fast-dividing cells, including marrow, so RBCs, WBCs and platelets fall. Infection from neutropenia is the most dangerous.\n\n## Anemia\nCheck for pallor; plan activity with rest periods; give erythropoietin as ordered.\n\n## Neutropenia\n- Watch for infection: fever, abnormal lung sounds, cough.\n- Strict handwashing before and after procedures; clean care.\n- Neutropenic diet: no flowers, fresh fruits, vegetables or raw food.\n\n## Thrombocytopenia (bleeding risk)\n- Check skin and mouth for bleeding; check stool, urine and emesis.\n- Avoid anticoagulants and antiplatelet drugs.\n- Electric razor only; soft toothbrush.\n- No suppositories or enemas; use stool softeners.\n- Water-based lubricant before sexual activity.\n- Platelet transfusion as prescribed.\n\n## Priority thinking\nFever in a neutropenic client is an emergency: assess and report early because infection can progress quickly.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Aplastic Anemia]], [[Chemotherapy Side Effects: GI, Hair and Mouth]], [[Blood Components and Normal Hematologic Values]]",

"title": "Pancreatic Cancer and Whipple Procedure",
"body": "## What it is\nA fast-growing cancer with poor prognosis. Very rare before 45; more common at 70-80 years.\n\n## Risk factors\nSmoking, diabetes mellitus, gastrectomy, high-fat or meat diet, family history, chronic pancreatitis, industrial chemicals, alcoholism.\n\n## Assessment findings\nAbdominal pain, nausea and vomiting, blood sugar problems, rapid progressive weight loss, clay-colored (acholic) stools.\n\n## Diagnosis and surgery\n- ERCP (endoscopic retrograde cholangiopancreatography).\n- Whipple procedure (pancreaticoduodenectomy): removes the gallbladder, common bile duct, part of the duodenum and the head of the pancreas. Other options: total pancreatectomy, pancreatic resection.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Pain management.\n2. Improve fluid and nutritional status.\n3. Skin care for pruritus (from bile salts).\n\n## Check against your textbook\nHandout lists no pre/post-op details for Whipple. Check for typical postoperative needs (blood glucose monitoring, enzyme replacement, anastomosis leaks) in your textbook.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Hepatocellular Cancer]], [[Gallbladder Cancer and Post-Cholecystectomy Care]]",

"id": "np3-ho-hepatocellular-cancer",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Hepatocellular Cancer",
"body": "## What it is\nCancer that starts in the liver cells. The liver does protein synthesis, bile production, drug metabolism, hormone metabolism, glucose storage and clotting factor production, so liver cancer can disturb all of these.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Risk factors listed in the handout: hepatitis B infection, liver cirrhosis, aflatoxin, oral contraceptives, insulin therapy, alcohol, smoking, and the parasite Clonorchis sinensis.\n- Diagnosis: liver biopsy.\n- Surgery is the only curative treatment.\n\n## Assessment\nThe handout does not list symptoms. Think from liver function: bleeding or bruising (low clotting factors), jaundice (bile), easy drug toxicity, weight loss, abdominal discomfort, and fluid in the abdomen (added from general knowledge).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. After biopsy or surgery, watch for bleeding first (vital signs, site, signs of shock). Why: a liver that makes fewer clotting factors bleeds more.\n2. Support breathing and pain control after surgery.\n3. Monitor for drug side effects, because the liver breaks down many drugs.\n4. Teach avoiding alcohol and protecting against hepatitis B.\n\n## Treatment and medications\n- Liver resection (removal of the diseased part).\n- Liver transplant.\n- Cryosurgery: a liquid or very cold probe freezes tissue and destroys it.\n- Ethanol injection (alcohol injected into the tumor).\n\n## Complications and red flags\nBleeding, jaundice, confusion, fluid in the belly, signs of infection after surgery.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"What is the only curative treatment?\" Surgery (resection or transplant). \"Which test confirms diagnosis?\" Biopsy. Risk-factor questions: hepatitis B, cirrhosis, aflatoxin.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Pancreatic Cancer and Whipple Procedure]], [[Colorectal Cancer]], [[Cancers of the Pancreas, Liver, Gallbladder, Colon, Bladder and Skin: Quick Comparison]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nSymptoms, post-biopsy positioning and the listing of insulin therapy and oral contraceptives as risks are not detailed or are doubtful in the handout; verify them.",

"title": "Gallbladder Cancer and Post-Cholecystectomy Care",
"body": "## What it is\nThe most common cancer of the biliary tract, with poor prognosis. Risk factors: female sex, age around 65, gallstones (cholelithiasis). Diagnosis: ultrasound, CT scan, ERCP. Treatment: cholecystectomy.\n\n## Care after gallbladder surgery\n- Position: low Fowler's.\n- Pain: handout names morphine as the drug of choice.\n- Splinting with a pillow, deep breathing and coughing.\n- NPO or NGT as prescribed; after laparoscopic surgery fluids come within hours; then a low-fat soft diet.\n- T tube: keeps the bile duct open; report a sudden increase in drainage; clamp before meals and unclamp if nausea or vomiting occurs.\n\n## Report these signs\nRight upper abdominal pain, nausea or vomiting, clay or pale stools, change in vital signs, jaundice, dark urine, itching, signs of inflammation. These suggest bile duct obstruction or infection.\n\n## Check against your textbook\nMany textbooks avoid morphine in biliary disease because it may cause sphincter of Oddi spasm (meperidine was historically preferred). Also T-tube clamp times are policy-dependent. Verify.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Pancreatic Cancer and Whipple Procedure]], [[Postoperative Respiratory Complications]]",

"title": "Colorectal Cancer",
"body": "## What it is\nCancer of the colon or rectum, equally common in men and women, usually after age 40.\n\n## Risk factors\nAge, family history, previous colon cancer or polyps, alcohol, smoking, obesity, gastrectomy, inflammatory bowel disease, diet high in fat and protein and low in fiber, genital cancer.\n\n## Assessment findings\nChange in bowel habits (most common), blood on or in stool (second most common), pencil-like stool, unexplained anemia, anorexia, weight loss, fatigue, dull abdominal pain, melena, constipation, distention.\n\n## Diagnosis\nDigital rectal exam yearly after 40; fecal occult blood test yearly after 50; colonoscopy, proctosigmoidoscopy, CEA (tumor marker), CT scan.\n\n## Complications\nBowel obstruction, hemorrhage, perforation, abscess, peritonitis, sepsis, shock.\n\n## Surgery\nHemicolectomy (right or left side), transverse colectomy, sigmoidectomy, Miles' procedure (tumor, part of sigmoid, whole rectum and anal sphincter; leads to a permanent colostomy).\n\n## Preoperative care\nBowel cleansing: low-residue diet for 3-5 days, clear liquids then NPO after midnight, laxative and cleansing enema, neomycin tablet as pre-op antibiotic.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Colostomy and Stoma Care]], [[Preoperative Assessment and Patient Teaching]], [[Cancer Warning Signs and Diagnostic Tests]]",
