"id": "np3-ho-colostomy-stoma-care",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Colostomy and Stoma Care",
"body": "## Stoma assessment (priority)\n- Normal: red and slightly protruding (about 1/2 inch), moist.\n- Abnormal: dark, dusky, brown to black means poor blood supply (necrosis); report at once.\n- The stoma has no touch or pain sensation.\n\n## Postoperative care\n- Warm sitz bath for the perineal wound.\n- Flatus and stool begin about 4-7 days.\n- Empty the pouch when 1/3 to 1/2 full (handout \"1/3 to 3/4\"; check).\n- Skin protection: karaya paste as a barrier, talc or cornstarch; never use mineral oil. Nystatin (written \"statin\") powder for Candida infection.\n\n## Colostomy irrigation (to train regular evacuation)\n1. Semi-Fowler's position; warm normal saline, start with about 200 mL.\n2. Clean gloves; dilate the stoma gently; lubricate the catheter with water-based lubricant and insert 2-4 inches.\n3. Hang the container 12-18 inches above the stoma.\n4. Retain the catheter 5-10 minutes; drain in 15-20 minutes.\n5. If cramps occur, stop momentarily, then continue slowly.\n\n## Diet\nAvoid gas-forming foods: cabbage, nuts, eggs, cauliflower, gum, carbonated drinks.\n\n## Check against your textbook\nPouch-emptying level and irrigation volumes vary between texts. Irrigation is for a descending/sigmoid colostomy with formed stool; confirm.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Colorectal Cancer]], [[Bladder Cancer and Urinary Diversions]]",

"id": "np3-ho-skin-cancer",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Skin Cancer",
"body": "## What it is\nCancer of the skin. Sun exposure is the leading cause.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Basal cell carcinoma: the most common type; rarely metastasizes; good prognosis.\n- Squamous cell carcinoma: can metastasize (spread).\n- Malignant melanoma: the deadliest; the handout calls it the third most common type; arises from birthmarks or moles anywhere on the body.\n- ABCDE rule for suspicious moles: Asymmetry, Border irregularity, Color variation, Diameter, Elevation (or evolution, change over time).\n- Risk factors: fair skin, fair hair and blue eyes; sunburns; chronic sun exposure; chemical pollutants; sun-damaged skin in the elderly; past x-ray therapy; immunosuppression; genetic factors.\n\n## Assessment\nLook at the skin head to toe, including the scalp, between toes and nail beds; note any new or changing lesion, a sore that will not heal, or a mole that bleeds or itches.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Teach self-examination of the skin and the ABCDE signs. Why: finding it early is the best chance of cure.\n2. Teach sun protection (sunscreen, hats, covering clothing, avoiding peak hours; general advice).\n3. After treatment: teach dressing changes; watch for excessive bleeding and dressings that are too tight; use emollient cream for dryness.\n4. Arrange follow-up examinations every 3 months (per handout).\n\n## Treatment and medications\nElectrosurgery (destroys tissue by electrical energy), cryosurgery (deep freezing), radiation therapy. Larger or advanced lesions need wider surgery, depending on the surgeon.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nSpread to lymph nodes (hard painful nodes), a mole that suddenly grows, bleeds or changes color, wound infection after treatment.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nIdentify the deadliest type (melanoma), the most common type (basal cell), which one spreads (squamous cell and melanoma); apply ABCDE to a described mole.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause (UV exposure) -> finding (changing lesion) -> priority action (refer for biopsy). The trap: advising to watch an irregular mole \"for another few months\".\n\n## Memory aid\nA-B-C-D-E: \"Asymmetric, Border, Color, Diameter, Evolving.\"\n\n## Related topics\n[[Cancer Surgery and Local Treatments]], [[Cancer Warning Signs and Diagnostic Tests]], [[Cancers of the Pancreas, Liver, Gallbladder, Colon, Bladder and Skin: Quick Comparison]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout's \"Elevation\" for E is given as written; many texts say \"Evolving\". The size threshold for Diameter is not in the handout.",

"id": "np3-ho-periop-phases-classifications",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Perioperative Phases and Surgical Classifications",
"body": "## Three phases\n- Preoperative: from the decision to operate until the client is transferred onto the OR table.\n- Intraoperative: from transfer to the OR table until admission to the PACU (post anesthesia care unit).\n- Postoperative: from PACU admission until follow-up evaluation in the clinic or at home.\n\n## Purpose of surgery\n- Diagnostic: biopsy, exploratory laparotomy.\n- Curative: removing a tumor or inflamed appendix.\n- Reparative: multiple wound repair.\n- Reconstructive or cosmetic: mammoplasty, facelift.\n- Palliative: relieves symptoms, e.g. a gastrostomy tube when swallowing is impossible.\n\n## Urgency\n- Optional: purely the client's preference (cosmetic).\n- Elective: at the client's convenience; delay is not catastrophic.\n- Required: needed within a few weeks (cataract).\n- Urgent: within 24-48 hours (cancer).\n- Emergency: immediate, without delay (intestinal obstruction).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch a case to its urgency level; the more life-threatening, the sooner.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Informed Consent for Surgery]], [[Preoperative Assessment and Patient Teaching]], [[Post Anesthesia Care Unit and Aldrete Score]]",

"id": "np3-ho-informed-consent-surgery",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Informed Consent for Surgery",
"body": "## What it is\nInformed consent is the client's own autonomous decision whether to undergo surgery, after being told the procedure, risks and possible complications of surgery and anesthesia. It is a legal requirement.\n\n## Who does what\n- The surgeon obtains the consent and explains the procedure.\n- The nurse checks that the form is signed and witnesses the signature; the circulating nurse verifies the consent again in the OR.\n\n## Purposes\n- Client understands the treatment and its complications.\n- The decision was made without pressure.\n- Protects the client from unauthorized procedures or the wrong body part.\n- Protects the surgical team and hospital from legal action.\n\n## Priority actions\nIf the client seems unsure or unaware, do not get the signature; notify the surgeon so the explanation is given first.\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe nurse-witness role and emergency/minor consent rules are added from general knowledge. Check Philippine rules in your textbook.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Operating Room Team Roles]], [[Immediate Pre-operative Preparation]]",

"id": "np3-ho-or-team-roles",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Operating Room Team Roles",
"body": "## Circulating nurse\nUnscrubbed, moves around the room.\n- Verifies consent and coordinates the team.\n- Ensures cleanliness, proper temperature, humidity, lighting, safe equipment and supplies.\n- Monitors aseptic practice for breaks in technique; manages traffic of x-ray, lab and medical staff; follows fire safety rules.\n- Makes sure the second verification of procedure and site is done and documented.\n\n## Scrub nurse\nScrubbed, gowned and gloved; handles sterile items.\n- Performs the surgical hand scrub; sets up sterile tables.\n- Prepares sutures, ligatures and special equipment (e.g. laparoscope).\n- Assists the surgeon by anticipating instruments, sponges and drains.\n- Counts needles, sponges and instruments with the circulator so none is left in the client.\n\n## Surgeon\nHeads the team, performs the surgery, and holds ultimate responsibility for effective, safe surgery.\n\n## Anesthesiologist\nAssesses the client, chooses and gives anesthesia, intubates if needed, and watches the client throughout: BP, pulse, respirations, ECG, oxygen saturation, tidal volume, blood gases, pH, anesthetic gas concentration and temperature.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nScrub = sterile, near the field; circulator = non-sterile, documents and coordinates. Counts are done by both.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Informed Consent for Surgery]], [[Operating Room Zones and Surgical Attire]], [[Principles of Surgical Asepsis]]",

"id": "np3-ho-or-zones-attire",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Operating Room Zones and Surgical Attire",
"body": "## Environment\nThe surgical suite is behind double doors with limited access and restricted traffic. Strict asepsis is required.\n\n## Three zones\n- Unrestricted: street clothes allowed; reception and holding area.\n- Semi-restricted: scrub attire and caps; may include instrument-processing areas.\n- Restricted: scrub clothes, caps, shoe covers and masks; the OR itself and the sterile core.\n\n## Attire\n- Scrub suit: two-piece, well fitted, drawstrings tucked in; change if wet or soiled.\n- Head cover: covers all hair; disposable caps preferred; do not comb hair while in scrubs; net caps do not stop contamination.\n- Shoes: comfortable and puncture resistant; shoe covers when splashes expected; no street shoes; dispose of covers before leaving the OR.\n- Mask: worn in the restricted area; covers nose and mouth completely and fits tightly; handle by ties; the front is contaminated; never hang it on the neck or place it on the cap or in a pocket; change between patients.\n- Eyewear or face shield for splash and bone drilling; laser eyewear for laser surgery.\n- Gloves: nonsterile for clean procedures, sterile for sterile procedures.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Principles of Surgical Asepsis]], [[Operating Room Team Roles]]",

"id": "np3-ho-surgical-asepsis-principles",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Principles of Surgical Asepsis",
"body": "## Core principles\n- Everything touching the wound or tissue is sterilized before use.\n- The team scrubs hands and arms with antiseptic soap and water, or an alcohol-based product.\n- Only scrubbed, gowned and gloved persons touch sterile objects.\n- The OR environment is meticulously cleaned; the skin around the site is cleaned over a larger area than needed and an antiseptic applied.\n\n## Rules for keeping sterility\n- Only sterile touches sterile.\n- Gowns are sterile in front from chest to the level of the sterile field; sleeves from 2 inches above the elbow to the cuff.\n- Only the top surface of a draped table is sterile.\n- Deliver sterile supplies and solutions without contaminating them.\n- Team members move sterile-to-sterile and unsterile-to-unsterile.\n- Movement around the field must not contaminate it.\n- A breached barrier means contaminated.\n- If in doubt, consider it unsterile.\n\n## Priority action\nWhen you think something may have been contaminated, treat it as contaminated and replace it.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Decontamination, Disinfection and Sterilization]], [[Operating Room Zones and Surgical Attire]]",

"id": "np3-ho-decontamination-sterilization",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Decontamination, Disinfection and Sterilization",
"body": "## Steps of decontamination\nCleaning, then disinfection (low, intermediate, high), then sterilization.\n- Cleaning: removes foreign material by scrubbing and enzymes or detergents.\n- High-level disinfection: boiling at 100 C (kills most pathogens except spores); pasteurization at 60-80 C for 30 minutes (respiratory devices, anesthesia circuits); chemical soaking for heat-sensitive items.\n- Sterilization: destroys all pathogens including spores; the highest level.\n\n## Methods of sterilization\n- Autoclave (steam): most common; 121 C, 1 atm pressure, 30 minutes per handout.\n- Ethylene oxide gas: heat- or moisture-sensitive items.\n- Dry heat: oils, petroleum products, talc powder.\n\n## Indicators\nIndicators do not prove sterility; they only show the item went through the process. Sterility is event-related, not time-based; expiry date is an estimate; shelf life depends on handling, packaging and storage.\n\n## Spaulding's classification\n- Non-critical (intact skin): stethoscope, BP cuff, tourniquet, floors, linens. Cleaning or low-level disinfection.\n- Semi-critical (mucous membranes, non-intact skin): anesthesia equipment, GI endoscopes, speculum, bronchoscope, laryngoscope, thermometer, respiratory therapy equipment. High-level disinfection.\n- Critical (enter sterile tissue, body cavity or blood vessels): surgical instruments, IUDs, vascular catheters, implants, urinary catheters, needles. Sterilization.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Principles of Surgical Asepsis]], [[Surgical Instruments]]",

"sterilization",
"infection control"

"id": "np3-ho-general-anesthesia-stages",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "General Anesthesia and Its Stages",
"body": "## What it is\nAnesthesia is narcosis (deep CNS depression by drugs) with analgesia, relaxation and loss of reflexes. General anesthesia is reversible complete loss of consciousness, with analgesia, muscle relaxation and sedation. Protective reflexes are lost and the client cannot keep their own airway or breathing, so airway support is essential.\n\n## Giving general anesthesia\n- Inhalation: volatile liquids (halothane, enflurane, isoflurane, sevoflurane, desflurane) and gas (nitrous oxide, most used, always combined with oxygen).\n- Intravenous: barbiturates, benzodiazepines, non-barbiturate hypnotics, dissociative agents, opioids.\n\n## Stages\n1. Beginning / induction: detachment, drowsiness, dizziness, hallucination, ringing in ears. Keep OR quiet, close doors, stand by.\n2. Excitement / delirium: dilated pupils, rapid pulse, irregular breathing, uncontrolled movement; restraints may be needed.\n3. Surgical anesthesia: unconscious, quiet; small reactive pupils; regular respirations; normal pulse; pink skin. This is the desired stage.\n4. Medullary depression / danger: too much anesthesia; shallow breathing, weak thready pulse, wide pupils; death can occur. Stop the anesthetic and support breathing and circulation.\n\n## Preoperative medications\nReduce anxiety, promote relaxation, reduce pharyngeal secretions, prevent laryngospasm, inhibit gastric secretion, and lower the amount of anesthetic needed.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Regional, Local and Sedation Anesthesia]], [[Post Anesthesia Care Unit and Aldrete Score]]",

"anesthesia"

"id": "np3-ho-regional-local-sedation",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Regional, Local and Sedation Anesthesia",
"body": "## Regional anesthesia\nAnesthetic is injected around nerves to numb the area they supply. The client is awake unless sedated.\n- Epidural: local anesthetic into the epidural space around the dura. Advantage: no headache. Disadvantage: technically harder than subarachnoid injection.\n- Spinal: local anesthetic into the subarachnoid space at the lumbar level, usually L4-L5; anesthetizes the lower extremities, perineum and lower abdomen.\n\n## Other types\n- Moderate sedation (formerly conscious sedation): IV sedatives or analgesics to reduce anxiety and pain during procedures.\n- Monitored anesthesia care (MAC): moderate sedation by an anesthesiologist or anesthetist who can convert to general anesthesia.\n- Local anesthesia: solution injected into the tissue at the incision site.\n\n## Nursing thinking\nAfter spinal anesthesia, keep close watch on breathing, blood pressure and return of sensation and movement. Spinal headache is a known concern (added from general knowledge).\n\n## Related topics\n[[General Anesthesia and Its Stages]], [[Post Anesthesia Care Unit and Aldrete Score]]",

"id": "np3-ho-preop-assessment-teaching",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Preoperative Assessment and Patient Teaching",
"body": "## Goal\nMake the client as healthy as possible before surgery.\n\n## Assessment\nConsent, health history, nutrition and fluids, dentition, alcohol and drug use, respiratory, cardiovascular, hepatic, renal and endocrine function, and medicines used.\n\n## Preoperative teaching\n- Deep breathing, coughing and the incentive spirometer: breathe deeply, exhale through the mouth, take a short breath and cough from deep in the lungs.\n- Early and frequent movement, leg exercises and position changes to prevent complications.\n- Pain control: take analgesics on schedule early after surgery.\n- Reduce anxiety: help the client find coping strategies used before.\n\n## Nutrition and fluids\nNPO overnight or longer to prevent aspiration; the length depends on age and the type of food (fatty food needs about 8 hours).\n\n## Bowel and skin preparation\n- Enemas are not routine, but may be given for abdominal or pelvic surgery for good visualization and to avoid fecal contamination; cleansing enema or laxatives the evening before and sometimes the morning of surgery.\n- Skin: aim to lower bacteria without injury; wash with detergent-germicide soap; if hair must be removed, use electric clippers.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Immediate Pre-operative Preparation]], [[Postoperative Respiratory Complications]], [[Informed Consent for Surgery]]",

"id": "np3-ho-immediate-preop-prep",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Immediate Pre-operative Preparation",
"body": "## What it is\nThe final checks and tasks done in the last hour or so before the client goes to the operating room.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- The client is moved to the holding area or presurgical suite 30 to 60 minutes before the anesthetic is given.\n- The complete chart goes with the client: preoperative checklist, verification form, signed consent form, laboratory reports and nurses' notes.\n- If premedication was given, the client stays in bed with side rails up.\n- Client safety in the preoperative area is the priority.\n\n## Assessment (checklist)\nIdentification band, consent signed, NPO status, allergies, last vital signs, voiding, removal of items, completeness of chart.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Help change into a hospital gown; cover the hair completely with a cap.\n2. Inspect the mouth; remove dentures and plates. Why: they can block the airway during anesthesia.\n3. Remove jewelry and body piercings; if the client objects to removing a wedding ring, secure it with tape.\n4. Valuables go to family members, or are labelled and stored securely per hospital policy.\n5. Have the client void before going to the OR.\n6. Give the premedication on time; keep the client in bed with side rails up afterward.\n7. Send the complete chart with the consent attached.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nMissing consent, wrong or missing identification, client ate or drank recently, new fever or cough, unreported allergies. Report these to the surgeon and anesthesiologist before transfer.\n\n## Client teaching\nExplain each step, what to expect in the OR and recovery, and reassure about pain control.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nA list of tasks where one is wrong (for example, leaving dentures in); or \"what is the first priority?\" (safety, then the checklist).\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause (anesthesia removes protective reflexes) -> risk (objects in mouth, full stomach) -> action (remove dentures, confirm NPO). The trap: sending the client with the consent form missing.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Preoperative Assessment and Patient Teaching]], [[Informed Consent for Surgery]], [[Preoperative Medications and Their Purpose]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nWedding ring taping follows the handout; many facilities prefer sending rings home. Follow the policy.",

"title": "Surgical Positions",
"body": "## Positions and typical uses\n- Supine (dorsal): front-of-body procedures (abdominal, some leg). Variants: dorsal recumbent (vaginal/perineal), frog-leg (groin and lower extremities), arm extension (breast, axilla, arm, hand). Small roll under the shoulder for shoulder surgery.\n- Trendelenburg: lower abdominal and pelvic surgery; the intestines shift upward for exposure.\n- Reverse Trendelenburg: thyroidectomy, gallbladder, biliary and stomach surgery.\n- Fowler's: shoulder, nasopharyngeal, facial and breast reconstruction.\n- Sitting: ear-nose-throat and neurosurgery (occasionally).\n- Lithotomy: perineal, vaginal, urologic and rectal procedures.\n- Prone: posterior approaches; modified prone for spine and neurosurgery.\n- Kraske (jack-knife): rectal surgery (pilonidal sinus, hemorrhoidectomy); hips over the table break, head and legs lowered.\n- Knee-chest: sigmoidoscopy or culdoscopy.\n- Lateral: renal surgery.\n\n## Memory aid\nMatch the position to where the surgeon needs access: legs apart and up (lithotomy) for the pelvis floor, side-lying for the kidney.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Intraoperative Complications and Positioning Safety]]",

"id": "np3-ho-intraop-complications-positioning",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Intraoperative Complications and Positioning Safety",
"body": "## Nausea and vomiting\nGive antiemetics as ordered to prevent aspiration. If gagging occurs: turn the client to the side, lower the head of the table, provide a basin and suction saliva and vomitus.\n\n## Respiratory complications\nCan cause brain damage if missed. Monitor oxygenation closely and check peripheral perfusion often.\n\n## Hypothermia\nCauses: cool OR, cold fluids, cold gases, open wounds or cavities, reduced muscle activity. Actions: warm IV and irrigation fluids, remove wet gowns and drapes promptly, replace with dry ones. The handout says room temperature may be temporarily set at 25-26.6 (degree symbol lost).\n\n## Preventing positioning injury\n- Keep the client as comfortable as possible and the site well exposed.\n- Stirrups, traction or pressure must not block circulation.\n- Chest pressure from arms or a tight gown must not limit breathing.\n- Protect nerves: pad shoulder braces well, especially in Trendelenburg; improper arm and leg placement can cause paralysis.\n- Extra care for thin, elderly, obese clients and those with deformities.\n- Light restraint before induction if the client may become excited.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Surgical Positions]], [[General Anesthesia and Its Stages]]",

"title": "Surgical Instruments",
"body": "## Cutting and dissecting\n- Scalpel blades 10, 11, 12, 15 fit handle #3 or #7; blades 20, 22, 25 fit handle #4.\n- #10 opens skin; #11 makes a small stab for deep tiny incisions; #12 is commonly used for tonsillectomy; #15 is for short, shallow, controlled incisions; #20 is like #10 but larger.\n- Scissors: suture scissors cut sutures; wire scissors cut wires; bandage scissors cut drains and dressings; Mayo scissors cut heavy fascia and sutures; Metzenbaum scissors are more delicate for delicate tissue.\n- Curettes scrape tissue from bone.\n\n## Grasping and holding\n- Delicate forceps (eye tissue), Adson (soft tissue during closure), smooth thumb forceps (protect sutures), toothed forceps (tough tissue), Allis (tough tissue), Babcock (rounded jaws; fallopian tube), Lahey (sharp tips; tough organs or tumors), stone forceps (kidney and gallbladder stones).\n\n## Clamping and occluding\nHemostatic forceps occlude blood vessels; crushing clamps crush tissue or clamp vessels.\n\n## Exposing and retracting\nMalleable retractors bend to fit; hooks retract skin edges (e.g. mastectomy); self-retaining retractors (e.g. Balfour) hold the incision open.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Sutures and Surgical Needles]], [[Decontamination, Disinfection and Sterilization]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nSome handout descriptions (e.g. #12 blade, forceps uses) are brief; confirm with your book.",

"id": "np3-ho-sutures-needles",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Sutures and Surgical Needles",
"body": "## Absorbable sutures\n- Surgical gut (from sheep or beef intestine): plain gut loses strength in 5-10 days and is digested within about 70 days; used for small vessels and subcutaneous fat.\n- Chromic gut: supports the wound about 14 days, loses tensile strength by 21 days, absorbed in about 90 days; used for larger vessels and urinary or biliary tract.\n- Synthetic absorbables (absorbed by slow hydrolysis): polydioxanone (PDS) for slow-healing tissue such as fascia; poliglecaprone 25 (Monocryl) loses all strength by 21 days, used in gynecologic, urologic and plastic surgery; polyglactin 910 (Vicryl) absorbs within about 90 days.\n\n## Non-absorbable sutures\n- Silk: loses strength when wet; GI serosa and fascia closure without infection.\n- Cotton: gains strength when wet; most tissues.\n- Stainless steel: abdominal wall and retention sutures to prevent dehiscence.\n\n## Needles\n- Point: cutting (skin, tendon, tough tissue; conventional, reverse, side cutting, trocar); taper or round (intestines, peritoneum); blunt (friable liver and kidney).\n- Body: straight for accessible tissue; curved for most tissue; French eye has a slit for threading; eyeless (swaged) needle is fused to the suture.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Surgical Instruments]], [[Surgical Wound Infection, Dehiscence and Evisceration]]",

"id": "np3-ho-pacu-aldrete",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Post Anesthesia Care Unit and Aldrete Score",
"body": "## PACU\nAlso called the recovery room, next to the OR suite.\n- Phase I: immediate care and close monitoring, intensive care if needed.\n- Phase II: client no longer needs close monitoring; prepared for self-care or care in the ward or extended care.\n- Phase III: continued care then preparation for discharge from the facility.\n\n## Aldrete score\nRates activity, respiration, circulation, consciousness and oxygen saturation. A score of 8-10 is usual before leaving the PACU; below 7 stays until improved or goes to intensive care.\n\n## Nursing management in the PACU\n1. Assess airway first, then breathing, circulation, skin color, level of consciousness and response to commands.\n2. Check the surgical site for drainage or bleeding; make sure drains and monitoring lines are connected and working.\n3. Vital signs every 15 minutes.\n4. Give postoperative analgesics.\n5. Keep the airway open: watch for choking, noisy irregular breathing; suction secretions or vomitus (careful after tonsil, oral or laryngeal surgery).\n6. Control pain and anxiety: IV opioids are mostly used, short acting with immediate relief.\n7. Control nausea at the first report (before vomiting): antiemetics such as promethazine (Phenergan) and hydroxyzine (Atarax); encourage deep breathing to remove anesthetic.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Postoperative Priorities of Care]], [[Postoperative Hemorrhage and Hypovolemic Shock]], [[General Anesthesia and Its Stages]]",

"id": "np3-ho-postop-priorities",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Postoperative Priorities of Care",
"body": "## Priorities in order (per handout)\n1. Restore homeostasis and prevent complications.\n2. Maintain a clear airway and good respiratory function.\n3. Maintain cardiac function and tissue perfusion.\n4. Maintain fluid and electrolyte balance and renal function: enough fluid for blood volume without overload (pulmonary congestion and edema); monitor electrolytes; accurate intake and output; do not force fluids too soon.\n5. Promote comfort and rest: analgesics and comfort measures.\n6. Promote nutrition and elimination: peristalsis returns in 48-72 hours; start liquids, then advance; early ambulation prevents distention.\n7. Promote wound healing and prevent dehiscence and evisceration.\n\n## Why this order\nIt follows airway, breathing, circulation first (Maslow physiological needs), then fluid balance, comfort and healing.\n\n## Definitions\n- Dehiscence: wound edges separate partly or fully, but deeper tissue is not open.\n- Evisceration: organs protrude through the wound opening.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Post Anesthesia Care Unit and Aldrete Score]], [[Surgical Wound Infection, Dehiscence and Evisceration]], [[Postoperative Respiratory Complications]]",

"id": "np3-ho-postop-hemorrhage-shock",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Postoperative Hemorrhage and Hypovolemic Shock",
"body": "## What it is\nHypotension after surgery can come from blood loss, hypoventilation, position change, blood pooling or drugs. Shock, from low blood volume (hypovolemia), is one of the most serious postoperative complications.\n\n## Classification of hemorrhage\n- By time: primary (at surgery); intermediary (first hours, when BP rising dislodges loose clots); secondary (days later, suture slips, infection or drain erosion).\n- By vessel: capillary (slow ooze); venous (dark blood flowing quickly); arterial (bright red spurts with heartbeat).\n- By visibility: evident (seen) or concealed (inside a cavity).\n\n## Signs of hemorrhage and early shock\nHypotension, rapid thready pulse, restlessness, apprehension, disorientation, oliguria, cold pale skin.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Call for help; treat the airway and circulation: oxygen; start or increase IV fluids/blood products as ordered; find the cause.\n2. Inspect the incision; if bleeding is visible, apply a sterile gauze and pressure dressing and elevate the site to heart level if possible.\n3. Shock position: flat on back, legs raised about 20 degrees and kept straight.\n4. Keep warm; monitor continuously.\n5. If internal hemorrhage is suspected, the client returns to the OR.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Types of Shock]], [[Management of Shock]], [[Post Anesthesia Care Unit and Aldrete Score]]",

"id": "np3-ho-postop-respiratory-complications",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Postoperative Respiratory Complications",
"body": "## Why they happen\nOpioid breathing depression, pain-limited lung expansion and reduced movement.\n\n## Atelectasis\nAlveolar collapse with incomplete expansion. Signs: decreased breath sounds over the area, crackles, cough. Most common early postoperative lung problem.\n\n## Pneumonia\nChills and fever, tachycardia, tachypnea; cough may or may not be productive.\n\n## Hypostatic pulmonary congestion\nSecretions pool at the lung bases in a weakened cardiovascular system: slight rise in temperature, pulse and respiration; cough; dullness and crackles at the bases.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Turn, deep breathe, cough and use the incentive spirometer at least every 2 hours.\n2. Splint the abdominal or chest incision with a pillow to lessen fear of opening it.\n3. Give oxygen; encourage yawning and sustained maximal inspirations.\n4. Early ambulation increases metabolism and lung aeration.\n5. Coughing is contraindicated after head injury, intracranial surgery, eye surgery and plastic surgery (pressure rises).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Preoperative Assessment and Patient Teaching]], [[Early Ambulation after Surgery]], [[Postoperative Pulmonary Embolism]]",

"id": "np3-ho-early-ambulation",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Early Ambulation after Surgery",
"body": "## What it is\nGetting the client up and moving soon after surgery, in safe steps, to prevent complications and speed recovery.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Benefits: prevents atelectasis, hypostatic pneumonia, gastrointestinal discomfort (distention), blood stasis and thromboembolism (clots).\n- Walking also raises metabolism and lung aeration.\n- Bed exercises help when the client cannot yet walk: arm exercises, hand and finger exercises, foot exercises (prevent DVT, foot drop and toe deformities), leg flexion and leg lifting (prepare for walking), abdominal and gluteal contractions.\n\n## Assessment\nBefore standing: vital signs, pain level, dizziness, incision and drains, IV lines, level of consciousness, ability to follow instructions, and order for activity.\n\n## Priority nursing actions (steps in order)\n1. Raise the head of the bed gradually; client splints the incision with a pillow.\n2. Help the client sit upright with both legs hanging over the bed edge; wait and watch for dizziness. Why: a sudden change in position can drop blood pressure after surgery or anesthesia.\n3. Help the client stand beside the bed, then walk.\n4. Stand on the client's side to give physical support and encouragement.\n5. Give pain relief beforehand if needed so the client can move.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nDizziness, fainting, pallor, sudden breathlessness or chest pain (possible pulmonary embolism), calf pain or swelling (possible DVT), new bleeding at the incision.\n\n## Client teaching\nExplain why moving matters; practice splinting, leg and foot exercises; call for help before getting up; avoid sitting or standing still for long.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"What is the most important reason for early ambulation?\" Prevention of complications (lung and clots). Safety question: dangle the legs first, then stand.\n\n## How to reason about it\nImmobility (cause) -> pooled secretions and slow blood flow (finding) -> clots and pneumonia (risk) -> move early with support (action). The trap: an answer that skips the dangling step or leaves the client alone.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Postoperative Deep Vein Thrombosis]], [[Postoperative Respiratory Complications]], [[Postoperative Pulmonary Embolism]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe timing of the first walk depends on the surgery and orders; the handout does not give hours.",

"id": "np3-ho-postop-dvt",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Postoperative Deep Vein Thrombosis",
"body": "## What it is\nA clot in the pelvic veins or deep leg veins. Most common after hip surgery. Clots above the knee are the main source of pulmonary emboli.\n\n## Signs\nCalf pain on dorsiflexion (Homan's sign), painful swelling of the whole leg, mild fever, chills, sweating, tenderness over the inner thigh. Circulation below the clot may be compromised.\n\n## Prevention and nursing actions\n- Hydrate to prevent hemoconcentration.\n- Leg exercises and early ambulation as permitted.\n- Avoid tight straps, bed rolls and knee-gatch; avoid sitting or standing still for long and crossing legs.\n- Do NOT rub or massage the calves or thighs (it can dislodge the clot).\n- Check distal pulses, capillary refill and sensation.\n- Elastic stockings or wraps from toes upward; intermittent pneumatic compression for highest-risk clients.\n- Anticoagulants IV, subcutaneously or orally as prescribed.\n\n## Check against your textbook\nMany textbooks now say Homan's sign is unreliable; if DVT is suspected, report it and keep the client on bed rest per order.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Postoperative Pulmonary Embolism]], [[Early Ambulation after Surgery]]",

"body": "## Wound infection\nSecond most common hospital-acquired infection. May stay at the site or become systemic.\n\n## Signs\nRedness, swelling, tenderness, warmth; red streaks; pus; tender enlarged nodes in the groin or axilla; foul smell; chills, fever, rising pulse; increasing pain.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Keep the dressing intact (reinforce if needed) until ordered otherwise.\n- Strict sterile technique for dressing changes.\n- Document the amount, type and location of drainage; make sure drains work.\n- Send a wound culture; give antibiotics as prescribed.\n- Irrigation with saline may be ordered; a drain or sterile packing may be used.\n- Deep infection may need return to the OR.\n\n## Dehiscence and evisceration\n- Dehiscence: wound edges separate; deeper tissue stays closed.\n- Evisceration: organs protrude through the wound.\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout only defines these two. "id": "np3-ho-x-parenteral-iron-z-track",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Parenteral Iron Therapy and the Z-Track Technique",
"body": "## What it is\nIron given by injection (instead of by mouth) when oral iron is not tolerated or not absorbed. The handout places this teaching under the pernicious anemia section, but it is iron therapy, so it belongs with iron deficiency anemia.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- The Z-track method keeps the drug from leaking back up the needle path into the skin and fat. This prevents staining of the skin (discoloration) and tissue irritation.\n- Do not massage the site afterwards. Massage pushes drug toward the surface; walking is encouraged instead, because muscle movement helps absorption.\n- Iron by injection can cause anaphylaxis, so the client is watched closely after the dose.\n\n## Assessment\nWatch for local and general side effects named in the handout:\n- Pain at the injection site and a localized abscess.\n- Lymphadenopathy (swollen nodes).\n- Fever and chills.\n- Skin rashes.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Before the dose: check the order, allergy history and the reason oral iron failed. Why: anaphylaxis is the one reaction that can kill, so you prepare for it first.\n2. Give by Z-track into a large muscle (general technique: pull the skin and tissue to one side, inject, wait a few seconds, release). Why: the tissue slides back and seals the path.\n3. Do not rub the site; encourage ambulation.\n4. Stay with or closely observe the client after the dose for breathing difficulty, hives, swelling, low blood pressure; keep emergency measures ready.\n5. Document site, response and any later pain, rash, fever or swelling.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nSudden shortness of breath, wheeze, throat tightness, hives, hypotension after the injection = anaphylaxis: stop and call for help, airway first. Late red flags: hot, painful, swollen injection site (abscess), fever, enlarged nodes.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"Which action is correct when giving iron IM?\" The answer set is: Z-track, no massage, ambulate. A wrong option is rubbing the site to speed absorption.\n\n## Memory aid\nZ-track = \"zig-zag seals the trail\": no rubbing, no leaking, no stain.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Iron Deficiency Anemia]], [[Intramuscular and Intravenous Medication Administration]], [[Pernicious Anemia (Vitamin B12 Deficiency)]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nNeedle size, exact wait time, injection site and the choice of IM or IV iron are not in the handout; confirm them in your book. Do not use this as instructions for a real client.",

"id": "np3-ho-x-anemias-compared",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Anemias Compared: Cause, Key Sign and First Priority",
"body": "## What it is\nA side-by-side study sheet of the six anemias in the handout so you can tell them apart quickly on the exam.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Iron deficiency: not enough iron for hemoglobin (blood loss, poor diet, poor absorption). Key sign: easy fatigability; spoon nails, pica, sore smooth tongue. Treat: iron, fix the bleeding source.\n- Pernicious (B12): no intrinsic factor, so B12 is not absorbed. Key sign: red beefy tongue plus nerve signs (paresthesia, poor balance, lost position sense). Treat: B12 injection for life.\n- Folic acid deficiency: poor intake, alcohol, pregnancy, malabsorption, chronic hemolysis. Key sign: sore tongue, cracked lips, tired, pale; large red cells on CBC. Treat: folic acid 1 mg daily.\n- Aplastic: marrow fails, pancytopenia (all three cell lines low). Key sign: infection plus bleeding plus anemia signs together. Treat: remove cause, transplant, immunosuppression, transfusions.\n- Sickle cell: inherited hemoglobin S; cells sickle when oxygen is low. Key sign: severe pain crisis. Treat: oxygen, fluids, pain control, hydroxyurea, transfusion, transplant.\n- Thalassemia: inherited faulty globin chain production; small pale cells. Key sign: severe anemia in thalassemia major. Treat: transfusion, chelation for iron overload, transplant.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause -> finding -> priority action:\n- Think \"which cell line is low\": red cells = oxygen problem (rest, oxygen); white cells = infection problem (protect); platelets = bleeding problem (avoid injuries).\n- Think \"can the client absorb it?\": if absorption is the problem (pernicious anemia), giving the vitamin by mouth is the trap; it must be injected.\n- Think \"does the treatment add iron?\": iron-deficiency gets iron; thalassemia and polycythemia vera do not, because iron piles up or blood is already too thick.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Reduce oxygen demand (rest) and give oxygen for hypoxia signs.\n2. Protect from the most dangerous problem for that disease: infection in aplastic anemia, pain and dehydration triggers in sickle cell crisis.\n3. Give the replacement the disease needs, by the right route.\n4. Teach diet, signs of worsening, and follow-up.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Iron Deficiency Anemia]], [[Pernicious Anemia (Vitamin B12 Deficiency)]], [[Folic Acid Deficiency Anemia]], [[Aplastic Anemia]], [[Sickle Cell Anemia and Crises]], [[Thalassemia]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThis is a summary built from the handout; normal red cell size terms (microcytic, macrocytic) for each disease are added from general knowledge and should be confirmed.",

"id": "np3-ho-x-wbc-types-functions",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "White Blood Cells: Types and Functions",
"body": "## What it is\nLeukocytes (white blood cells) defend the body. The normal total count in the handout is 4,500 to 11,000 per mm3. They are split into granulocytes and agranulocytes.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Granulocytes\n  - Neutrophil: first responder to bacteria; kills by phagocytosis (eating the germ). Key for preventing or limiting bacterial infection.\n  - Eosinophil: allergic reactions; neutralizes histamine; digests foreign proteins.\n- Agranulocytes\n  - Monocyte: moves into tissue as a macrophage; strongly phagocytic, especially against fungus; immune surveillance.\n  - T lymphocyte: cell-mediated immunity.\n  - B lymphocyte, matured into a plasma cell: makes antibodies (immunoglobulins).\n- Red cell: carries hemoglobin, lifespan about 120 days.\n- Platelet: fragment of a megakaryocyte; lifespan about 10 days; hemostasis.\n\n## How to reason about it\nLow neutrophils (neutropenia) = bacterial infection risk, the main danger of chemotherapy and aplastic anemia. Raised eosinophils point to allergy. Plasma cells = antibodies. A question that names \"cell-mediated immunity\" wants the T cell; one that names \"antibodies\" wants the plasma cell.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- If the counts are low (chemotherapy, aplastic anemia): protect from infection first (hand washing, reverse isolation as ordered, no fresh flowers or raw foods), and report fever early.\n- Why: infection kills faster than the other problems, and neutropenic clients may show few signs.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nFever, cough, abnormal lung sounds in a client with low white cells.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch the cell to the job; or identify which count matters most for a patient on chemotherapy.\n\n## Memory aid\nNeutrophil = \"bacteria first\"; Eosinophil = \"E for allergy\"; Monocyte = \"macrophage, mop-up crew\".\n\n## Related topics\n[[Blood Components and Normal Hematologic Values]], [[Chemotherapy Side Effects: Bone Marrow Suppression]], [[Aplastic Anemia]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nPercent ranges for each white cell type are not in the handout; look them up in your book.",

"area": "NP3",
"title": "Schilling Test and Vitamin B12 Absorption Testing",
"body": "## What it is\nA test used in pernicious anemia to find out whether the body absorbs vitamin B12. The client swallows a small amount of radioactive vitamin B12, and a 24-hour urine collection is done afterwards.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Positive result: vitamin B12 is absent in the urine, meaning it was not absorbed (fits pernicious anemia, lack of intrinsic factor).\n- Negative result: vitamin B12 is present in the urine, meaning it was absorbed.\n- Why the urine? Absorbed B12 that the body does not need is excreted in urine; if it never gets absorbed, none appears.\n\n## Assessment\nOther diagnostic help in the handout: CBC and blood smear show low hemoglobin and hematocrit.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Explain the test and the need to collect all urine for the full 24 hours; a missed void spoils the result.\n2. Label and store the container as directed; keep the client hydrated within limits.\n3. Remember the client received a small radioactive dose; follow facility rules for handling urine.\n4. After diagnosis, teach about lifelong B12 injections.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"A positive Schilling test means...?\" Positive = abnormal = B12 absent in urine. The word \"positive\" misleads people who think it means the vitamin is present.\n\n## Memory aid\nPositive = problem = \"Poor absorption, empty urine\".\n\n## Related topics\n[[Pernicious Anemia (Vitamin B12 Deficiency)]], [[Anemias Compared: Cause, Key Sign and First Priority]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout test details are brief; many current textbooks stress B12 blood levels and antibody tests instead of the Schilling test. Check what your review book expects.",

"area": "NP3",
"title": "Routes of Chemotherapy Administration",
"body": "## What it is\nChemotherapy drugs can be put into the body in several ways, chosen to reach the tumor while limiting harm to normal tissue.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Oral: the most convenient route.\n- Intravenous: the most common route.\n- Intramuscular.\n- Intrathecal: into the fluid around the spinal cord and brain, often through an Ommaya reservoir (a small device placed under the scalp). Used when drug must reach the central nervous system.\n- Intra-arterial: into the artery feeding the tumor.\n- Intracavitary: into a body cavity.\n- Intravesical: into the bladder (links to bladder cancer).\n- Topical: on the skin (links to chemosurgery for skin lesions).\n- Nitrosoureas cross the blood-brain barrier, which is why they are used for brain-related disease.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Verify the route in the order; confusing an IV and an intrathecal drug can be fatal. Why: some drugs are safe by one route only.\n2. Follow safe-handling rules for every route: gloves, gown, face shield, hazardous labels.\n3. For IV: check the vein for phlebitis and watch for extravasation (leakage into tissue).\n4. For an Ommaya or other device: aseptic technique; watch for infection and headache.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nPain, redness or swelling at an IV site during infusion (extravasation); fever or neck stiffness after intrathecal dosing (infection); bladder irritation after intravesical dosing.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"Which route is most common / most convenient?\" IV and oral. \"Which route delivers to the brain and spinal fluid?\" Intrathecal.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Chemotherapy Drug Classes and Cell Cycle]], [[Safe Handling of Chemotherapy Drugs]], [[Bladder Cancer and Urinary Diversions]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nVesicant lists and extravasation management are not in the handout; learn them from your pharmacology text.",

"body": "## What it is\nAfter bladder removal or when the bladder cannot work, urine is rerouted. The handout lists nine surgeries in two groups.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Stoma-forming surgeries (urine flows out to a pouch):\n  - Ileal conduit: a piece of ileum is used as the stoma.\n  - Ureterostomy: the ureters themselves are brought out as the stoma.\n  - Vesicostomy: the bladder is used for the stoma.\n  - Nephrostomy: a tube is placed into the kidney.\n- Continent or bladder-replacement surgeries:\n  - Ureterosigmoidostomy: the ureters are attached to the sigmoid colon (urine leaves with stool).\n  - Neobladder: a new bladder made from bowel is joined to the urethra (the handout says ascending colon).\n  - Kock pouch: ileum made into a pouch with a valve.\n  - Indiana pouch: ascending colon used as the bladder.\n  - Mitrofanoff: appendix and ileocecal valve used as the channel.\n\n## Assessment\nPainless hematuria is the earliest and most common bladder cancer sign; other findings: dysuria, hesitancy, pelvic or back pain.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Give IV fluids and pain management as ordered.\n3. Provide comfort measures and rest; teach pouch care and skin protection.\n4. Support body-image concerns before and after surgery.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nStoma necrosis, no urine for hours, infection signs, leakage, skin breakdown around the stoma.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatching: surgery name to \"what is used\" (ileum, ureters, bladder, kidney, sigmoid, ascending colon, appendix).\n\n## Memory aid\n\"Conduit = ileum; Indiana = ascending colon; Kock = valve; Mitrofanoff = appendix.\"\n\n## Related topics\n[[Bladder Cancer and Urinary Diversions]], [[Colostomy and Stoma Care]], [[Bowel Ostomies]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout's neobladder description (ascending colon to urethra) differs from many texts (usually ileum). "title": "PACU Airway and Circulation Management",
"body": "## What it is\nThe first priorities in the recovery room: keep the airway open, keep blood pressure and perfusion stable.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Airway blockage by the tongue or secretions shows as choking, noisy, irregular breathing (hypopharyngeal obstruction).\n- Hypotension after surgery can come from blood loss, hypoventilation, position change, blood pooling in the limbs, or drug and anesthetic effects.\n- Shock after surgery commonly comes from low circulating volume (hypovolemia). The main treatment is volume replacement.\n- Vital signs are checked every 15 minutes in the PACU (per handout).\n\n## Assessment\nAirway, breathing, circulation, skin color, level of consciousness, response to commands, surgical site drainage, tubes and lines.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Airway first: suction mucus or vomitus; take extra care after tonsil, mouth or laryngeal surgery. Why: no airway means no oxygen, and this outranks every other problem.\n2. Give oxygen.\n3. Check the incision and drains for bleeding.\n4. Treat hypovolemia with fluids or blood as ordered; keep the client warm so normal temperature is maintained.\n5. Monitor continuously until stable.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nSnoring, gurgling, stridor or silent chest; falling blood pressure with rapid weak pulse; restlessness; oliguria.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause -> finding -> action: low volume (cause) -> low BP, fast pulse, cold skin -> fluids/blood and find the bleeding. The trap: choosing pain medicine or paperwork before checking airway.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Post Anesthesia Care Unit and Aldrete Score]], [[Postoperative Hemorrhage and Hypovolemic Shock]], [[Management of Shock]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nPositioning of the client in PACU is not stated in the handout; confirm side-lying versus other positions in your book.",

"title": "Postoperative Pain and Nausea Control",
"body": "## What it is\nRelieving pain and nausea right after surgery so the client can breathe deeply, move and heal.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- In the PACU, opioid pain relievers are mostly given IV because they act immediately and are short acting.\n- Pain control also supports lung health: pain limits breathing, and uncontrolled pain leads to atelectasis.\n- Act at the first mention of nausea, before vomiting starts. The handout names promethazine (Phenergan) and hydroxyzine (Atarax) as examples of drugs ordered to prevent postoperative nausea and vomiting.\n- Deep breathing helps remove anesthetic from the body.\n- Opioids can depress breathing; this is an important balancing act.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Monitor physiological status and pain level regularly.\n2. Give analgesics as ordered, and use comfort measures and psychological support to ease fear.\n3. Watch breathing rate and alertness after opioids. Why: respiratory depression is the main danger and takes priority over comfort.\n4. Treat nausea at the first report; keep an emesis basin; turn the head to the side if vomiting to prevent aspiration.\n5. Teach the client to ask for pain medicine early and to splint the incision.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nSlow or shallow breathing, hard to wake, vomiting in a drowsy client (aspiration), pain that is suddenly worse (bleeding, dehiscence).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"First action for a client who says 'I feel sick'?\" Intervene at once (position, antiemetic as ordered), not wait for vomiting.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Post Anesthesia Care Unit and Aldrete Score]], [[Antiemetic Drugs]], [[Postoperative Respiratory Complications]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nPain scales and opioid doses are not in the handout; use your pharmacology text.",

"title": "Postoperative Nutrition, Elimination and Fluid Balance",
"body": "## What it is\nAfter surgery the body needs enough fluid, careful feeding and a working bowel. The handout lists these as priorities 4 and 6.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Fluid and electrolytes: give enough fluid to maintain blood volume, but avoid overload, which causes pulmonary congestion and edema. Do not force fluids too soon.\n- Keep accurate intake and output records and monitor serum electrolytes.\n- Normal peristalsis returns in about 48 to 72 hours after surgery.\n- Diet starts with liquids (broth, tea, juice, gelatin, soup) and is advanced as tolerated.\n- Early ambulation helps prevent abdominal distention.\n\n## Assessment\nBowel sounds, passing of flatus, abdominal distention, nausea, urine output, intake and output balance, breathing (for overload).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Encourage breathing exercises and walking.\n4. Report distention, vomiting or absent flatus after expected return time.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nFluid overload (crackles, shortness of breath, edema); dehydration (low urine, dry mucosa); distention and vomiting with no bowel activity.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Postoperative Priorities of Care]], [[Early Ambulation after Surgery]], [[Fluid Volume Excess (Hypervolemia)]], [[Fluid Volume Deficit (Hypovolemia)]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nPost-abdominal-surgery nothing-by-mouth rules, nasogastric tube care and the term paralytic ileus are not in the handout; check your book.",

"title": "Radiation Therapy Compared: External, Sealed and Unsealed",
"body": "## What it is\nA comparison table in note form of the three radiation situations, since exam questions hinge on whether the client is radioactive.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- External (teletherapy): source far from the client; client NOT radioactive; no special isolation needed.\n- Internal sealed (needles, seeds or beads, catheter, intracavitary): client IS radioactive; the source stays in place; body fluids NOT radioactive.\n- Internal unsealed (IV or oral, e.g. iodine-131 for thyroid cancer): client IS radioactive; body fluids ARE radioactive.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Identify which type is in use. Why: the type decides how much protection staff and visitors need.\n2. For internal radiation: shielding (lead gown, dosimeter badge), time (limit contact), distance (about 6 feet) and a private room.\n3. Handout limits: 30 minutes per shift and 5 minutes per visit.\n4. Never assign pregnant staff; no children as visitors.\n5. Intracavitary: empty bladder and bowel, complete bed rest, low-residue diet, indwelling urinary catheter.\n6. Unsealed: flush the toilet 3 times; visitors do not share the toilet.\n7. External: protect the skin, no lotion or powder, leave markings, plan for fatigue.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nRadiodermatitis, severe fatigue, and for sealed implants, a dislodged source (do not touch with bare hands; follow policy).\n\n## How to reason about it\nThe trap: assuming every radiation client is a hazard. Teletherapy = safe to touch. Sealed = body fluids are safe. Unsealed = all fluids are hot.\n\n## Memory aid\nShielding, Time, Distance (\"STD\" in the handout).\n\n## Related topics\n[[External Radiation Therapy (Teletherapy)]], [[Internal Radiation Therapy (Brachytherapy) and Radiation Precautions]], [[Chemotherapy Drug Classes and Cell Cycle]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nTime limits vary by policy and isotope; learn the handout numbers for the exam and confirm in your text.",

Tests: cystoscopy, CT. Surgery: urinary diversions.\n- Skin: sun exposure. Types: basal cell (most common), squamous cell (can spread), melanoma (deadliest). ABCDE rule. Treatment: electrosurgery, cryosurgery, radiation.\n\n## How to reason about it\nMatch the \"first sign\" to the organ: painless hematuria -> bladder; clay stools and weight loss -> pancreas; change in bowel habit -> colon; changing mole -> melanoma. "id": "np3-er-kidney-anatomy",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Kidney Anatomy and Functions",
"body": "## What it is\nThe kidneys are paired, bean-shaped organs (about 150 g each) lying against the back wall of the abdomen, behind the peritoneum (retroperitoneal), roughly from T12 to L3. The right kidney sits a little lower than the left. At rest they receive about 20% of cardiac output, which falls to roughly 2-4% under stress when blood is diverted to vital organs.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Functions: form urine, remove waste, balance electrolytes, balance acid and base, control water balance, help control blood pressure (renin), clear substances from blood, make erythropoietin (red cell production), activate vitamin D, secrete prostaglandins, and regulate calcium and phosphorus.\n- Blood path: renal artery enters at the hilum, splits into afferent arterioles, goes through the glomerular capillary bed, leaves by the efferent arteriole, then drains to the renal vein.\n- Ureters: about 25-30 cm long, widen into the renal pelvis (calyces and papillae), and push urine to the bladder by peristalsis.\n- Bladder: hollow smooth-muscle bag behind the symphysis pubis; reservoir holding about 300-500 mL in an adult; controlled by the autonomic nervous system.\n- Urethra: a muscle-and-mucosa tube ending at the meatus. Female urethra is short (3-5 cm), in front of the vagina; the male urethra passes through the prostate.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nBecause the kidney makes erythropoietin and activates vitamin D, kidney failure leads to anemia and bone disease. A short female urethra explains why women get more bladder infections. See [[Chronic Kidney Disease: Findings by Body System]] and [[Cystitis and Urethritis]].\n\n## Related topics\n[[Nephron and Urine Formation]], [[Urine Volume Control and Normal Urine Values]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout says the glomerulus lies between \"afferent and afferent\" vessels; this is a typo, it should be afferent and efferent.",

"id": "np3-er-nephron-urine-formation",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Nephron and Urine Formation",
"body": "## What it is\nThe nephron is the working unit of the kidney (about a million per kidney). Urine is made in three steps: filtration (water and small solutes leave blood into the glomerulus), reabsorption (needed water and ions go back to blood), and excretion (waste and extra substances leave in urine).\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Glomerulus: tuft of capillaries that filters water and solutes. Blood cells, albumin and fibrinogen are normally too large to pass, so protein or blood in urine means glomerular damage.\n- Bowman's capsule: cup that surrounds the glomerulus and collects the filtrate.\n- Proximal convoluted tubule: reabsorbs glucose and sodium (active transport), chloride and bicarbonate (diffusion), and about 80% of the water (osmosis).\n- Loop of Henle: reabsorbs water, chloride and sodium.\n- Distal convoluted tubule: reabsorbs sodium (aldosterone) and water (ADH); secretes hydrogen, potassium and ammonia.\n- Collecting tubules: last water reabsorption, again under ADH.\n\n## Why it matters\nEach part explains a disease or drug: loop diuretics act on the loop of Henle, thiazides on the distal tubule, and aldosterone-blocking drugs on the distal sodium-potassium exchange (hyperkalemia risk). See [[Fluid Volume Excess (Hypervolemia)]].\n\n## Memory aid\nMade-up: \"PCT takes the most\" (glucose, sodium, 80% water).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Kidney Anatomy and Functions]], [[Urine Volume Control and Normal Urine Values]], [[Acute Glomerulonephritis (Nephritic Syndrome)]]",

"renal",
"physiology",

"area": "NP4",
"title": "Urine Volume Control and Normal Urine Values",
"body": "## What it is\nHow the body decides how much urine to make, plus what normal urine looks like.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Glomerular filtration rate (GFR) is about 125 mL/min and is kept fairly constant.\n- Renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (RAAS): when kidney perfusion is poor, juxtaglomerular cells release renin. Renin turns angiotensinogen into angiotensin I; ACE in the lungs turns that into angiotensin II, a very strong vasoconstrictor that raises blood pressure. The adrenal cortex then releases aldosterone, which keeps sodium and loses potassium. Water follows sodium, so blood volume and pressure rise.\n- ADH (antidiuretic hormone) makes the collecting tubules reabsorb water; plasma and urine osmolarity guide its release.\n\n## Normal urine values (handout)\n- Color light yellow/straw, aromatic odor, clear or slightly turbid.\n- pH 4.5-8; specific gravity 1.010-1.020.\n- No WBC, RBC, albumin or E. coli; a few mucus threads; no amorphous urates.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nLow perfusion (shock, dehydration) activates RAAS: the client retains sodium and water and loses potassium, with low urine output. Specific gravity above 1.020 suggests concentrated urine (dehydration); below 1.010 suggests dilute urine. See [[Electrolyte Normal Values and Fluid Balance Lab Tests]].\n\n## Related topics\n[[Nephron and Urine Formation]], [[Acute Glomerulonephritis (Nephritic Syndrome)]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout calls the renin-secreting cells \"densa cells\"; renin comes from the juxtaglomerular (granular) cells, while the macula densa senses sodium. "body": "## What it is\nCystitis is infection of the bladder, usually from bacteria (most often E. coli) climbing up the urethra. Urethritis is inflammation of the urethra.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Women get cystitis more because of the short urethra, closeness of urethra to rectum, childbirth and sexual intercourse. In men it follows epididymitis, prostatitis or stones.\n- Risk factors: obstruction, urine retention, high estrogen, intercourse.\n- Cystitis findings: dysuria, urgency, frequency, nocturia, suprapubic pain, hematuria, pyuria, fever, chills, sometimes flank pain.\n- Diagnosis: urine culture and sensitivity.\n- Urethritis: caused by E. coli, staphylococci, streptococci or Pseudomonas; burning on urination and pus-like discharge appearing 3-14 days after exposure; looks like gonorrhea but sexual contact is not the cause. It can spread to cause prostatitis or epididymitis.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Encourage high fluid intake and frequent voiding (flushes bacteria).\n- Give antibiotics as ordered (co-trimoxazole in the handout), plus antispasmodics and analgesics.\n- Comfort: warm sitz bath.\n\n## Client teaching\nCranberry or orange juice; avoid coffee, tea and alcohol (bladder irritants); empty the bladder after intercourse; wipe front to back and keep good hygiene. Urethritis treatment in the handout: tetracycline or doxycycline.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Pyelonephritis]], [[Urolithiasis: Kidney and Urinary Stones]], [[Benign Prostatic Hyperplasia (BPH)]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nDrug choices (co-trimoxazole, tetracycline) vary by guideline and local resistance; confirm.",

Uric acid: low purine. Cystine: low methionine. "body": "## What it is\nSlow enlargement of the prostate in men over about 40 that squeezes the urethra and interferes with urination. "body": "## What it is\nSurgical choices for an enlarged prostate and what the nurse watches afterward.\n\n## Surgical approaches (handout)\n- TURP (transurethral resection): no incision, prostate removed through the urethra; continuous bladder irrigation afterward; incontinence and impotence usually not expected.\n- Suprapubic prostatectomy: lower abdominal incision into the bladder, cystostomy tube and two-way Foley.\n- Retropubic prostatectomy: low abdominal incision.\n- Perineal prostatectomy: carries impotence, incontinence and possible rectal injury.\n\n## Priority nursing actions after surgery\n- Hemorrhage is the top danger: watch drainage color and vital signs. "body": "## What it is\nInflammation and damage of the glomeruli. Antibody-antigen complexes get trapped in the glomeruli, reducing filtration. "id": "np3-er-arf-phases",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Acute Renal Failure: Causes, Phases and Findings",
"body": "## What it is\nA sudden, often reversible drop in kidney function with build-up of waste (rising BUN and creatinine). Includes acute tubular necrosis.\n\n## Causes\n- Pre-renal: poor blood flow to the kidney (volume loss, weak heart, vasodilation).\n- Intra-renal: actual kidney damage (prolonged ischemia, nephrotoxic drugs, infection).\n- Post-renal: blocked urine flow (stones, tumors, BPH, clots).\n\n## Phases\n- Onset: from the injury until oliguria starts (about 1-3 days).\n- Oliguric: urine under 400 mL/24 h for about 3 days to 2 weeks; BUN and creatinine rise; edema, hypertension, hyperkalemia, hyponatremia, hyperphosphatemia, metabolic acidosis.\n- Diuretic: urine climbs to about 3-5 L/day; BUN stays high at first; hypokalemia and dehydration become the risk.\n- Recovery: function improves over 3-12 months; avoid nephrotoxic drugs; can progress to chronic failure.\n\n## Findings\nLab: hyperkalemia, hyperphosphatemia, hypocalcemia, metabolic acidosis, azotemia, casts, RBCs and WBCs in urine. Clinical: sudden fall in urine output, irritability, headache, anorexia, tingling, lethargy, twitching or seizures, pallor, ammonia breath, edema, hypertension that can lead to pulmonary edema.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nOliguric phase = watch fluid overload and hyperkalemia; diuretic phase = watch fluid loss and hypokalemia. See [[Hyperkalemia]] and [[Hypokalemia]].\n\n## Related topics\n[[Acute Renal Failure: Treatment and Nursing Care]], [[Metabolic Acidosis]]",
"tags": [
"renal",
"AKI",
"area-guess"

"title": "Acute Renal Failure: Treatment and Nursing Care",
"body": "## What it is\nManagement centers on fixing the cause and protecting the client while kidneys recover.\n\n## Treatment (handout)\n- Correct underlying cause (restore perfusion, stop nephrotoxic drugs, relieve obstruction).\n- Loop diuretics, ACE inhibitors for hypertension, H2 blockers to prevent stomach ulcers.\n- Kayexalate (sodium polystyrene) to lower potassium; sodium bicarbonate for acidosis.\n- Dialysis when needed (peritoneal or hemodialysis).\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Monitor intake and output, weigh daily, check for overhydration (edema, crackles, headache, neck vein distention, hypertension).\n- Watch for hyperkalemia and acidosis first: dangerous rhythms are life-threatening.\n- Diet: modest protein restriction, high carbohydrate, restricted potassium; TPN if needed.\n- Watch for oliguria followed by polyuria.\n- Protect from injury, give skin care, allow undisturbed rest.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nThe priority question is usually the electrolyte that can stop the heart (potassium). "title": "Chronic Kidney Disease: Causes and Stages",
"body": "## What it is\nPermanent, progressive loss of nephrons and glomerular function with build-up of waste (uremia).\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Leading causes: diabetic nephropathy (number one), hypertension, glomerulonephritis, lupus (SLE).\n- Normal GFR is about 125 mL/min/1.73 m2.\n- Stages by GFR (handout): stage 1 above 90 (damage with normal GFR); stage 2 60-89; stage 3 30-59; stage 4 15-29; stage 5 below 15 (kidney failure, ESRD).\n- Course by function: diminished reserve (no symptoms), renal insufficiency (waste accumulates, GFR falls), then end-stage renal disease with uremia, which is life-threatening.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nKnow that the first sign of trouble can be silent. Control blood pressure and blood sugar to slow progression. See [[Chronic Kidney Disease: Findings by Body System]].\n\n## Related topics\n[[Acute Glomerulonephritis (Nephritic Syndrome)]], [[Nephrotic Syndrome]], [[Chronic Kidney Disease: Diet and Nursing Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout says GFR is \"mildly increased\" in stages 2-4; it is actually mildly, moderately and severely decreased. "title": "Chronic Kidney Disease: Findings by Body System",
"body": "## What it is\nUremia affects nearly every body system.\n\n## Findings by system (handout)\n- GI: nausea, vomiting, stomatitis, uremic breath (ammonia odor), diarrhea or constipation.\n- Respiratory: Kussmaul respirations (deep, rapid, from acidosis), weak cough reflex.\n- Fluid and electrolytes: hyperkalemia, dilutional hyponatremia, hypermagnesemia, hyperphosphatemia.\n- Skin: itching, dry skin, uremic frost, edema.\n- Cardiovascular: hypertension (RAAS activation), pericarditis from uremic toxins.\n- Blood: anemia (low erythropoietin) and thrombocytopenia (bleeding).\n- Bones and muscle: cramps, weakness, renal osteodystrophy, bone pain and fractures.\n- Reproductive: amenorrhea, testicular atrophy, infertility, low libido.\n- Nervous system: confusion, disorientation, seizures, burning feet, behavior change.\n\n## Why it happens\nFailing kidneys cannot clear waste, make erythropoietin, activate vitamin D (low calcium, high phosphorus, bone loss) or excrete acid, potassium and water.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nLife-threatening items to spot: hyperkalemia, pulmonary edema, pericarditis, seizures. See [[Hyperkalemia]] and [[Phosphate Imbalances]].\n\n## Related topics\n[[Chronic Kidney Disease: Causes and Stages]], [[Metabolic Acidosis]]",

"title": "Chronic Kidney Disease: Diet and Nursing Care",
"body": "## What it is\nCare aims to slow decline, keep fluid and electrolytes balanced, prevent complications, and prepare for dialysis or transplant.\n\n## Medications\nCalcium and phosphorus binders (for example aluminum hydroxide gel), antihypertensives and cardiac drugs, antiseizure drugs, and erythropoietin for anemia.\n\n## Nutrition\n- Regulate protein; allow high biologic value protein (dairy, eggs, meat).\n- Fluid allowance: urine output of the previous 24 hours plus about 500-600 mL.\n- Some potassium restriction; enough calories and vitamins.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Monitor vital signs, intake and output, daily weight, edema; listen to breath sounds.\n- Prevent infection and injury: meticulous skin care, soft toothbrush, avoid aspirin (bleeding), protect from infection, balance activity with rest.\n- Comfort: analgesics, antipruritics.\n- Emotional support: promote hope, allow expression of feelings, link to community resources, prepare for dialysis or kidney transplant.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nExpect questions on which foods to limit (potassium, phosphorus, sodium) and on the fluid allowance.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Chronic Kidney Disease: Findings by Body System]], [[Hemodialysis and Vascular Access]], [[Peritoneal Dialysis]], [[Kidney Transplant: Donation and Nursing Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout names Amphojel as a binder; many units now use other binders. "id": "np3-er-peritoneal-dialysis",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Peritoneal Dialysis",
"body": "## What it is\nDialysis solution is run into the abdominal cavity through a catheter. The peritoneum acts as the filter: waste (urea, creatinine) and extra fluid move into the dialysate by diffusion and osmosis, then the fluid is drained.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- The client can do it alone, at home, and it can be used in hemodynamically unstable clients.\n- Drainage color: normally clear/colorless; cloudy = infection (peritonitis); bloody is common in the first exchanges; yellow may mean the bladder was punctured.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Before: consent, baseline vital signs, explain the procedure, empty bladder and bowel (avoid puncture), warm the dialysate.\n- Antibiotic and heparin may be added as ordered.\n- During: regulate inflow and drainage, record the fluid balance, keep comfort.\n- Watch for leakage, blocked catheter, peritonitis (cloudy fluid, fever, abdominal pain), and infection at the exit site.\n- Monitor blood glucose (the solution contains glucose); teach catheter care.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nCloudy return fluid is the red flag; use aseptic technique. Compare with [[Hemodialysis and Vascular Access]].\n\n## Related topics\n[[Chronic Kidney Disease: Diet and Nursing Care]], [[Acute Renal Failure: Treatment and Nursing Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nHandout says to give broad-spectrum antibiotics and heparin before treatment; practice varies, so confirm with your textbook.",

"id": "np3-er-hemodialysis",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Hemodialysis and Vascular Access",
"body": "## What it is\nBlood is pumped through a machine; waste and fluid pass across a semi-permeable membrane into dialysate by diffusion, osmosis and ultrafiltration.\n\n## Vascular access\n- AV fistula: surgical joining of an artery to a vein (usually forearm); needs at least about 14 days to mature. Check for a thrill (feel) and bruit (listen).\n- AV graft: synthetic or biologic tube placed between artery and vein when the client's own vessels are unsuitable.\n- Catheter: double-lumen large catheter into the subclavian, jugular or femoral vein.\n- Absent thrill or bruit means clotting or blockage: report immediately.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Protect the access: no blood pressure, venipuncture or IV in the access arm.\n- Give IV drugs slowly; watch heart and lungs for complications.\n- Control electrolytes and diet.\n- Hold antihypertensives before dialysis if ordered to avoid low pressure.\n- Prevent infection: keep catheter site clean and dry, do not submerge it, keep it secured.\n- Comfort for itching: antihistamine, bath oils, lotion, short nails.\n\n## Complications of access\nPoor flow, clotting, infection, aneurysm, hand ischemia, heart failure.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Peritoneal Dialysis]], [[Chronic Kidney Disease: Diet and Nursing Care]], [[Acute Renal Failure: Treatment and Nursing Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout does not list \"no BP or venipuncture in the access arm\", but this is standard teaching; confirm.",

"title": "Kidney Transplant: Donation and Nursing Care",
"body": "## What it is\nA kidney from a living or deceased donor is placed in a client whose kidneys no longer work.\n\n## Law\nOrgan Donation Act of 1991 (Republic Act 7170): any person at least 18 years old and of sound mind may donate all or part of the body after death for education, research, therapy or transplant. In deceased donation, death is diagnosed separately by two qualified physicians, neither of whom is on the removal team or the head of the hospital authorizing removal.\n\n## Donors\nLiving related, living unrelated (spouse, friend), and deceased donors (for example after severe head injury, stroke, drowning, brain tumor).\n\n## Before surgery\n- Tissue typing, blood typing, antibody screening, full physical and psychosocial evaluation.\n- Teach breathing exercises, pain options, diet limits and early ambulation.\n- Avoid blood transfusion for at least 2 weeks before; erythropoietin treats the anemia.\n\n## After surgery\n- Give immunosuppressants as ordered.\n- Watch for rejection (oliguria, edema, fever, rising blood pressure and weight, swelling or tenderness over the graft, rising creatinine) and infection (chills, fever, fast pulse and breathing, WBC change). Hand hygiene is vital.\n- Monitor urine output, prevent lung complications and care for the incision; assess coping.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Kidney Transplant Rejection Types]], [[Chronic Kidney Disease: Diet and Nursing Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nHandout says \"cerebral anorexia\" (probably cerebral anoxia) and \"decreased donor\" (deceased). Verify the RA 7170 wording.",

"id": "np3-er-transplant-rejection",
"area": "NP4",
"title": "Kidney Transplant Rejection Types",
"body": "## What it is\nRejection is the recipient's immune system attacking the transplanted kidney as foreign. The type is named by how soon it happens after surgery and tells you what to expect and what to do.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Hyperacute: within hours after surgery; an antibody-antigen reaction. Signs: no urine output; the kidney looks blue and flaccid. The graft must be removed and the client returns to hemodialysis.\n- Acute: days to months after surgery. The body mounts an immune defense against the donor kidney. Signs: urine output drops sharply, BUN and creatinine rise, fever, graft tenderness and swelling. Treated by increasing immunosuppressant drugs.\n- Chronic: months to years after surgery. Cause is unclear. Kidney function declines gradually, and there is no specific treatment.\n- General rejection warning signs: oliguria, edema, fever, rising blood pressure, weight gain, swelling or tenderness over the transplant, rising serum creatinine.\n- Diagnosis: ultrasound can show enlargement; percutaneous renal biopsy is the most reliable test; x-ray techniques also help.\n\n## Assessment\nCheck the daily urine volume, weight, temperature, blood pressure and graft tenderness; trend creatinine.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n- Report any drop in urine output, fever or graft pain right away: early treatment may save the kidney in acute rejection.\n- Give immunosuppressants on time and never skip doses.\n- Prepare for biopsy or return to dialysis as ordered.\n- Infection control: immunosuppression raises the risk of infection, so use hand hygiene and watch for chills, fever and fast pulse.\n- Emotional support: rejection can be devastating.\n\n## Treatment and medications (general terms)\nImmunosuppressants, higher doses for acute rejection; removal of the graft for hyperacute rejection; dialysis if function is lost.\n\n## Complications and red flags\nGraft loss, return to dialysis, infection, medication side effects.\n\n## Client teaching\nTake immunosuppressants exactly as prescribed, weigh and record daily, report warning signs, avoid sick contacts.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch the type to its timing. A blue, flaccid graft with no urine on the operating table or within hours = hyperacute. Fever, tenderness and falling urine over the first weeks = acute.\n\n## How to reason about it\nFaster rejection = more severe, less treatable. The trap: do not confuse infection (from immunosuppression) with rejection; both cause fever, so report both.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Kidney Transplant: Donation and Nursing Care]], [[Hemodialysis and Vascular Access]], [[Peritoneal Dialysis versus Hemodialysis Compared]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nSpecific drugs and the timing ranges are not given in the handout beyond the labels above. Confirm in your transplant nursing text.",

"id": "np3-er-fluid-compartments",
"area": "NP3",
"title": "Body Fluid Compartments and Fluid Movement",
"body": "## What it is\nWater and electrolytes make up about 60% of body weight, held in compartments separated by semi-permeable membranes.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Intracellular fluid (inside cells) is the larger share (handout: 65%); extracellular fluid (outside cells) is the rest (35%). Extracellular fluid includes interstitial fluid between cells and plasma in the vessels (plasma and lymph about 8%); transcellular fluid (spinal, synovial, pleural, pericardial, peritoneal, eye fluids) is about 2%.\n- Main intracellular electrolytes: potassium and phosphorus. Main extracellular electrolytes: sodium and chloride.\n- Diffusion: solute moves from high to low concentration. Osmosis: water (solvent) moves toward the side with more dissolved particles. Homeostasis: balance of body fluid.\n- Gender: males about 60% water, females about 50% (muscle holds more water than fat).\n- Age: infants about 80%; the elderly have less muscle and a weaker thirst sensation. Both groups are at high risk for fluid and electrolyte imbalance.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nInfants and elders become dehydrated fastest, so they are priority clients in vomiting, diarrhea or fever. See [[Fluid Volume Deficit (Hypovolemia)]].\n\n## Related topics\n[[Electrolyte Normal Values and Fluid Balance Lab Tests]], [[Fluid Volume Excess (Hypervolemia)]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout's percentages (65/35 and interstitial 25%) do not add up neatly. Usual teaching is about two-thirds of body water inside cells and one-third outside; confirm.",
